Throughout the article, we only apply Assumption
2
under conditions that also imply strong well-posedness of (
3.4
), in which case we always denote the unique strong solution to (
3.4
) by
\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle
. Thus, the reader may assume that
\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle
in Assumption
2
has a well defined law.

Theorem 4.7

(Local weak convergence of IPS)
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds, and
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
,
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle,n\in{\mathbb{N}},
are a.s. finitely dissociable
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random elements (in the sense of Definition
5.11
) such that
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
satisfies Assumption
2
. If
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\Rightarrow\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
, then
\langle(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\Rightarrow\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]
.

Theorem
4.7
follows from a more general almost sure version of this statement proved in Proposition
6.11
under weaker assumptions that only require Assumption
′
‣
5.1
, a consistent spatial localization condition introduced in Section
5.1
, and a weaker finite convergence condition, Assumption
′
‣
6.2
, in place of Assumption
2
. The weaker Assumption
′
‣
6.2
is useful for some applications.

4.3
Hydrodynamic Limit and Correlation Decay

Given a Polish space
{\mathcal{Z}}
and a finite, unrooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-graph
G
, define the (global) empirical measure of the finite, unrooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}]
-graph
(G,z)
by

\pi^{G,z}(\cdot):=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{v\in V_{G}}\delta_{z_{v}}(\cdot),

where
\delta_{z_{v}}(\cdot)
is the Dirac delta measure concentrated at
z_{v}
. Also consider the more general
neighborhood empirical measure
given by

\overline{\pi}^{G,z}_{\overline{o}}(\cdot):=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{v\in V_{G}}\delta_{\langle B_{1}(\mathscr{C}_{v}(G,z))\rangle}(\cdot),

(4.2)

where
\mathscr{C}_{v}(G,z)
denotes the rooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}]
-graph obtained by restricting
(G,z)
to the connected component of
v
in
[G]
, equipped with
v
as its root. Note that
\pi^{G,z}=\pi^{G^{\prime},z^{\prime}}
and
\overline{\pi}^{G,z}_{\overline{o}}=\overline{\pi}^{G^{\prime},z}_{\overline{o}}
whenever
(G^{\prime},z^{\prime})\cong(G,z)
. In other words, both empirical measures are determined only by the isomorphism class
\langle(G,z)\rangle
of
(G,z)
. In particular,
\overline{\pi}^{G,z}_{\overline{o}}
is a
{\mathcal{P}}({{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}])
-valued random element that describes the empirical measure of the isomorphism class of a uniformly distributed root in
(G,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},z)
and its neighborhood, and the
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{Z}})
-valued random element
\pi^{G,z}
is the
z
-root mark marginal of
\overline{\pi}^{G,z}_{\overline{o}}
.

Since
\pi^{G,z}
and
\overline{\pi}^{G,z}_{\overline{o}}
are global quantities, their asymptotic behavior cannot be deduced from the local convergence result established in Theorem
4.7
. Moreover, as discussed in the introduction, unlike in the case of IPS on dense graphs, states of neighboring vertices of IPS on sequences of converging sparse graphs remain strongly correlated and do not become asymptotically independent; that is, propagation of chaos typically fails. Hence, the analysis of the convergence of
\pi^{G,z}
is more subtle for IPS on sparse (as opposed to dense) graph sequences, and due to this strong dependence between neighboring vertices, more complex empirical quantities such as
\overline{\pi}^{G,z}_{\overline{o}}
are also of interest. Nevertheless, we show that under a slightly stronger convergence condition on the initial data than that imposed in Theorem
4.7
, these empirical quantities do have a deterministic limit; see Theorem
4.11
and Corollary
4.12
, which are presented in Section
4.3.2
. A key ingredient of the proof is a certain asymptotic correlation decay property, which is first stated in Section
4.3.1
(see Theorem
4.10
).

4.3.1
An Annealed Correlation Decay Property

We start by introducing a slightly stronger notion of local convergence that applies to graphs that are not necessarily connected and which, in a sense, has a more global flavor.

Definition 4.8

(Local convergence in probability)
.

Consider a sequence
\{G_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of finite, unrooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-random graphs. Then
G_{n}
converges to a
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-random element
\langle G\rangle
in probability in the local weak sense (abbreviated to locally in probability) if for every bounded,
\mathcal{L}(\langle G\rangle)
-a.s. continuous mapping
f:{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

\frac{1}{|G_{n}|}\sum_{v\in V_{G_{n}}}f(\langle\mathscr{C}_{v}(G_{n})\rangle)\to\mathbb{E}\left[f(\langle G\rangle)\right]\text{ in probability,}

(4.3)

as
n\to\infty
.

Most definitions of convergence in probability only require that (
4.3
) hold for bounded and everywhere continuous functions
f
(e.g.,
[
30
, Definition 2.6]
). However, it can be shown that these definitions are equivalent. The version of the definition given above is more convenient for direct application to certain examples where one has to use the fact that (
4.3
) holds for the larger class of bounded, a.s. continuous functions
f
, such as Examples A and C of Section
4.4
.

Remark 4.9

(Examples of graphs converging locally in probability)
.

All the examples of sequences of finite random graphs provided in Section
2.4.2
, equipped with roots chosen uniformly at random, turn out to converge in this stronger sense (and not just locally in distribution).

We now state an asymptotic correlation decay property.

Theorem 4.10

(Asymptotic correlation decay)
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds, and let
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
be a
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random element that satisfies Assumption
2
, and is a.s. finitely dissociable in the sense of Definition
5.11
. Suppose there exists a countable deterministic set
{\mathcal{S}}
and a sequence
\{(G_{n},\xi^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of finite, unrooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random graphs, each of whose vertex sets a.s. lie in
{\mathcal{S}}
. Also, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})
denote the strong solution to (
3.4
) for the initial data
(G_{n},\xi^{n})
. Suppose that
|G_{n}|\to\infty
in probability, and
(G_{n},\xi^{n})
converges locally in probability to
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
. If
o^{i}_{n}
,
i=1,2,
are independent, uniformly distributed vertices of
G_{n}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then for any bounded continuous functions
f_{i}:{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]\to{\mathbb{R}},

i=1,2,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\text{Cov}\left(f_{1}\left(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{1}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\right),f_{2}\left(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{2}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\right)\right)=0,

(4.4)

where
Cov
represents the covariance functional.

The proof of Theorem
4.10
is given in Section
7
. The assumption in the statement of the theorem that the vertex sets of each
G_{n}
a.s. lie in a countable deterministic set is not restrictive because it is satisfied by most common random graph sequences of interest including Erdös-Rényi graphs, configuration models, and the Barabàsi-Albert model. It is imposed merely for technical convenience. It enables the driving noises on the graph sequence
\{G_{n}\}
to be coupled in a measurable way, thereby allowing the application of the results of Section
6.1
in the proof (in particular, of the intermediate result stated in Lemma
7.2
).

When the jump rates of the SDE (
3.4
) are strongly Lipschitz continuous in the sense described in the introduction, then arguments similar to those used for diffusions in
[
30
, Lemma 5.2]
can be applied to obtain stronger quantitative quenched (i.e., conditioned on the graph) bounds on the decay of correlation of IPS that are uniform with respect to graphs, and only depend on the cardinality of the sets of particles being compared and the graph distance between the sets. Under Assumption
1
, such a strong Lipschitz condition holds for Markov IPS on graphs with bounded maximal degree but fails to hold for many interesting IPS on graphs with unbounded maximal degree. In such situations, one does not expect there to be a similar quenched correlation bound that is uniform over all graphs (or even all finitely dissociable graphs), and thus, the arguments we use to prove Theorem
4.10
are crucially different from those used in
[
30
]
. Specifically, to establish the
annealed asymptotic
correlation decay property (
4.4
), which is averaged over the randomness of the initial data, we first use the fact that the initial data satisfies an analogous asymptotic correlation decay property (due to the assumed local convergence in probability), then carefully construct an appropriate coupling and leverage Proposition
6.11
, which establishes a stronger almost sure version of the local convergence result from Theorem
4.7
, to extend the correlation decay property to the solution process.

4.3.2
Hydrodynamic Limits

The existence of the hydrodynamic limit follows as a simple consequence of well-posedness of the limit, local convergence, and asymptotic correlation decay.

Theorem 4.11

(Local convergence in probability of IPS)
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds and the sequence of finite, unrooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random graphs
\{(G_{n},\xi^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and the
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random element
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
4.10
. Let
(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi_{n}})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
(G,X^{G,\xi})
be weak solutions to (
3.4
) for
(G_{n},\xi_{n})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
(G,\xi)
, respectively defined on a common probability space. Then the sequence
\{(G,X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges locally in probability to
\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle
.

Proof.

First note that, given any driving noise,
\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle
and
(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, are well defined unique strong solutions to (
3.4
) by Theorem
4.3
and Lemma
3.15
. For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
(o^{1}_{n},o^{2}_{n})
be two i.i.d. uniformly distributed vertices in
G_{n}
. Then, by Theorem
4.10
and Theorem
4.7
, for any bounded, continuous functions
f_{1},f_{2}:{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{2}f_{i}(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{i}(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle)\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{i}(\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle)\right].

That this implies the desired result follows from
[
30
, Lemma 2.8]
.
∎

We now show that Theorem
4.11
implies the convergence in probability of both the empirical measure and the empirical neighborhood measure to corresponding deterministic limits.

Corollary 4.12

(Hydrodynamic limit)
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds and the sequence of finite, unrooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random graphs
\{(G_{n},\xi^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and the
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random element
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
4.10
. Also, let
(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi_{n}})
and
(G,X^{G,\xi})
be weak solutions to (
3.4
) for
(G_{n},\xi_{n})
and
(G,\xi)
, respectively. Then the
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{D}})
-valued random empirical measure sequence
\{\pi^{G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges in probability to
\mathcal{L}(X^{G,\xi}_{o})\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{D}})
, and the
{\mathcal{P}}({{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}])
-valued random empirical neighborhood measure sequence
\{\overline{\pi}^{G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}}_{\overline{o}}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges in probability to
\mathcal{L}(\langle B_{1}(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle)\in{\mathcal{P}}({{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}])
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.11
, the sequence
\{(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges locally in probability to
\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle
. By
[
30
, Lemma 2.7]
(or rather its immediate extension to the setting with edge marks), this directly implies that the sequence
\{\pi^{G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{D}})
-valued random measures satisfies

\pi^{G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}}\mbox{ converges in probability to }\mathcal{L}(X^{G,\xi}_{o}).

(4.5)

This proves the first assertion of the corollary.

Note that the map
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]\ni\langle(G,x)\rangle\mapsto\langle B_{1}(G,x)\rangle\in{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]
is continuous. Thus, for any bounded, continuous function
f:{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]\to{\mathbb{R}}
, the function
g:{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]\to{\mathbb{R}}
given by
g(\langle(G,x)\rangle):=f(\langle B_{1}(G,x)\rangle)
is also bounded and continuous. Hence, recalling the definition of
\overline{\pi}^{G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}}_{\overline{o}}
from (
4.2
), we have as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\int_{{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]}f(\langle(G,x)\rangle)\,\overline{\pi}^{G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}}_{\overline{o}}(d\langle(G,x)\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|G_{n}|}\sum_{v\in V_{G_{n}}}f(\langle B_{1}(\mathscr{C}_{v}((G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|G_{n}|}\sum_{v\in V_{G_{n}}}g(\langle\mathscr{C}_{v}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[g(\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(\langle B_{1}(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle)\right],

where the convergence
\to
in the penultimate line is in probability and justified by (
4.5
) and (
4.3
). Thus,
\overline{\pi}^{G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}}_{\overline{o}}\to\mathcal{L}(\langle B_{1}(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle)
in probability in
{\mathcal{P}}({{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}])
. This proves the second assertion of the corollary.
∎

As was already observed in
[
30
, Theorem 6.4 and Proposition 7.7]
in the context of interacting diffusions, the stronger local convergence in probability is, in general, necessary to obtain deterministic hydrodynamic limits that coincide with
\mathcal{L}(X_{o}^{G,\xi})
because if one only has local weak convergence of the initial data as in Theorem
4.7
, the hydrodynamic limit can fail to be deterministic or fail to coincide with
\mathcal{L}(X_{o}^{G,\xi})
even when deterministic.

4.4
Illustrative Example: Ramifications for the SIR Model

In this section, we use our running example of the heterogeneous SIR model from (
1.2
) to highlight some direct implications of Theorems
4.3
,
4.7
,
4.11
, and Corollary
4.12
. Similar conclusions can be drawn for the other examples presented in Section
3.2
and other IPS models that satisfy our assumptions.

Recall that
{\mathcal{X}}=\{0,1,2\}
is the state space of the model,
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is the vertex mark space representing the recovery rates of different individuals, and
\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}={\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is the edge mark space containing marks that capture the rate at which a vertex infects its neighbor. Let
\nu_{v}
and
\nu_{e}
denote two probability distributions with bounded support on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Recall that the (possibly time-varying) functions
\lambda,\rho:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
represent the respective rates at which an infected particle infects its neighboring particles and at which a particle recovers from infection. Assume that the two functions are bounded on compact sets:
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max\{\lambda(t),\rho(t)\}<\infty
for all
T<\infty
. Let
G_{n}
be a sequence of Erdös-Rényi graphs on
n
vertices with edge probability
c/n\wedge 1
for some
c\in(0,\infty)
, and let
o_{n}
be uniformly distributed in
V_{G_{n}}
. Also, let
G
be the UGW(Poiss(
c
)) tree, with
o
denoting its root. In addition, let
\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n},n\in{\mathbb{N}},
be i.i.d distributed according to
\nu_{e}
and let
\mathbf{\kappa},\mathbf{\kappa}^{n},n\in{\mathbb{N}},
be i.i.d. distributed according to
\nu_{v}
. Fix
p\in(0,1)
and let
\xi^{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\xi
be vectors of i.i.d.
\{0,1\}
Bernoulli (
p
) random variables. This model is precisely Example
3.4
, so it follows from Appendix
C.3
that Assumptions
1
and
2
hold for this model, and from Examples
2.5
and
2.8
that
(G_{n},\xi_{n})
converges locally (and in probability) to
(G,\xi)
. Then by Theorem
4.3
,
\langle(G,X)\rangle
is well defined. And by Theorem
4.7
(respectively, Theorem
4.11
)
\langle(G_{n},o_{n},X^{n})\rangle
converges locally weakly (respectively, in probability) to
\langle(G,X)\rangle
.

We present examples of several macroscopic quantities of interest whose convergence can be deduced from Corollary
4.12
. When
G
is a UGW tree, a tractable characterization of the limit has recently been obtained in
[
13
]
.

A. Proportion of Infected Particles:

We start with a macroscopic quantity that depends only on time marginals. Fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and let
P_{n}(t)
be the proportion of infected particles at time
t
for the SIR model on
G_{n}
. Then we claim that

P_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{|V_{G_{n}}|}\sum_{v\in V_{G_{n}}}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{X^{n}_{v}(t)=1\right\}}\to{\mathbb{P}}(X_{o}(t)=1)\text{ in probability}.

Justification
: Since discontinuities of the function
f_{1}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
defined by
f_{1}(x)=\mathbb{I}_{\left\{x(t)=1\right\}}
lie in the
\mathcal{L}(X_{o})
-null set
\{x:t\in{\rm Disc}_{\infty}\left(x\right)\}
,
f_{1}
is a.s. continuous with respect to
\mathcal{L}(X_{o})
. Hence, applying Corollary
4.12
, it follows that in probability,

\int f_{1}(x)\,\pi^{G_{n},X^{n}}(dx)=\frac{1}{|V_{G_{n}}|}\sum_{v\in V_{G_{n}}}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{X^{n}_{v}(t)=1\right\}}\to\int f_{1}(x)\,\mu[\{o\}](dx)={\mathbb{P}}(X_{o}(t)=1).

B. The Average Duration of Infection of Particles:

We now look at a macroscopic quantity that depends on the trajectories of particles. Fix
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\infty
, and let
T_{n}(t)
denote the average duration of the infection time of a particle in the interval
[t_{1},t_{2}]
for the SIR model on
G_{n}
. Then we claim that

T_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{|V_{G_{n}}|}\sum_{v\in V_{G_{n}}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{X^{n}_{v}(t)=1\right\}}\,dt\to\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{o}(t)=1)\,dt\quad\text{ in probability.}

Justification:
Note that the above display is equivalent to the statement that
\int f_{2}(x)\,\pi^{G_{n},X^{n}}(dx)\to\int f_{2}(x)\,{\rm Law}(X_{o})(dx)
in probability, where
f
is the bounded, continuous function
f_{2}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
defined as
f_{2}(x):=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{x(t)=1\right\}}\,dt.
The claim then follows from Corollary
4.12
, which ensures that
\pi^{G_{n},X^{n}}
converges to
{\rm Law}(X_{o})
in probability.

C. The Average Proportion of Infected Neighbors of a Susceptible Particle:

We now look at a quantity that depends on the neighborhood graph structure. Fix

t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and let
A_{n}(t)
denote the average proportion of neighbors of susceptible particles in the graph
G_{n}
that are infected at time
t
(interpreted as equal to zero if no particle is susceptible at time
t
). Then we claim that

A_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{|\{v\in V_{G_{n}}:X^{n}_{v}(t)=0\}|}\sum_{v:X^{n}_{v}(t)=0}\frac{1}{|{\mathcal{N}}_{v}(G_{n})|}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{N}}_{v}(G_{n})}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{X^{n}_{u}(t)=1\right\}}

converges in probability to

A(t):=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{|{\mathcal{N}}_{o}(G)|}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{N}}_{o}(G)}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{X_{u}(t)=1\right\}}\middle|X_{o}(t)=0\right]

where
0/0
is assigned the value
0
(
A(t)
is well defined by the remark below). In other words, the limit is the expected proportion of neighbors of the root
o
for the SIR model on
G
that are infected at time
t
, conditioned on the event that
X_{o}
is susceptible at time
t.

Justification:
Recall that
{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}[\{1\},{\mathcal{D}}]\subset{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\{1\},{\mathcal{D}}]
is the space of isomorphism classes of graphs of radius
1
with vertex marks in
{\mathcal{D}}
. Fix
t>0
, and let
f_{3},f_{4}:{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}[\{1\},{\mathcal{D}}]\to[0,1]
be defined by

f_{3}(\langle(H,x)\rangle):=\frac{\mathbb{I}_{\left\{x_{o_{H}}(t)=0\right\}}}{|V_{H}|-1}\sum_{u\in V_{H}\setminus\{o_{H}\}}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{x_{u}(t)=1\right\}}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{4}(\langle(H,x)\rangle):=\mathbb{I}_{\left\{x_{o_{H}}(t)=0\right\}}.

Also, recall from (
4.2
) that

\overline{\pi}^{G_{n},X^{n}}_{\overline{o}}=\frac{1}{|G_{n}|}\sum_{v\in V_{G_{n}}}\delta_{\langle B_{1}(\mathscr{C}_{v}(G,z))\rangle},

and set
\overline{\mu}:=\mathcal{L}(\langle B_{1}{(G,X)}\rangle).
Then observe that

A_{n}(t)=\frac{\int f_{3}(\langle(H,x)\rangle)\,\overline{\pi}^{G_{n},X^{n}}_{\overline{o}}(d\langle(H,x)\rangle)}{\int f_{4}(\langle(H,x)\rangle)\,\overline{\pi}^{G_{n},X^{n}}_{\overline{o}}(d\langle(H,x)\rangle)}

and

A(t)=\frac{\int f_{3}(\langle(H,x)\rangle)\,\overline{\mu}[\text{cl}_{o}](d\langle(H,x)\rangle)}{\int f_{4}(\langle(H,x)\rangle)\,\overline{\mu}[\text{cl}_{o}](d\langle(H,x)\rangle)}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{I}_{\left\{X_{o}(t)=0\right\}}}{|{\mathcal{N}}_{o}(G)|}\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{N}}_{o}(G)}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{X_{u}(t)=1\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{P}}(X_{o}(t)=0)},

once more assigning the value of
0
to
0/0
. Since the dynamics are driven by i.i.d. Poisson processes, it is not hard to see that the discontinuities of both
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
lie in the
\overline{\mu}[\text{cl}_{o}]
-null set
\{\langle(H,x)\rangle\in{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]:t\in{\rm Disc}_{\infty}\left(x\right)\}
, so both functions are bounded and a.s. continuous with respect to
\overline{\mu}
. Hence, by Corollary
4.12
and the continuous mapping theorem,
\langle f_{j},\overline{\pi}^{G_{n},X^{n}}_{\overline{o}}\rangle\to\langle f_{j},\overline{\mu}[\text{cl}_{o}]\rangle
in probability for
j=3,4.
Below, we show that the denominator of the limit is a.s. positive, so the claim follows.

Remark.
We claim that
A(t)
is well defined because
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{o}(t)=0)>0
. To see why this holds, note that because
|{\mathcal{N}}_{o}(G)|
is an a.s. finite, non-negative (integer) random variable, there must exist
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
q\in(0,1]
such that
{\mathbb{P}}(|{\mathcal{N}}_{o}(G)|=k)=q
. Moreover,
X_{o}(0)=0
with probability
p
, which was assumed to lie in
(0,1).
Lastly, by assumption the distribution
\nu_{e}
has bounded support, and so there exists a constant
\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{*}<\infty
such that
\max\{\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{e^{\prime}}\}\leq\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{*}
a.s. for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
e\in E_{G}
, and
e^{\prime}\in E_{G_{n}}
. Finally, the maximal rate at which
X_{o}(0)
transitions to
1
is bounded from above at each time
s\in[0,t]
by
|{\mathcal{N}}_{o}(G)|\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{*}\lambda(s)
. It therefore follows that

{\mathbb{P}}(X_{o}(t)=0)\geq pq\exp\left(-k\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{*}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s)\,ds\right)\geq pq\exp\left(-kt\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{*}\left(\sup_{s\in[0,t)}\lambda(s)\right)\right)>0,

thus proving the claim.

5
Spatial Localization and the Proof of Well-Posedness

Throughout this section, we assume, sometimes without explicit mention, that we are given a family of regular local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
and that for any graph
G
, the jump rates
{\bf r}^{G}
satisfy the standing assumption, that is, are given in terms of the local jump rates
{\bar{r}}
via (
3.1
). In Section
5.1
, we introduce the notions of spatial localization and consistent spatial localization (see Definitions
5.1
and
5.4
) and in Section
5.2
show that the SDE (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for any marked graph that “spatially localizes” the IPS. In Section
5.3
, we define the class of finitely dissociable graphs and provide examples of graphs in this class. In Section
5.4
, we show that sequences of such graphs consistently spatially localize the SDE (
3.4
).

5.1
Spatial Localization and Consistent Spatial Localization

Throughout this section, we assume the local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
satisfy the following mild assumption, which is clearly weaker than Assumption
1
in light of Proposition
4.2
.

Assumption
1
′

(Strong well-posedness for finite initial data)
.

The family of local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
is such that for any finite initial data
(G,\xi)
, the SDE (
3.4
) with jump rates
{\bf r^{G}}
defined in terms of
{\bf\bar{r}}
via (
3.1
) is strongly well-posed.

We now introduce the notion of spatial localization for a given rooted graph
G
(and local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
). Intuitively, we would like to say that a deterministic
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-graph
G
spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
) if for any finite
T>0
and
{\mathcal{O}}\subseteq V_{G}
there exists an (in general random)
finite
vertex subset
U\subseteq V_{G}
that contains
{\mathcal{O}}
with the property that on the time interval
[0,T]
, the
{\mathcal{O}}
-marginal of any weak solution to the IPS on
G
coincides with the
{\mathcal{O}}
-marginal of the IPS dynamics on the subgraph
G[U]
. However, for such a characterization to make sense, the IPS dynamics
X^{G[U],\xi_{U}}
on
G[U]
needs to be well defined, that is, the IPS needs to be well-posed for the initial data
(G[U],\xi_{U})
. Unfortunately, the subtlety is that even though
U
is (almost surely) finite, Assumption
′
‣
5.1
does not automatically guarantee this well-posedness. This is because if
X^{G,\xi}
is an
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
-weak solution (in the sense of Remark
3.9
) to the SDE (
3.4
) for the initial data
(G,\xi)
, then for any finite
{\mathcal{O}}
, the corresponding random graph
G[U]
will generally fail to be
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable. Thus
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G[U]}):=({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G}_{U})
need not be a valid driving noise compatible with the IPS on
G[U]
(in the sense of Definition
3.7
).

However, this technical difficulty can be overcome by instead considering the sequence of deterministic graphs
H_{\ell}:=B_{\ell}(G)
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},
on each of which the IPS (with the corresponding initial condition) is well-posed by Assumption
′
‣
5.1
. One can then equivalently define spatial localization for
G
via the condition that almost surely the
{\mathcal{O}}
-marginal dynamics of any weak solution
X^{G,\xi}
to the IPS on
G
coincides with the
{\mathcal{O}}
-marginal dynamics of the IPS on
H_{\ell}
for some
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+},
or in other words,
\bigcup_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}\{X^{G,\xi}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{V_{H_{\ell}}}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]\}
.

We now state the precise formulation. Recall from Section
2.1
that

\Lambda_{G}:=\{U\subset V_{G}:|U|<\infty\}.

In what follows, we deal with
deterministic
initial data
(G,\xi)
, recall the notion of a filtration-Poisson process pair
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
from Remark
3.9
, and recall its associated augmented filtration
{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathbf{N}^{G}}=\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}^{G}}\}_{t\geq 0}
introduced prior to Definition
3.8
.

Definition 5.1

(Spatial localization)
.

A deterministic
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-graph
G
is said to spatially localize the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
if for any filtration-Poisson process pair
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
defined on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
and
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, there exists a (random) mapping
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G,\mathbf{N}^{G}):\Lambda_{G}\times\Omega\to 2^{V_{G}}
such that for every
{\mathcal{O}},U\in\Lambda_{G}
,

\{\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})\subseteq U\}:=\{\omega\in\Omega:\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})(\omega)\subseteq U\}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{T}^{\mathbf{N}^{G}},

(5.1)

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})\in\Lambda_{G})=1,

(5.2)

and the following properties hold:

1.

for each
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
,
\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})(\omega)\supseteq{\mathcal{O}}
for every
\omega\in\Omega
;

2.

given any
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{G}
, and
{\mathcal{O}},U\subset V
such that
{\mathcal{O}}\subseteq B_{\ell}(G)\subseteq U
, every
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
-weak solution
X^{G[U],\xi_{U}}
to the SDE (
3.4
) (with local jump rates
\mathbf{\bar{r}}
) for the initial data
(G[U],\xi_{U})
satisfies

X^{G[U],\xi_{U}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]=X^{B_{\ell}(G),\xi_{B_{\ell}(G)}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]\quad\text{ a.s. on the event }\{\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})\subseteq B_{\ell}(G)\},

(5.3)

where
X^{B_{\ell}(G),\xi_{B_{\ell}(G)}}
is the unique
\mathbf{N}^{G}_{B_{\ell}(G)}
-strong solution to the SDE (
3.4
) (with local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
) for the initial data
(B_{\ell}(G),\xi_{B_{\ell}(G)})
. In this case
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
is said to be a localizing map for the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
on
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
.

Remark 5.2

(Definition
5.1
notation)
.

In Definition
5.1
, we may omit one or both of the last two arguments of
\mathcal{S}_{T}
when the graph and/or Poisson processes are clear from the context. We will also typically omit explicit mention of the local jump rates when clear from context.

Condition (
5.1
) ensures that the localizing set
\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
is measurable (with respect to
{\mathcal{F}}_{T}^{\mathbf{N}^{G}}
), and condition (
5.2
) encodes the first key property that it must be almost surely finite. Property 1 simply states the obvious fact that the set of particles, equivalently driving Poisson processes, that affect the dynamics on any set
{\mathcal{O}}
must clearly include those associated with the set
{\mathcal{O}}
itself, and property 2 captures the key intuitive property that the marginal dynamics on the set
{\mathcal{O}}
of the IPS on the graph
G
and the localizing set (almost surely) coincide on the interval
[0,T]
, or stated more precisely, requires the marginal dynamics on
{\mathcal{O}}
over the interval
[0,T]
of the IPS on the graph
G
and on the
\ell
-neighborhood of the root to be (almost surely) identical on
[0,T]
whenever the
\ell
-neighborhood is large enough to include the spatially localizing set.

Remark 5.3

(Spatial localization non-uniqueness)
.

It should be clear from the above discussion that when
G
spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
), there may be many choices for the localizing map
\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
. Indeed, any almost surely finite, measurable set that contains a localizing set is also a localizing set. One natural choice is to let
\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
be minimal, that is, define it to be precisely the set of particles in
G
that influence the IPS dynamics of the particles in
{\mathcal{O}}
during the time interval
[0,T]
, as illustrated in Figure
1
. Two alternative constructions are used in the proof of Proposition
5.17
in Section
5.4
.

{\mathcal{O}}

(a)

\mathbb{T}_{3}[U]

{\mathcal{O}}

(b)

B_{3}(\mathbb{T}_{3})

{\mathcal{O}}

(c)

B_{2}(\mathbb{T}_{3})

Figure 1
:
\mathbb{T}_{3}
is the infinite regular tree,
{\mathcal{O}},U
are deterministic subsets of its vertices, with

U
containing
S:=\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};\mathbb{T}_{3},\mathbf{N}^{\mathbb{T}_{3}})(\omega)
, a realization of the (minimal) spatially localizing set for
{\mathcal{O}}
, and
\mathbb{T}_{3}[U]
is the subgraph of
\mathbb{T}_{3}
induced by
U
;
Figures (a), (b), and (c) describe the (realization
\omega
of the) dynamics of the IPS on
\mathbb{T}_{3}[U]
,
B_{3}[\mathbb{T}_{3}]
, and
B_{2}[\mathbb{T}_{3}]
, respectively, with particles colored black if their dynamics on
[0,T]
coincide with the IPS on
\mathbb{T}_{3}[U]
, and colored red if their dynamics differ. The fact that all particles in
{\mathcal{O}}
are colored black in (b) but not in (c) reflects the fact that

S\subseteq B_{3}[\mathbb{T}_{3}]
, but

S\not\subseteq B_{2}[\mathbb{T}_{3}]
, as is consistent with property (
5.3
).

Given the fixed IPS model (equivalently, a family of local jump rates
\mathbf{\bar{r}}
satisfying Assumption
′
‣
5.1
) we now introduce the concept of consistent spatial localization of a sequence of graphs, which will be used in the proofs of local convergence and the hydrodynamic limit in Sections
6
and
7
, respectively.

Definition 5.4

(Consistent Spatial Localization)
.

A sequence of
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-graphs
\{G_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
defined on some common probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
is said to consistently spatially localize the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
if for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and corresponding filtration-Poisson process pair
({\mathbb{F}}^{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
, there exists a mapping
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}):\Lambda_{G_{n}}\times\Omega\to 2^{V_{G_{n}}}
that satisfies the following properties:

1.

for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
is a localizing map for the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
\mathbf{\bar{r}}
on
(G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
in the sense of Definition
5.1
;

2.

for every
n,n^{\prime},\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that there exists an isomorphism
\varphi\in I(B_{\ell}([G_{n^{\prime},*}],B_{\ell}([G_{n,*}]))
, the following property holds: for each pair of sets
{\mathcal{O}}\subseteq B_{\ell}(G_{n})
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}:=\varphi^{-1}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq B_{\ell}(G_{n^{\prime}})
,

\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})=\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}^{\prime};G_{n^{\prime}},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}}}))

a.s. on the event
\{\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})\subseteq B_{\ell}(G_{n})\}\cap{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}
, where
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}
is the
{\mathcal{F}}_{T}^{\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}}}}\vee{\mathcal{F}}_{T}^{\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}}
measurable event given by

{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}:=\{\varphi\in I(B_{\ell}([G_{n^{\prime},*}],\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}}}),B_{\ell}([G_{n,*}],\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}))\}.

In this case
\{\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is said to be a consistent sequence of localizing maps for the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
\mathbf{\bar{r}}
on
\{(G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.

In Proposition
5.17
, we show that any sequence of graphs that is a subset of the class of so-called finitely dissociative graphs (see Definition
5.11
) consistently spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
).

It is not hard to show that (consistent) spatial localization is a property of isomorphism classes. We omit the proof as it follows easily from the definition.

Definition 5.5

(Spatial localization by isomorphism classes)
.

An isomorphism class
\langle G\rangle\in{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
is said to spatially localize the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
\mathbf{\bar{r}}
whenever each representative
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-graph
G
does the same. A sequence of isomorphism classes
\{\langle G_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
is said to consistently spatially localize the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
\mathbf{\bar{r}}
if every sequence of representative graphs
G_{n}\in\langle G_{n}\rangle,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, does the same.

Note that while spatial localization is a class property, the notion of a localizing map given in Definition
5.1
applies to a given graph and not its isomorphism class.

Remark 5.6

(Spatial localization for more general IPS)
.

Definitions
5.1
and
5.4
are abstract properties that easily extend to a more general class of graph-indexed jump processes
X^{G,\xi}
that satisfy a different Poisson-driven SDE from (
3.4
), as long as Assumption
′
‣
5.1
still holds for that SDE. For instance, they could apply to IPS such as the exclusion process in which particles may experience simultaneous jumps, where it would be more natural to index
\mathbf{N}^{G}
by the edges, rather than the vertices, of
G
.

5.2
Well-posedness on spatially localizing graphs

We now show that strong well-posedness holds on spatially localizing graphs under the rather mild Assumption
′
‣
5.1
.

Proposition 5.7

(Well-posedness for IPS with spatially localizing initial data)
.

Suppose the local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
satisfy Assumption
′
‣
5.1
and that
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
is a
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-valued random element such that
\langle G\rangle
a.s. spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
in the sense of Definition
5.5
. Then the SDE (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for the initial data
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.7
, there exists a
\sigma(\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
-measurable
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random graph
(G,\xi)
such that
(G,\xi)\in\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
a.s.. By Definition
3.13
and Lemma
3.12
, it suffices to prove that (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for a.s. every realization of
(G,\xi)
. Therefore, for the remainder of the proof, we assume without loss of generality that
(G,\xi)
is a deterministic
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-graph where
G
spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
).

We now explicitly construct a strong solution to the SDE (
3.4
) for
(G,\xi)
. Let
\mathbf{N}^{G}=\{\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}\}_{v\in V_{G}}
be a collection of i.i.d. Poisson processes on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\times{\mathcal{J}}
with intensity
{\rm Leb}^{2}\otimes\varsigma
. Let
{\mathbb{F}}:=\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}:={\mathbb{F}}^{\mathbf{N}^{G}}
be the associated filtration (as defined prior to Definition
3.8
). It is clear from Definition
3.7
that
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
is an
{\mathbb{F}}
-driving noise. So by Remark
3.9
,
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
is a filtration-Poisson process pair. Fix
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot):=\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
be a corresponding localizing map, which exists due to our assumption that
G
spatially localizes the SDE. For notational conciseness, let
B_{m}:=B_{m}(G)
for each
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and additionally fix
\xi
and omit the dependence on
\xi
from the superscript, with the understanding that the mark is always the restriction of
\xi
to the corresponding graph in the superscript. Furthermore, for any
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
, recalling that by Assumption
′
‣
5.1
the SDE (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for the finite data
(B_{\ell},\xi_{B_{\ell}})
, we let
X^{B_{\ell}}
denote the corresponding
\mathbf{N}^{G}_{B_{\ell}}
-strong solution to (
3.4
). By Definition
5.1
, for each
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a random finite set
U_{m}:=\mathcal{S}_{T}(B_{m})
such that for any
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell\geq m
, by (
5.1
) and applying (
5.3
) with
U=B_{\ell^{\prime}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}=B_{m}
,

X^{B_{\ell}}_{B_{m}}[T]=X^{B_{\ell^{\prime}}}_{B_{m}}[T]\quad\mbox{ on the event }\{U_{m}\subseteq B_{\ell}\}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{T}.

For
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define the
{\mathcal{D}}_{T}^{B_{m}}
-valued random element
X^{m}
and the random integer
M_{m}
as follows:

X^{m}[T]:=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}X^{B_{\ell}}_{B_{m}}[T]\quad\mbox{ and }\quad M_{m}:=\inf\{\ell\geq m:U_{m}\subseteq B_{\ell}\}.

(5.4)

Since
\bigcup_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}B_{\ell}=V_{G}
and
U_{m}
is a.s. finite,
X^{m}
is well defined on a set of full measure and
M_{m}
is a.s. finite. Moreover, since
X^{B_{\ell}}
is
{\mathbb{F}}
-adapted for every
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathbb{F}}
is complete, it follows that
X^{m}[T]
is also
{\mathbb{F}}
-adapted. The last two displays together then imply that a.s.,

X^{m}[T]=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}X^{B_{\ell}}_{B_{m}}[T]=X^{B_{M_{m}}}_{B_{m}}[T].

(5.5)

Furthermore, clearly the sequence
(X^{m}[T])_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is consistent: for any
m^{\prime}>m
, a.s.,

X^{m^{\prime}}_{B_{m}}[T]=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}(X^{B_{\ell}}_{B_{m^{\prime}}})_{B_{m}}[T]=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}X^{B_{\ell}}_{B_{m}}[T]=X^{m}[T]=X^{m}_{B_{m}}[T],

(5.6)

where the first and third equalities invoke (
5.5
) and the remaining equalities hold trivially. Now, for every
v\in V_{G}
, there exists an integer
{m_{v}}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
v\in B_{m_{v}}
, and the last display shows that
X^{m_{v}}_{v}[T]=X^{m^{\prime}}_{v}[T]
a.s. when
m^{\prime}\geq{m_{v}}
. Because
V_{G}
is countable and
{\mathbb{F}}
is complete, we can define the
{\mathbb{F}}
-adapted
{\mathcal{D}}^{G}_{T}
-random element
X[T]
by setting

X_{v}[T]:=\lim_{m\to\infty}X^{m}_{v}[T]=X^{m_{v}}_{v}[T],\quad v\in V_{G},

(5.7)

on the set of measure one where the latter limits exist, and setting
X_{v}[T]\equiv\xi_{v},v\in V_{G},
on the complement.

To show that the
X[T]
thus constructed is an
\mathbf{N}^{G}
-strong solution to the SDE (
3.4
) on
[0,T]
, fix
v\in V_{G}
and define
\bar{m}_{v}:=\max\{m_{u}:u\in\text{cl}_{v}\}
. Then
\text{cl}_{v}\subset B_{\bar{m}_{v}}
and from (
5.5
)–(
5.7
), it follows that for
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

X_{\text{cl}_{v}}[T]=X^{\bar{m}_{v}}_{\text{cl}_{v}}[T]=X^{B_{\ell}}_{\text{cl}_{v}}[T]\quad\mbox{ on }\quad A_{\ell}(v):=\{\ell\geq M_{\bar{m}_{v}}\}.

(5.8)

Since
X^{B_{\ell}}
is a
\mathbf{N}^{G}_{B_{\ell}}
-strong solution to the SDE (
3.4
), we obtain a.s., for
t\in[0,T],

\displaystyle X_{v}(t)\mathbb{I}_{A_{\ell}(v)}

\displaystyle=X^{B_{\ell}}_{v}(t)\mathbb{I}_{A_{\ell}(v)}

\displaystyle=\left(\xi_{v}+\int_{(0,t]\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{J}}}j\mathbb{I}_{\left\{r\leq r^{G[B_{\ell}],v}_{j}(s,X^{B_{\ell}})\right\}}\,\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}(ds,dr,dj)\right)\mathbb{I}_{A_{\ell}(v)}.

By (
3.1
) and (
5.8
), it follows that with
H:=G[\text{cl}_{v}]
,

\displaystyle r^{G[B_{\ell}],v}_{j}(s,X^{B_{\ell}})

\displaystyle=\bar{r}^{H}_{j}\left(s,X^{B_{\ell}}_{\text{cl}_{v}},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e})_{e\in E_{H}},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{u})_{u\in\text{cl}_{v}}\right)

\displaystyle=\bar{r}^{H}_{j}\left(s,X_{\text{cl}_{v}},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e})_{e\in E_{H}},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{u})_{u\in\text{cl}_{v}}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{G,v}_{j}(s,X),

for every
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
and
s\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
on the event
A_{\ell}(v)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle X_{v}(t)\mathbb{I}_{A_{\ell}(v)}

\displaystyle=\left(\xi_{v}+\int_{(0,t]\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{J}}}j\mathbb{I}_{\left\{r\leq r^{G,v}_{j}(s,X)\right\}}\,\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}(ds,dr,dj)\right)\mathbb{I}_{A_{\ell}(v)}.

Then, a.s., since
M_{\bar{m}_{v}}
is finite, taking the limit as
\ell\to\infty
shows that

X_{v}(t)=\xi_{v}+\int_{(0,t]\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{J}}}j\mathbb{I}_{\left\{r\leq r^{G,v}_{j}(s,X)\right\}}\,\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}(ds,dr,dj).

Thus, we have proved the existence of an
\mathbf{N}^{G}
-strong solution to (
3.4
) on any interval
[0,T]
.

We now turn to the proof of pathwise uniqueness. Suppose that
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
are any two
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
-weak solutions to the SDE (
3.4
) for
(G,\xi)
. Since
G
spatially localizes the SDE, for any
\ell>m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
M_{m}
defined as in (
5.4
), invoking (
5.1
) and applying (
5.3
), with
U=V
and
{\mathcal{O}}=B_{m}
, to both weak solutions
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{X}^{\prime}
, we obtain a.s. on the event
\{\ell\geq M_{m}\}=\{\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})\subseteq B_{\ell}(G)\}\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbf{N}^{G}}_{T}
,

\widetilde{X}_{B_{m}}[T]=X^{B_{\ell}(G)}_{B_{m}}[T]=\widetilde{X}^{\prime}_{B_{m}}[T],

where recall from Remark
5.1
that
X^{B_{\ell}(G)}
is the unique
\mathbf{N}^{G}_{B_{\ell}(G)}
-strong solution to (
3.4
) for the initial data
B_{\ell}(G,\xi)
. Taking the limit as
\ell\to\infty
and using the almost sure finiteness of
M_{m}
, it follows that
\widetilde{X}_{B_{m}}[T]=X_{B_{m}}[T]=\widetilde{X}^{\prime}_{B_{m}}[T]
a.s. for every
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, which in turn shows that
X[T]=\widetilde{X}[T]=\widetilde{X}^{\prime}[T]
a.s..

Since
T
is arbitrary for both existence and pathwise uniqueness,
X[T]
,
T>0,
are consistent. So there exists a.s. a unique pathwise extension
X
of the strong solution to all of
[0,\infty)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.8

(Well-posedness for more general IPS)
.

Most of the proof of Proposition
5.7
also extends to more general IPS. For instance, if an IPS has simultaneous jumps, then the proof will hold given Assumption
′
‣
5.1
and spatial localization (see Remark
5.6
) except for the verification that
X:=\lim_{m\to\infty}X^{m}
solves (
3.4
). Instead of the latter, one would have to prove that if
X^{B_{\ell}}
satisfies the SDE defining the new model on the finite graph
B_{\ell}(G)
, and
X^{m}
is as defined in (
5.5
), then the limit
X=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}X^{m}
also satisfies that SDE on the infinite graph
G
.

5.3
Finitely Dissociable Graphs

5.3.1
Definition of Finitely Dissociable Graphs

We now introduce the class of finitely dissociable graphs, which are defined in terms of an inhomogeneous site percolation on the graph. Recall the intuitive description of finite dissociability given prior to Theorem
4.3
, and also recall the definition of the measure space
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathbb{N}}}(0,T]
from Section
2.3
.

Definition 5.9

(Percolation)
.

For any
0<T<\infty
, let
(G,\mathbf{\zeta})
be a
[\{1\},{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathbb{N}}}(0,T]]
-random graph. Fix
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T
, and set
R_{v}=R_{v}(t_{1},t_{2}):=\mathbb{I}_{\left\{\mathbf{\zeta}_{v}(t_{1},t_{2}]>0\right\}}
for
v\in V_{G}
. Then the percolated graph
\text{perc}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(G,\mathbf{\zeta})
is defined to be the (possibly disconnected and random) subgraph of
G
induced by the vertex set
\{v\in V_{G}:R_{v}=1\}
. When
t_{1}=0
, we write
\text{perc}_{t_{2}}(G,\mathbf{\zeta}):=\text{perc}_{0,t_{2}}(G,\mathbf{\zeta})
.

In the percolation, we refer to vertices
v\in V_{G}
with
R_{v}=1
as active and those with
R_{v}=0
as inactive. In our application of Definition
5.9
, the vertex marks
\mathbf{\zeta}
of the graph
G
are realizations of (Poisson) point processes.

Definition 5.10

(
\Delta
-dissociation)
.

Given
0<\Delta\leq T<\infty
, we say a
[\{1\},{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathbb{N}}}(0,T]]
-graph
(G,\mathbf{\zeta})

\Delta
-
dissociates
if all connected components of
\text{perc}_{\Delta}(G,\mathbf{\zeta})
are finite.

For any graph
G
, let
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
be a driving noise in the sense of Definition
3.7
. Also, suppose we are given a family of constants
{\bf C}:=\{C_{k,T}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}},T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}
. Then for
T\in(0,\infty)
, let
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}=(\mathbf{N}_{v}^{G,T})_{v\in V_{G}}
be the collection of point processes on
[0,T]
defined by

\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v}(t_{1},t_{2}]:=\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}\left((t_{1},t_{2}]\times(0,C_{|\text{cl}_{v}|,T}]\times{\mathcal{J}}\right),\quad\mbox{ for }0<t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T<\infty,v\in V_{G},

(5.9)

Note that the union of the events of
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v},v\in V_{G},
almost surely contains the set of discontinuities of any weak solution
X^{G,\xi}[T]
of the SDE (
3.4
) with jump rates satisfying Assumption
1
with the family of constants
C
.

Definition 5.11

(Finite dissociability)
.

A deterministic graph
G
is said to be
finitely dissociable
with respect to the family of constants
{\bf C}:=\{C_{k,T}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}},T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}\subset(0,\infty)
if for any
T\in(0,\infty)
, there exists
\Delta=\Delta_{T}>0
such that the associated collection of point processes
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}
defined in (
5.9
) is such that
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})

\Delta
-dissociates a.s.. If
G
is a marked graph, we say
G
is finitely dissociable (with respect to
{\bf C}
) if and only if the corresponding unmarked graph
[G]
is finitely dissociable.

Remark 5.12

(Vertex removal probabilities)
.

In the percolated graph
\text{perc}_{\Delta}(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
described in Definition
5.11
, setting

\bar{C}_{k,T}:=\varsigma({\mathcal{J}})C_{k,T},\quad\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}},T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

(5.10)

each vertex
v
is removed from
G
independently with a probability
\exp\left(-\bar{C}_{|\text{cl}_{v}|,T}\Delta\right)
, which is decreasing in the degree of the vertex
v
.

Remark 5.13

(Finite dissociability of isomorphism classes)
.

Finite dissociability with respect to any family of constants
\bm{C}
is a “class property” in that it depends only on the isomorphism class
\langle G\rangle\in{\mathscr{G}}_{*}
of the graph
G
, and not on the particular representative or choice of the driving noise. Indeed, if
G_{1}\cong G_{2}
, then for any fixed
T\in(0,\infty)
and Poisson processes
\mathbf{N}^{G_{i},T}
,
i=1,2
, constructed as in (
5.9
), it is easy to construct a coupling
(\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{G_{1},T},\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{G_{2},T})
of
(\mathbf{N}^{G_{1},T},\mathbf{N}^{G_{2},T})
in which
\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{G_{i},T}\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\mathbf{N}^{G_{i},T}
for
i=1,2,
such that
(G_{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{G_{1},T})\cong(G_{2},\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{G_{2},T})
a.s.. Hence, for
\Delta\in[0,T]
,

\text{perc}_{\Delta}(G_{1},\mathbf{N}^{G_{1},T})\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\text{perc}_{\Delta}(G_{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{G_{1},T})\cong\text{perc}_{\Delta}(G_{2},\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{G_{2},T})\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\text{perc}_{\Delta}(G_{2},\mathbf{N}^{G_{2},T}),

with the equivalence holding a.s.. Since the finite dissociability of
G_{i}
only depends on the probability that
\text{perc}_{\Delta}(G_{i},\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
has an infinite component for some
\Delta>0
, and the existence of an infinite component is isomorphism invariant, this shows that the finite dissociability property is also invariant with respect to graph isomorphisms. Thus, the statement “
\langle G\rangle\in{\mathscr{G}}_{*}
is (or is not) finitely dissociable with respect to the family of constants
\bm{C}
” is well defined .

5.3.2
Examples of Finitely Dissociable Graphs

We now show that the class of (almost surely) finitely dissociable graphs encompasses several interesting classes of graphs of interest in applications, including lattices and regular, GW and UGW trees.

We start by recalling that a rooted tree is a connected, rooted acyclic graph
{\mathcal{T}}=(V,E,o)
. Any pair of vertices
u,v\in V
has a unique path connecting them. A vertex
v\in V
is said to be in the
\ell
th generation of
{\mathcal{T}}
if
d_{\mathcal{T}}(o,v)=\ell
. A finite tree
{\mathcal{T}}
is said to be an
\ell
-generation tree if
\max_{v\in V_{{\mathcal{T}}}}d_{{\mathcal{T}}}(o,v)=\ell
. For
v\in V\setminus\{o\}
, the parent of
v
, denoted
\pi_{v}({\mathcal{T}})
, is the unique neighbor
u
of
v
such that
d_{\mathcal{T}}(o,u)<d_{\mathcal{T}}(o,v)
. Also,
c_{v}({\mathcal{T}}):={\mathcal{N}}_{v}({\mathcal{T}})\setminus\pi_{v}({\mathcal{T}})
is said to be the children of
v
. For any vertex set
U\subseteq V
, let
c_{U}({\mathcal{T}})=\bigcup_{u\in U}c_{u}({\mathcal{T}})\setminus U.
In addition, given
v,w\in V
,
w
is said to be a descendant of
v
if there exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a path
(v=u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}=w)
in
{\mathcal{T}}
such that for every
i=1,\ldots,n
,
u_{i}=c_{u_{i-1}}({\mathcal{T}})
.

Fix
\rho\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{N}}_{0})
. In view of Definition
2.4
and the notation above, a random tree
{\mathcal{T}}
is a GW
(\rho)
tree if for all
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\{|c_{v}({\mathcal{T}})|\}_{\{v:d_{{\mathcal{T}}}(o,v)=\ell\}}
is a collection of conditionally i.i.d.
\rho
-distributed random variables given
B_{\ell}({\mathcal{T}})
(the conditional independence arises because the set of vertices
\{v:d_{{\mathcal{T}}}(o,v)=\ell\}
is itself random and
\sigma(B_{\ell}({\mathcal{T}}))
-measurable).

Proposition 5.14

(Finite dissociability of GW trees)
.

If
\rho\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{N}}_{0})
has a finite first moment, that is,
\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}k\rho_{k}<\infty
, then the GW tree
{\mathcal{T}}:=GW(\rho)
is a.s. finitely dissociable with respect to any family of constants
{\bf C}
.

Proof.

Fix
T<\infty
,
\Delta\in(0,T)
, and let
{\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}:=\text{perc}_{\Delta}({\mathcal{T}},\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}},T})
and
R_{v}=R_{v}^{\Delta}:=\mathbb{I}_{\left\{\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}},T}_{v}(0,\Delta]>0\right\}}
,
v\in V,
be as in Definition
5.9
. Recall that
{\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}
is precisely the subgraph of
{\mathcal{T}}
induced by active vertices in
V
. Also, recall that for
v\in V_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}}
,
\mathscr{C}_{v}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta})
denotes the connected component of
{\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}
containing
v
, with
v
as its root. With a small abuse of notation, we extend the definition of
\mathscr{C}_{v}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta})
to all
v\in V
by setting
\mathscr{C}_{v}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta})
to be the
1
-vertex graph
\{v\}
for
v\in V\setminus V_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}}
. In addition, for
v\in V
, let
{\mathcal{T}}_{v}
and
{\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}_{v}
be the restrictions of
{\mathcal{T}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}
, respectively, to the set containing
v
and its descendants in
{\mathcal{T}}
. By the self-similarity of the GW tree, for each
v\in V
,
\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{T}},(R_{w})_{w\in V})=\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{T}}_{v},(R_{w})_{w\in V_{{\mathcal{T}}_{v}}})
, and hence,
\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}_{o}|R_{o}=1)=\mathcal{L}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}_{v}|R_{v}=1)
. For
v\in V
,
R_{v}=0
implies
\mathscr{C}_{v}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}_{v})
consists of a single isolated vertex. Hence,
|\mathscr{C}_{o}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta})|<\infty
a.s. if and only if
|\mathscr{C}_{v}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}_{v})|<\infty
a.s. for all
v\in V
, in which case all connected components of
{\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta}
must be a.s. finite. Thus, it suffices to prove that
|\mathscr{C}_{o}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta})|<\infty
a.s..

Since the percolation probability at a site or vertex depends on its degree via the dependence on
C_{k,T}
in (
5.9
), to bound the size of
\mathscr{C}_{o}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta})
, we couple
\mathscr{C}_{o}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta})
with a larger set obtained from a simpler percolation that only removes vertices from the odd generations of
{\mathcal{T}}
. To this end, for any rooted tree
{\mathcal{T}}
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
L_{n}({\mathcal{T}}):=\{v\in V:d_{{\mathcal{T}}}(o,v)=n\}
denote the set of vertices in the
n
th generation. Define the half-percolated forest
\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}:=\text{hperc}_{\Delta}({\mathcal{T}},\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}},T})
to be the subgraph of
{\mathcal{T}}
induced by the vertex set
\{v\in V:R_{v}=1\text{ or }d_{\mathcal{T}}(o,v)\text{ is even}\}
. Then let
\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o}:=\mathscr{C}_{o}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta})
be the subtree of
\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}
that contains the root (note that the root always belongs to
\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}
). Clearly,
|\mathscr{C}_{o}({\mathcal{T}}^{\Delta})|\leq|\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o}|
. Thus, to prove the proposition, it suffices to show that for all sufficiently small
\Delta>0
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})\right|\right]=0,

(5.11)

since then

{\mathbb{P}}(|\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o}|=\infty)={\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})\right|>0\text{ for all }n\in{\mathbb{N}}\right)\leq\inf_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})\right|>0\right)=0.

To prove (
5.11
), choose
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
with
L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})\neq\emptyset
, and fix
v\in L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})
. Recall that for any tree
{\mathcal{T}}
and
v\in V
,
c_{v}({\mathcal{T}})
denotes the collection of children of
v
in
{\mathcal{T}}
, and
\pi_{v}({\mathcal{T}})
is the parent of
v
in
{\mathcal{T}}
whenever
v\neq o
. Moreover, since
c_{v}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})\subseteq L_{2n+1}({\mathcal{T}})
, it follows that
c_{v}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})=c_{v}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta})=\{u\in c_{v}({\mathcal{T}}):R_{u}=1\}
. Also, observe that

\displaystyle c_{c_{v}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o}):=\bigcup_{u\in c_{v}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})}c_{u}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})=\{w\in c_{u}({\mathcal{T}}):u\in c_{v}({\mathcal{T}})\text{ and }R_{u}=1\},

(5.12)

where the equality uses the fact that the half-percolation does not remove any vertices from even generations of the tree. For each
w\in L_{2n}({\mathcal{T}})
, note that
w\in\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}
, but it is possible that
w\notin\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o}
. Define
Z_{\Delta,w}
to be the number of grandchildren of
w
in
\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}
. Since
v\in L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})
,
Z_{\Delta,v}
is also the number of grandchildren of
v
in
\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o}
and hence, by (
5.12
),

\displaystyle Z_{\Delta,v}:=\left|c_{c_{v}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})\right|=\left|c_{c_{v}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})}({\mathcal{T}})\right|

\displaystyle\text{ is }\sigma\left(\{c_{u}({\mathcal{T}})\}_{u\in\{v\}\cup c_{v}({\mathcal{T}})},\{R_{u}\}_{u\in c_{v}({\mathcal{T}})}\right)\text{-measurable.}

(5.13)

Furthermore,
L_{2n}({\mathcal{T}})
and
L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})
are both measurable with respect to
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}:=\sigma(B_{2n}({\mathcal{T}}),\{R_{w}\}_{w\in B_{2n-1}({\mathcal{T}})})
. Let
A\in{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
be an atomic event, that is,
A=\{B_{2n}({\mathcal{T}})=\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}},\{R_{w}\}_{w\in B_{2n-1}({\mathcal{T}})}=\{r_{w}\}_{w\in B_{2n-1}({\mathcal{T}})}\}
for some
\ell
-generation tree
\widetilde{{\mathcal{T}}}
, with
\ell\leq 2n
and some
(r_{w})_{w\in B_{2n-1}({\mathcal{T}})}\in\{0,1\}^{B_{2n-1}({\mathcal{T}})}
. Then, conditioned on
A
, the collection of random variables

\left(\{c_{u}({\mathcal{T}})\}_{u\in\{v\}\cup c_{v}({\mathcal{T}})},\{R_{u}\}_{u\in c_{v}({\mathcal{T}})}\right)_{v\in L_{2n}({\mathcal{T}})}

is equal in distribution to
|L_{2n}({\mathcal{T}})|
independent copies of
\left(\{c_{u}({\mathcal{T}})\}_{u\in\{o\}\cup c_{o}({\mathcal{T}})},\{R_{u}\}_{u\in c_{o}({\mathcal{T}})}\right)
. Together with (
5.12
), this implies that
\gamma_{\Delta}=\gamma_{\Delta,v}:=\mathcal{L}(Z_{\Delta,v}|A)
does not depend on
A
or the specific choice of
v
in
L_{2n}({\mathcal{T}})
. Moreover, conditioned on
A
, (
5.12
) implies

Z_{\Delta,v}=\sum_{u\in c_{v}({\mathcal{T}})}R_{u}|c_{u}({\mathcal{T}})|\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\sum_{u\in c_{o}({\mathcal{T}})}R_{u}|c_{u}({\mathcal{T}})|=Z_{\Delta,o}.

Thus, conditioned on
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
,
\{Z_{\Delta,v}\}_{v\in L_{2n}({\mathcal{T}})}
is equal in distribution to
L_{2n}({\mathcal{T}})
i.i.d. copies of
Z_{\Delta,o}
. Also, for
v\in L_{2n}({\mathcal{T}})
, by the assumption that
\rho
has finite mean

\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\Delta,v}|{\mathcal{H}}_{n}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\Delta,o}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\in c_{o}({\mathcal{T}})}|c_{u}({\mathcal{T}})|\right]=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k\rho_{k}\right)^{2}<\infty.

Since
L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})
is
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
-measurable and
\gamma_{\Delta,v}=\gamma_{\Delta}
for all
v\in L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})
, a recursive calculation shows

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|L_{2n+2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{v\in L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\Delta,v}|{\mathcal{H}}_{n}\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[|L_{2n}(\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\Delta}_{o})|\right]\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\Delta,o}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\Delta,o}\right]^{n+1}.

Thus, to show (
5.11
), it suffices to prove that for sufficiently small
\Delta>0
,
\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\Delta,o}\right]<1
. However, note that for all
\Delta>0
,
\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\Delta,o}\right]<\infty
. Furthermore, the definition of
\Delta
-dissociation clearly implies
\lim_{\Delta\to 0}Z_{\Delta,o}=0
a.s., so the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem implies
\lim_{\Delta\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\Delta,o}\right]=0
, which concludes the proof.
∎

In light of Definition
2.4
, this immediately implies the corresponding result for UGW trees.

Corollary 5.15

(Finite dissociability of UGW trees)
.

If
{\mathcal{T}}\sim\text{UGW}(\rho)
, where
\rho\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{N}}_{0})
has a finite second moment, then
{\mathcal{T}}
is a.s. finitely dissociable with respect to any family of constants
{\bf C}
.

Proof.

The fact that
\rho
has a finite second moment implies that
\widehat{\rho}
in Definition
2.4
has a finite first moment. Let
{\mathcal{T}}_{v}
denote the descendant tree of
T
rooted at
v
. Then for each
v\neq o
,
{\mathcal{T}}_{v}
is a GW
(\widehat{\rho})
-tree, so by Theorem
5.14
, for sufficiently small
\Delta>0
,
\mathscr{C}_{v}(\text{perc}_{\Delta}({\mathcal{T}}_{v},\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{v},T}))
is a.s. finite. Since the
\Delta
-percolated descendant tree of every non-root vertex is finite, it follows that
{\mathcal{T}}
a.s.
\Delta
-dissociates.
∎

For completeness, we also show that graphs with bounded maximum degree (such as infinite lattices) are also finitely dissociable.

Proposition 5.16

(Finite dissociability of bounded degree graphs)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph with finite maximum degree:
d^{*}:=\sup_{v\in V}|{\mathcal{N}}_{v}|<\infty
. Then
G
is finitely dissociable with respect to any family of constants
\bf{C}
.

Proof.

Fix
0<T<\infty
and
\Delta\in(0,T]
. Then by Remark
5.12
, each vertex in
G
is inactive independently with probability

p_{v}=\exp\left(-\Delta\bar{C}_{|\text{cl}_{v}|,T}\right)\geq\exp\left(-\Delta\bar{C}_{d^{*}+1,T}\right):=p_{\Delta,T},

where the inequality invokes (
5.10
), the monotonicity of
C_{k,T}
in both its subscripts, and the definition of
d^{*}
. Therefore, the probability that
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})

\Delta
-dissociates is greater than or equal to the probability that
G
fails to percolate with respect to a standard site percolation in which each vertex is independently removed with probability
p_{\Delta,T}
. For any
T<\infty
,
\lim_{\Delta\to 0}p_{\Delta,T}=1
, but as is well known, the critical probability (i.e., the probability
p_{c}
such that
G
fails to percolate a.s. when vertices are independently removed with probability
p>p_{c}
) is strictly less than 1 (see
[
22
, Equation (0.3)]
). Thus, for all
T
, there exists a sufficiently small
\Delta
such that
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})

\Delta
-dissociates a.s. By Definition
5.11
, this shows that
G
is finitely dissociable.
∎

As demonstrated in Appendix
A
, even for Markovian IPS with very regular jump rate functions, finite dissociability, and well-posedness can fail on some graphs.

5.4
Consistent Spatial Localization on Finitely Dissociative Graph Sequences

The main result of this section is the following:

Proposition 5.17

(Consistent spatial localization by finitely dissociable graphs)
.

Suppose
{\bf\bar{r}}
is a family of regular local jump rates that satisfies Assumption
1
(in the sense of Remark
4.1
) with an associated family of constants
{\bf C}
. If
G
is a deterministic graph that is finitely dissociable with respect to
{\bf C},
then
G
spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rate
{\bf\bar{r}}
in the sense of Definition
5.1
. Moreover, any sequence of deterministic finitely dissociable
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-graphs
\{G_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
that are all finitely dissociable with respect to the constants
{\bf C}
consistently spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
\bf\bar{r}
in the sense of Definition
5.4
.

We first show why the proposition directly implies Theorem
4.3
and subsequently present its proof.

Proof of Theorem
4.3
given Proposition
5.17
:

Given Assumption
1
, Proposition
5.17
, and Definition
5.5
show that
\langle G\rangle
a.s. spatially localizes (
3.4
), and Proposition
4.2
shows that Assumption
′
‣
5.1
holds. Therefore, the theorem follows from Proposition
5.7
.
∎

A key challenge in establishing Proposition
5.17
is to find an explicit and consistent representation of the localizing map
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
on a sequence of finitely dissociative graphs
G_{n}
. To this end, given the family of constants
{\bf C}
, for a fixed graph
G
and
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, we consider the associated processes
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}=(\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v})_{v\in V_{G}}
specified in (
5.9
), and introduce the notion of a
causal chain
associated with
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
. First, define the event set of
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v}
as follows:

{\mathcal{E}}_{v,T}:=\{t\in(0,T]:\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v}(\{t\})=1\}.

(5.14)

Definition 5.18

(Causal chains)
.

Given
T\in(0,\infty)
, an interval
I:=[t_{1},t_{2}]\subseteq[0,T]
and vertices
u,v\in V_{G}
, a
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
-causal chain from
v
to
u
during
I
is either the singleton
(u)
when
v=u
, or for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, a path
\Gamma:=(v=u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{n}=u)
in
G
such that there exists an increasing sequence
t_{1}<s_{1}<\cdots<s_{n}\leq t_{2}
for which
s_{i}\in{\mathcal{E}}_{u_{i},T}
,
i=1,\dots,n
. We write
v\rightsquigarrow_{t_{1},t_{2}}u
if there exists a
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
-causal chain from
v
to
u
during
I=[t_{1},t_{2}]
, and for any
U\subset V_{G}
, we write
v\rightsquigarrow_{t_{1},t_{2}}U
if
v\rightsquigarrow_{t_{1},t_{2}}u
for some
u\in U
.

Intuitively, causal chains describe long-range interactions over the graph that can develop over an interval
I
, even though the instantaneous evolution of the state of a vertex is only influenced by the states of neighboring vertices. Specifically, given a graph
G
, processes
\mathbf{N}^{G,T},T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
, and
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\infty,
define

\mathcal{A}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}):=\{v\in V_{G}:v\rightsquigarrow_{t_{1},t_{2}}{\mathcal{O}}\}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{A}_{t_{1}}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}):=\mathcal{A}_{0,t_{1}}^{G}({\mathcal{O}}),

(5.15)

where
\rightsquigarrow_{t_{1},t_{2}}
indicates the existence of a
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
-causal chain during
[t_{1},t_{2}].
Then
\mathcal{A}_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{G}({\mathcal{O}})
represents the set of vertices in
G
that are “seen” by vertices in
{\mathcal{O}}
through a causal chain during some time interval
[t_{1},t_{2}]
. The proof of Proposition
5.17
proceeds by showing that the (random) map that takes
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
to
\mathcal{A}_{T}^{G}({\mathcal{O}})
for finitely dissociable graphs
G
defines a localizing map, and moreover, that given a sequence of finitely dissociable graphs
G_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the (random) maps that take
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G_{n}}
to the closure
\mathcal{A}_{T}^{G_{n}}({\mathcal{O}})
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
define a consistent family of localizing maps.

Proof of Proposition
5.17
:

We start with the proof of the first statement of the proposition. Fix a deterministic
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-graph
G
and let
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
be a filtration-Poisson process pair. For each (not necessarily finite)
W\subseteq V_{G}
, let
X^{G[W],\xi_{W}}
be an arbitrary
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G}_{W})
-weak solution to (
3.4
) for
(G[W],\xi_{W})
assuming one exists, which is always the case when
W
is finite by Proposition
4.2
and the fact that Assumption
1
holds. Fix the family of constants
{\bf C}
specified in the proposition, and consider the associated processes
(\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v})_{v\in V_{G}},T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, as in (
5.9
). Let
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
, and for
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, set
\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G}):=\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}):=\mathcal{A}_{T}^{G}({\mathcal{O}})
, with the latter defined as in (
5.15
) in terms of
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
causal chains. By Definition
5.18
, for any
U^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{G}
,
\{\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subset U^{\prime}\}
lies in
{\mathcal{F}}_{T}^{\mathbf{N}^{G}}
, and hence
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G,\mathbf{N}^{G})=\mathcal{A}_{T}
satisfies condition (
5.1
) of Definition
5.1
. Moreover, since
u\rightsquigarrow_{0,T}u
for all
u\in{\mathcal{O}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\supseteq{\mathcal{O}},
thus verifying that
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
satisfies property 1 of Definition
5.1
. To prove that any finitely dissociable graph (with respect to
{\bf C}
) spatially localizes (
3.4
), it only remains to verify that
\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})
also satisfies condition (
5.2
) and property 2 of Definition
5.1
. We argue below that it suffices to establish the following claims for every
T\in(0,\infty)
:

Claim 1:

If
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})

T
-dissociates in the sense of Definition
5.10
, then
|\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})|<\infty
a.s. for every
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
.

Claim 2:

If
G
is finitely dissociable, then
|\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})|<\infty
a.s. for every
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
.

Claim 3:

Fix any
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and (not necessarily finite)
U\subseteq V_{G}
such that
B_{\ell}(G)\subseteq U
and a weak solution
X^{G[U],\xi_{U}}
to the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
exists. Then for each
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G[U]}
,

X^{B_{\ell}(G),\xi_{B_{\ell}(G)}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]=X^{G[U],\xi_{U}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]\quad\mbox{ a.s. on }\{\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq B_{\ell}(G)\}.

(5.16)

Claim 2 shows that
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
satisfies (
5.2
) when
G
is finitely dissociable. Suppose Claims 1-3 hold, and let
U\supseteq B_{\ell}(G)
be any (not necessarily finite) vertex set for which there exists a weak solution
X^{G[U],\xi_{U}}
as stated in Claim 3. Then clearly
B_{\ell}(G[U])=B_{\ell}(G)
, so for
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G[U]}
and on the event
\{\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq B_{\ell}(G)\}=\{\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq B_{\ell}(G[U])\}
, the
(G[U],\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{U})
-causal chains ending at
{\mathcal{O}}
at time
T
are the same as the
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
-causal chains ending at
{\mathcal{O}}
at time
T
, that is,
\mathcal{A}_{T}^{G[U]}({\mathcal{O}})=\mathcal{A}_{T}^{G}({\mathcal{O}})=\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})
. Thus, by Claim 3, (
5.16
) holds, which verifies property 2 of Definition
5.1
. Since we have verified all properties of Definition
5.1
when
G
is dissociable (with respect to
{\bf C}
), the first assertion of the proposition follows.

We now turn to the proofs of the claims.

Proof of Claim 1.

Fix
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
. Define
\widehat{G}:=\text{perc}_{T}(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
as in Definition
5.9
. If
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})

T
-dissociates, then each of the connected components of
\widehat{G}
is a.s. finite, and
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{w}\left(0,T\right]=0
for all
w\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}}
, where
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}:={\mathcal{O}}\cup\bigcup_{v\in{\mathcal{O}}}\mathscr{C}_{v}(\widehat{G})
. Since
{\mathcal{O}}
is finite,
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}
is a.s. finite. Now suppose
u\in{\mathcal{O}}
and
v\in\mathcal{A}_{T}(u)
. Let
(v=u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{n}=u)
be a
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
-causal chain with respect to
[0,T]
. Then, for any
i=1,\dots,n
,
u_{i}
must be active, that is,
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{u_{i}}\left(0,T\right]>0.
Thus, for each
i=1,\dots,n
,
u_{i}\in\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}
, and so
v\in\text{cl}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}}(G)
. Hence,
\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})=\bigcup_{u\in{\mathcal{O}}}\mathcal{A}_{T}(u)\subseteq\text{cl}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}}(G)
, which is a.s. finite since
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}
is a.s. finite, and
G
is locally finite.

Proof of Claim 2.
Fix
u\in{\mathcal{O}}
and
v\in\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})
. Because
G
is finitely dissociable, there must exist
\Delta>0
such that
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})

\Delta
-dissociates. If
\Delta\geq T
, the result follows from Claim 1. If
\Delta\in(0,T)
, then Claim 1 implies that a.s.,

|\mathcal{A}_{\Delta}({\mathcal{O}})|<\infty\quad\mbox{ for every }{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}.

(5.17)

To complete the proof, for every
t\in[0,T-\Delta]
, we show that if
|\mathcal{A}_{t}({\mathcal{O}})|<\infty
a.s. for every
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
, then we also have
|\mathcal{A}_{t+\Delta}({\mathcal{O}})|<\infty
a.s. for every
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
.

To this end, fix
t\in[0,T-\Delta]
, and suppose
|\mathcal{A}_{t}({\mathcal{O}})|<\infty
a.s. for all
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
. Fix
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{G}
,
u\in{\mathcal{O}}
, and
v\in\mathcal{A}_{t+\Delta}(u)
. Then there exists a
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
-causal chain
\Gamma=(v=u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{n}=u)
during the interval
[0,t+\Delta]
, with the corresponding sequence of times
0=s_{0}<s_{1}<\cdots<s_{n}\leq t+\Delta
. Let
i_{*}
be the largest integer
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
such that
s_{i}\leq\Delta
. Then
v\in\mathcal{A}_{\Delta}(u_{i_{*}})
. Furthermore, by considering the path
(u^{\prime}_{0}=u_{i_{*}},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{i}=u_{i+i_{*}},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{n-i^{*}}=u)
with times
s^{\prime}_{0}=\Delta
and
s^{\prime}_{i}=s_{i+i^{*}}
,
i=1,\ldots,n-i_{*}
, it follows that
u_{i_{*}}\rightsquigarrow_{\Delta,t+\Delta}u
. Thus,
v\in\mathcal{A}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{A}_{\Delta,t+\Delta}(u))
, which implies
\mathcal{A}_{t+\Delta}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\Delta}(U)
, where
U:=\mathcal{A}_{\Delta,t+\Delta}({\mathcal{O}})
. In view of (
5.17
), it suffices to show that
U
is a.s. finite, but this holds because by time homogeneity of Poisson processes,
U\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\mathcal{A}_{t}({\mathcal{O}})
, which is a.s. finite by assumption.

Proof of Claim 3.
For notational conciseness, let
H:=G[U]
, where we recall that
U
is a deterministic vertex set such that
B_{\ell}(G)\subseteq U
and a weak solution
X^{H,\xi_{U}}=X^{G[U],\xi_{U}}
exists. Additionally, let
H_{\ell}:=B_{\ell}(G)
, and note that
H_{\ell}=B_{\ell}(H)
. Fix
{\mathcal{O}}\in\Lambda_{H}
with
|\mathcal{A}_{T}^{H}({\mathcal{O}})|<\infty
and note that
\mathcal{A}_{T}^{H}({\mathcal{O}})=\mathcal{A}_{T}^{G}({\mathcal{O}})=\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})
on the event
\{H_{\ell}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{T}^{H}({\mathcal{O}})\}
. To prove Claim 3, we need to show that

X_{{\mathcal{O}}}^{H,\xi_{H}}[T]=X_{{\mathcal{O}}}^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}[T]\text{ a.s. on }\{\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\}.

(5.18)

Assume
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{U}(\{T\})=0
, which holds a.s. because
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{U}
is a countable collection of homogeneous Poisson processes. We prove this claim in a recursive fashion by iterating over events in the driving noise
\mathbf{N}^{G}
, and relating them to the dynamics of the SDE (
3.4
). For each
v\in U
, let
\{t_{i}^{v},i\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
be an enumeration of the (a.s. finite) set
{\mathcal{E}}_{v,T}
from (
5.14
) of events of
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v}
in
[0,T]
, arranged in increasing order. Below, we use the conventions that
\max\emptyset=0
,
\inf\emptyset=\infty
, and
[0)=\{0\}
. Define
U_{0}:={\mathcal{O}}
,
\tau_{0}:=T
, choose an arbitrary vertex
v_{0}\in{\mathcal{O}}
and for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, recursively define

\displaystyle\tau_{k}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\max\{t_{i}^{v}:v\in U_{k-1},t_{i}^{v}<\tau_{k-1}\}&\text{ if }\tau_{k-1}>0,\\
0&\text{ if }\tau_{k-1}=0,\end{cases}

(5.19)

\displaystyle v_{k}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}v\in U_{k-1}\text{ s.t. }\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v}(\{\tau_{k}\})=1&\text{ if }\tau_{k}>0,\\
v_{k-1}&\text{ if }\tau_{k}=0,\end{cases}

(5.20)

\displaystyle U_{k}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}U_{k-1}\cup\text{cl}_{v_{k}}&\text{ if }\tau_{k}>0,\\
U_{k-1}&\text{ if }\tau_{k}=0,\end{cases}

(5.21)

where
v_{k}
in (
5.20
) is a.s. well defined because the sets
{\mathcal{E}}_{v,T}
,
v\in U_{k-1}
, are a.s. distinct. Also, set

K:=\inf\{k\in{\mathbb{N}}:\tau_{k}=0\}.

(5.22)

Note that the above construction is well defined even if
K=\infty
, and furthermore, the sequence
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is strictly decreasing and the sequence
\{U_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is non-decreasing but with possible repetitions (for instance, if
v_{k}
lies in the interior of
U_{k-1}
).

As shown below, the set
U_{K}
is specifically constructed so that
U_{K}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})
and so that
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]
on the event
\{V_{H_{\ell}}\supseteq U_{K}\}
, which immediately implies (
5.18
) and hence, Claim 3. In fact, we will show that the recursive construction (
5.19
)-(
5.21
) is such that the following two claims are true.

Claim 3A:
For every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
U_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})
.

Claim 3B:
For every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,

X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{k}}[\tau_{k})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{k}}[\tau_{k})\Rightarrow X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]\mbox{ a.s. on }\{U_{k}\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\}.

(5.23)

We first show how Claim 3 follows from the auxiliary claims. Since
\{\tau^{v}_{i}\}
are events of
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v}
, (
5.19
)-(
5.21
) imply that the set
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\setminus\{0\}
is contained in the events of
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{\cup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}U_{k}}
. In view of Claim 3A, on the event
\{\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\}
, the set of events of
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{H_{\ell}}
contains that of
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{\cup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}U_{k}}
and hence contains
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\setminus\{0\}
. By the definition (
5.22
) of
K
, since
H_{\ell}
is finite, this implies
K\leq 1+\sum_{v\in V_{H_{\ell}}}\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v}(0,T]<\infty
a.s.. Also, by (
5.22
), (
5.19
), and (
5.21
) this implies that a.s. on the event
\{\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\}
,
\tau_{K}=0
,
U_{K}=\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}U_{k}
, and (applying Claim 3A with
k=K
)
U_{K}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\subseteq U
. Together, these properties imply,
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{K}}[\tau_{K})=\xi_{U_{K}}=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{K}}[\tau_{K})
. Invoking Claim 3B with
k=K
, we see that a.s. on
\{U_{K}\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\}\supseteq\{\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\}
, we have
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]
. This proves (
5.18
) and hence, Claim 3 follows.

We first provide a rough idea of the proof of the auxiliary claims. When
k=0
,
U_{k}={\mathcal{O}}
and it is easy to see that claims 3A and 3B hold trivially (due to the stipulation that
{\mathcal{O}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})
and the assumption that there is no jump at
T
). If there is any jump in the driving processes
\mathbf{N}_{{\mathcal{O}}}^{G,T}
in
[0,T)
, then by the construction (
5.19
)-(
5.21
), the most recent event before
\tau_{0}=T
that could have influenced the value of the process
X^{H,\xi_{H}}
at
\tau_{0}
occurs at
\tau_{1}
corresponding to a transition at the vertex
v_{1}
. The local nature of the dynamics implies that this transition is influenced by the particles in
\text{cl}_{v_{1}}
so that the trajectory of
X^{H,\xi_{H}}
up to time
\tau_{1}
is influenced by the trajectories of the particles in
{\mathcal{O}}\cup\text{cl}_{v_{1}}=U_{0}\cup\text{cl}_{v_{1}}=U_{1}
before time
\tau_{1}
. Any vertex
u
in
