U_{1}\setminus{\mathcal{O}}
belongs to
{\mathcal{N}}_{v_{1}}
and so
(u,v_{1})
forms a
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
causal chain on the interval
[\tau_{1},T]
, showing that
U_{1}\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})
. Claims 3A and 3B follow by proceeding inductively in this manner, tracing backward in time the
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
-causal chains that end in
{\mathcal{O}}
. We now provide fully rigorous proofs of the auxiliary claims.

Proof of Claim 3A:
We prove the following assertion using induction: For any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
v\in U_{k}
,
v\rightsquigarrow_{\tau_{k+1},T}{\mathcal{O}}
. This is obviously true in the case
k=0
, in which case
U_{0}={\mathcal{O}}
and for any
v\in{\mathcal{O}}
, trivially
v\rightsquigarrow_{\tau_{1},T}v\in{\mathcal{O}}
by Definition
5.18
of a causal chain. Now suppose that for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, we have
v\rightsquigarrow_{\tau_{k},T}{\mathcal{O}}
for all
v\in U_{k-1}
. Then because
\tau_{k+1}\leq\tau_{k}
, it follows that
v\rightsquigarrow_{\tau_{k+1},T}{\mathcal{O}}
. There are now two cases to consider. In the first case,
U_{k}=U_{k-1}
in which case the conclusion
v\rightsquigarrow_{\tau_{k+1},T}{\mathcal{O}}
for all
v\in U_{k}
holds trivially. In the second case, by (
5.20
) and (
5.21
),
U_{k}\setminus U_{k-1}\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{v_{k}}
, where
v_{k}\in U_{k-1}
, and furthermore,
\tau_{k}>0
and
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v_{k}}(\{\tau_{k}\})=1
. Since
v_{k}\in U_{k-1}
, the induction assumption implies there exists a
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
-causal chain
\Gamma=(v_{k}:=u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in{\mathcal{O}})
from
v_{k}
to
{\mathcal{O}}
contained in the time interval
[\tau_{k},T]
with corresponding times
\tau_{k}:=\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}<\cdots<\theta_{n}\leq T
. Then because
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v_{k}}(\{\tau_{k}\})=1
, it follows that
(\tau_{k+1}=\theta<\theta_{0}<\cdots<\theta_{n})
is a sequence of times such that for all
i=0,\dots,n
,
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{u_{i}}(\{\theta_{i}\})=1
, and for any
w\in U_{k}\setminus U_{k-1}
,
(w,v_{k},u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
is a
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G,T})
-causal chain ending in
{\mathcal{O}}
. Thus,
w\rightsquigarrow_{\tau_{k+1},T}{\mathcal{O}}
for all
w\in U_{k}
. By induction, we may conclude that for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
v\in U_{k}
,
v\rightsquigarrow_{\tau_{k+1},T}{\mathcal{O}}
, which proves the assertion. Now, by Definition
5.18
, the assertion in turn implies
v\rightsquigarrow_{0,T}{\mathcal{O}}
. By (
5.15
), this implies that
U_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, concluding the proof of claim 3A.

Proof of Claim 3B:
We will again use an argument by induction. First, note that the base case
k=0
in (
5.23
) is true because
U_{0}={\mathcal{O}}
,
\tau_{0}=T
, and both
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}
and
X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}
a.s. do not have a jump at
T
because
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{{\mathcal{O}}}(\{T\})=0
a.s.. Now, suppose (
5.23
) holds for
k=m-1
, for some
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. To show it holds for
k=m
, we first argue that it suffices to show that

X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{m}}[\tau_{m})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{m}}[\tau_{m})\quad\Rightarrow\quad X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{m-1}}[\tau_{m-1})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{m-1}}[\tau_{m-1})\text{ a.s. on }\{U_{m}\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\}.

(5.24)

Indeed,
U_{m-1}\subseteq U_{m}
implies
U_{m-1}\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}
on the event
\{U_{m}\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\}
, and so (
5.24
) and (
5.23
), the latter with
k=m-1
, shows that (
5.23
) holds for
k=m
. Claim 3B then follows by induction.

To establish (
5.24
), assume
U_{m}\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}
and
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{m}}[\tau_{m})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{m}}[\tau_{m}).
Since
U_{m-1}\subset U_{m}
, this implies
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{m-1}}[\tau_{m})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{m-1}}[\tau_{m})
. Moreover, from (
5.19
) note that
\tau_{m}
is the largest time prior to
\tau_{m-1}
that there is an event for any of the Poisson processes in
U_{m-1}
and hence,
\sum_{v\in U_{m-1}}\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v}(\tau_{m},\tau_{m-1})=0
. The form of the SDE (
3.4
) then implies that both
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{m-1}}
and
X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{m-1}}
are constant on
(\tau_{m},\tau_{m-1})
. Thus, to establish (
5.24
), it suffices to show that
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{m-1}}(\tau_{m})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{m-1}}(\tau_{m})
a.s.. Now, by (
5.19
)-(
5.20
),
v_{m}
is the only vertex in
U_{m-1}
such that
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}_{v_{m}}(\{\tau_{m}\})=1
. Thus,

X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{m-1}\setminus\{v_{m}\}}(\tau_{m})=X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{m-1}\setminus\{v_{m}\}}(\tau_{m}-)=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{m-1}\setminus\{v_{m}\}}(\tau_{m}-)=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{m-1}\setminus\{v_{m}\}}(\tau_{m}),

(5.25)

and so it only remains to show that
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{v_{m}}(\tau_{m})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{v_{m}}(\tau_{m})
a.s.. Since for
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\text{cl}_{v_{m}}(G)\subset U_{m}
by (
5.21
), and
U_{m}\subseteq V_{H_{\ell}}\subseteq U
by assumption,
\text{cl}_{v_{m}}(H_{\ell})=\text{cl}_{v_{m}}(G)=:\text{cl}_{v_{m}}
. Then the assumption
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{U_{m}}[\tau_{m})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{U_{m}}[\tau_{m})
implies
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{\text{cl}_{v_{m}}}[\tau_{m})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{\text{cl}_{v_{m}}}[\tau_{m})
a.s. The locality and predictability (see Definition
3.1
) of the jump rates stated in the standing assumption then imply that for
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
, denoting by
(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})
the marks of
G[\text{cl}_{v_{m}}]
we have

r^{H,v_{m}}_{j}(s,X^{H,\xi_{H}})=\bar{r}^{H[\text{cl}_{v_{m}}]}_{j}(s,X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{\text{cl}_{v_{m}}},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})=r^{H_{\ell},v_{m}}_{j}(s,X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}),\quad s\in[0,\tau_{m}].

Due to the form of the SDE (
3.4
), this shows
X^{H,\xi_{H}}_{v_{m}}(\tau_{m})=X^{H_{\ell},\xi_{H_{\ell}}}_{v_{m}}(\tau_{m})
, as desired. This completes the proof of Claim 3B, and therefore of the first assertion of the proposition.

We now turn to the proof of the second assertion of the proposition. Let
\{G_{n}\}
be a sequence of deterministic finitely dissociable
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-graphs, and for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}
be a driving noise compatible with
G_{n}
. Then define
\mathcal{A}^{n}_{t_{1},t_{2}}
analogously to
\mathcal{A}_{t_{1},t_{2}}
, but with
G
and
\mathbf{N}^{G}
replaced by
G_{n}
and
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}
, respectively, but using the same family of constants
{\bf C}
to define
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}
and
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n},T}
,
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. By the first assertion of the proposition just established above, for each
n
,
\mathcal{A}^{n}_{T}
is a localizing map of the SDE (
3.4
) on
(G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
. It follows (e.g., by Remark
5.3
) that
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}):=\text{cl}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}_{T}(\cdot)}(G_{n})
is likewise a localizing map of the SDE (
3.4
) on
(G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
. Note that
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
satisfies (
5.1
) as a consequence of the fact that
\{\mathcal{A}^{n}_{T}({\mathcal{O}})\subset U\}
lies in
{\mathcal{F}}_{T}^{\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}}
for every
{\mathcal{O}},U\in\Lambda_{G_{n}}
. With this choice of localizing maps
\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
, clearly property 1 of Definition
5.4
holds.

It only remains to show that property 2 of Definition
5.4
is also satisfied. Fix
n,n^{\prime},\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that there exists an isomorphism
\varphi\in I(B_{\ell}([G_{n^{\prime},*}]),B_{\ell}([G_{n,*}]))
. Define the
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable event

{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}:=\{\varphi\in I(B_{\ell}([G_{n^{\prime},*}],\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}}}),B_{\ell}([G_{n,*}],\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}))\},

and for notational conciseness, set
U:=B_{\ell-1}(G_{n})
and
U^{\prime}:=B_{\ell-1}(G_{n^{\prime}})
. Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{n}\subset V_{G_{n}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{n^{\prime}}\subset V_{G_{n^{\prime}}}
be any pair of sets such that
\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{n^{\prime}})={\mathcal{O}}_{n}
. Then it suffices to prove that on the event
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}\cap\{U\supseteq\mathcal{A}^{n}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n})\}={\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}\cap\{B_{\ell}(G_{n})\supseteq\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n};G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})\}
,
\mathcal{A}^{n}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n})=\varphi(\mathcal{A}^{n^{\prime}}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n^{\prime}}))
a.s..

For any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and any
(G_{m},\mathbf{N}^{G_{m},T})
-causal chain
\Gamma
up to time
T
ending in
{\mathcal{O}}_{m}
, we refer to
\Gamma
as a
G_{m}
-causal chain. We now claim that
\varphi
induces a bijection between
G_{n}
-causal chains and
G_{n^{\prime}}
-causal chains. By the definition of
\mathcal{A}^{n}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n})
, it follows that
\Gamma\subseteq U
on the event
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}\cap\{U\supseteq\mathcal{A}^{n}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n})\}
. Moreover, on the event
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}
,
\varphi^{-1}|_{U}\in I(([G_{n,*}[U]],\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}_{U}),([G_{n^{\prime},*}[U^{\prime}]],\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}}}_{U^{\prime}}))
so
\varphi^{-1}(\Gamma)
is a
G_{n^{\prime}}
-causal chain (noting that on this event,
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}},T}_{U^{\prime}}=(\mathbf{N}^{G_{n},T}_{\varphi(v)})_{v\in U^{\prime}}
as a consequence of the fact that
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}},T}
and
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n},T}
are constructed using the same family of constants
\mathbf{C}
). In the other direction, suppose
\Gamma^{\prime}:=(v^{\prime}=u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},\dots,u^{\prime}_{m}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{n^{\prime}})
is a
G_{n^{\prime}}
-causal chain. If
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq U^{\prime}
, then by the same argument,
\varphi(\Gamma^{\prime})
is a
G_{n}
-causal chain. On the other hand, suppose there exists
k\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
such that
u^{\prime}_{k}\notin U^{\prime}
. Note that
u^{\prime}_{m}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{n^{\prime}}\subseteq U^{\prime}
, so
k<m
. Choose the maximal
k
such that
u^{\prime}_{k}\notin U^{\prime}
and note that then
\{u^{\prime}_{k+1},\dots,u^{\prime}_{m}\}\subseteq U^{\prime}
. It immediately follows that
\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}:=(u^{\prime}_{k},\dots,u^{\prime}_{m})
is also a
G_{n^{\prime}}
-causal chain. Furthermore,
u^{\prime}_{k}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{U^{\prime}}(G_{n^{\prime}})
, so on the event
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}
,
\varphi(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})
is a
G_{n}
-causal chain and
\varphi(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})\nsubseteq U
, which contradicts our assumption that
\mathcal{A}^{n}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n})\subseteq U
. Thus, for any
G_{n^{\prime}}
-causal chain
\Gamma^{\prime}
ending in
{\mathcal{O}}_{n^{\prime}}
,
\varphi(\Gamma^{\prime})
is a
G_{n}
-causal chain ending in
{\mathcal{O}}_{n}
. This proves the claim.

Because
\varphi
a.s. induces a bijection between causal chains in
G_{n}
and
G_{n^{\prime}}
that end in
{\mathcal{O}}_{n}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{n^{\prime}}
respectively, it follows that
\mathcal{A}_{T}^{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{n})=\varphi(\mathcal{A}_{T}^{n^{\prime}}({\mathcal{O}}_{n^{\prime}}))
on the event
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}\cap\{\mathcal{A}_{T}^{n}({\mathcal{O}}_{n})\subseteq U\}
. Equivalently,
\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n};G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})=\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n^{\prime}};G_{n^{\prime}},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}}}))
on the event
\{\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}}_{n};G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})\subseteq B_{\ell}(G_{n})\}\cap{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}
. It follows that property 2 of Definition
5.4
also holds for the sequence
\{G_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.

This proves the second assertion and, hence, concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

6
Local Convergence of the IPS

The main goal of this section is to prove the local convergence result of Theorem
4.7
. First, consider the case when the initial data
\langle(G_{n},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n},\mathbf{\kappa}^{n},\xi^{n})\rangle,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\langle(G,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},\xi)\rangle
are finite and have deterministic and equal unmarked representatives, and thus differ only in their vertex and edge marks and initial states. Suppose also that the corresponding SDEs in (
3.4
) are coupled so that they are driven by the same Poisson processes for different
n
. Then Theorem
4.7
follows from pathwise continuity of the dynamics of the SDE (
3.4
) with respect to the vertex and edge marks of the initial data, which is essentially a consequence of the continuity condition in Assumption
2
. In the more general case when the unmarked graphs need not coincide but each IPS in the sequence and the limit IPS, all still have finite initial data, the proof entails carefully constructed couplings involving graph isomorphism (see Assumption
′
‣
6.2
and Lemma
6.9
below). In the fully general setting, when the limit graph could be infinite, the proof proceeds by first using consistent spatial localization to reduce the analysis to the finite initial data setting and then invoking the finite initial data results. While the localizing maps associated with consistent spatial localization (see Definition
5.4
) only make sense on graphs rather than their equivalence classes, local convergence results are defined in terms of equivalence classes. To bridge this gap, we need to carefully select suitable representatives of equivalence classes, as well as establish correspondences between statements about the convergence of equivalence classes and statements about representative graphs. This necessitates some technicalities related to representative graphs and associated driving noises, which we introduce in Section
6.1
. But it is not hard to see from our arguments that if one specializes to the case of convergent graphs whose vertex sets are subsets of a fixed countable set (see the space
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
defined in (
6.1
) below), then we can do away with many of these technicalities.

Section
6.1
defines canonical representatives with a view to constructing suitable couplings. These are then used in Section
6.2
to establish a more general almost sure local convergence result in Proposition
6.11
. The latter result is used in Section
6.3
to prove the local convergence result (Theorem
4.7
), and also in Section
7
in the proof of the hydrodynamic limit (Theorems
4.11
and
4.12
).

6.1
Canonical Representative Graphs and Consistent Extensions

Let
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}
be Polish spaces. We begin by defining a space of
[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-graphs:

\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]=\{[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]\text{-graphs }G:=(V,E,o,\overline{\vartheta},\vartheta)\text{ s.t. }V\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}\}.

(6.1)

Properties of this space are elucidated in Appendix
B
. In particular, it follows from Lemmas
B.5
,
B.6
, and
B.7
that
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
can be equipped with a Polish topology that is compatible with the topology of
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
, and that for any
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-random element
\langle G\rangle
, there exists a
\sigma(\langle G\rangle)
-measurable
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-random element
G
such that
G\in\langle G\rangle
almost surely. In the latter case,
G
is referred to as a
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-random representative of
\langle G\rangle
, and thus,
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
can be viewed as a canonical space of measurable representative graphs compatible with the local topology. We begin with the definition of a representative convergent sequence.

Definition 6.1

(Representative convergent sequences)
.

Let
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle
be a random sequence converging a.s. to
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
on some complete probability space. Then a
representative convergent sequence
(henceforth abbreviated to
rep-con sequence
) of
(\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
is a
\sigma(\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
-measurable tuple
\left(\{(G_{n},\xi^{n}),M_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G,\xi),\{\varphi_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}}\right)
, defined on the same probability space, that satisfies the following properties:

1.

for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(G_{n},\xi^{n})=(V_{n},E_{n},o_{n},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n},\mathbf{\kappa}^{n},\xi^{n})
is a
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random representative of
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle
;

2.

(G,\xi)=(V,E,o,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},\xi)
is a
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random representative of
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
;

3.

\lim_{n\to\infty}M_{n}=\infty
a.s. and
B_{m}([G_{*}],\xi)\cong B_{m}([G_{n,*}],\xi^{n})
on the event
\{m\leq M_{n}\}
;

4.

for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq m^{\prime}\leq M_{n}
,
\varphi_{n,m}
lies in
I(B_{m}([G_{*}],\xi),B_{m}([G_{n,*}],\xi^{n}))
, and
\varphi_{n,m^{\prime}}|_{B_{m}(G)}=\varphi_{n,m}
;

5.

for every
n^{\prime},m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
m\leq M_{n^{\prime}}
and
e\in E_{B_{m}(G)}
,
\lim_{n\to\infty,n>n^{\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{\varphi_{n,m}(e)}=\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e}
;

6.

for every
n^{\prime},m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
m\leq M_{n^{\prime}}
and
v\in B_{m}(G)
,
\lim_{n\to\infty,n>n^{\prime}}\mathbf{\kappa}^{n}_{\varphi_{n,m}(v)}=\mathbf{\kappa}_{v}
.

In Definition
6.1
,
M_{n}
represents the size of a random neighborhood of the root for which the unmarked versions of
G_{n}
and
G
are isomorphic (in the notation of Definition
2.1
of local convergence,
M_{n}
is any random integer bounded from above by
\sup\{m:n\geq n_{m}\}
). Likewise
\varphi_{n,m}
is a random isomorphism of the
m
-neighborhoods of the roots of
G_{n}
and
G
(analogous to the deterministic isomorphisms
\varphi_{n,m}
introduced in Definition
2.1
), and property 4 imposes a consistency property on the sequence of random isomorphisms.

The next lemma shows that there is a canonical way to identify rep-con sequences. A constructive proof of the lemma, which leverages the existence of suitably measurable representative graph sequences, isomorphisms, and driving maps, is deferred to Appendix
B.2
.

Lemma 6.2

(Existence of rep-con sequences)
.

Fix a complete probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
that supports the random sequence
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle
converging a.s. to
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
. Then there exists an
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable sequence of
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random elements
\{(\overline{G}_{n},\overline{\xi}^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(\overline{G},\overline{\xi})
satisfying Properties 1 and 2 of Definition
6.1
. In addition, given any sequence
\{(G_{n},\xi^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G,\xi)
satisfying Properties 1 and 2 of Definition
6.1
, the probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
also supports a rep-con sequence
\left(\{(G_{n},\xi^{n}),M_{n}\},(G,\xi),\{\varphi_{n,m}\}\right)
.

To extend the notion of rep-con sequences from initial data consisting of
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random elements to the corresponding IPS characterized by
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]
-random elements, we will need a common probability space on which we can define both the driving noise and a rep-con sequence. As mentioned in Remark
3.16
, this driving noise is a key element of the coupling we later construct to prove local convergence of the IPS.

Definition 6.3

(Consistent representative convergent extensions)
.

Given a complete probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
that supports a sequence
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle
converging a.s. to
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
, a
consistent representative convergent extension
(henceforth abbreviated to consistent rep-con extension) of
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
is a 4-tuple
((\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}}),\{(G_{n},\xi^{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}),M_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
(G,\xi,\mathbf{N}^{G}),\{\varphi_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}})
such that

1.

(\{(G_{n},\xi^{n}),M_{n}\},(G,\xi),\{\varphi_{n,m}\})
is a rep-con sequence of
(\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
;

2.

(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
is a complete extension of the probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
such that
{\mathbb{F}}
satisfies the usual conditions and
(G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
, respectively, are
{\mathbb{F}}
-driving noises that are compatible with
G_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
G
, as defined in Definition
3.7
;

3.

for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
v\in B_{M_{n}}(G)
,
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}_{\varphi_{n,M_{n}}(v)}=\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}
a.s..

The construction of consistent rep-con extensions is facilitated by the use of so-called
driving maps
defined below. Let
{\mathscr{W}}
be the space of maps from subspaces
W\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
to
{\mathbb{N}}
, which can be equipped with a Polish topology by Remark
B.8
.

Definition 6.4

(Driving maps)
.

Given a
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random element
(G,\xi):=(V,E,o,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},\xi)
, a
driving map
is a random injective map
\psi:V\to{\mathbb{N}}
, that is,
\psi
is a
\sigma(G,\xi)
-measurable random element taking values in
{\mathscr{W}}
. Suppose
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
is a filtered probability space supporting a collection of i.i.d.
{\mathbb{F}}
-Poisson processes
\{\mathbf{N}_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\times{\mathcal{J}}
with intensity
{\rm Leb}^{2}\otimes\varsigma
. Suppose also that
{\mathbb{F}}
satisfies the usual conditions and
(G,\xi)
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable. Then the
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathbb{N}}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\times{\mathcal{J}})]
-random graph
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
defined by
\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}=\mathbf{N}_{\psi(v)}
is said to be an
{\mathbb{F}}
-driving noise generated by
\psi
.

Remark 6.5

(Driving maps generate driving noise)
.

We now justify our reference to
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
as a
{\mathbb{F}}
-driving noise in Definition
6.4
. It is easy to see that on any complete, filtered probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
supporting the
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable initial data
(G,\xi)
and
{\mathbb{F}}
-Poisson processes
\{\mathbf{N}_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, the
\sigma(G,\xi)
-measurability (and thus
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurability) of driving maps ensures that for any
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable
v\in V_{G}
,
\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}
is
{\mathbb{F}}
-adapted and therefore supported on
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
, and condition 3 of Definition
3.7
is satisfied. Furthermore, conditioned on
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
,
\mathbf{N}^{G}
is a collection of i.i.d. Poisson processes indexed by
V_{G}
. Thus, condition 1 of Definition
3.7
is satisfied. Lastly, because
\{\mathbf{N}_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are
{\mathbb{F}}
-Poisson processes, for any
t>0
and
A\in{\mathcal{B}}((t,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{J}})
,
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G}(A))
is conditionally independent of
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
given
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
so condition 2 of Definition
3.7
is satisfied. Assuming
{\mathbb{F}}
satisfies the usual conditions, all three conditions of Definition
3.7
are satisfied.

The following lemma shows that there always exists a consistent rep-con extension of any convergent sequence of random elements in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
. We prove this at the end of Appendix
B
.

Lemma 6.6

(Existence of consistent rep-con extensions)
.

Let
\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a random sequence on
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
that converges a.s. to
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
. Then there exists a consistent rep-con extension
((\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}}),\{(G_{n},\xi^{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}),M_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G,\xi,\mathbf{N}^{G}),\{\varphi_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}})
of
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
such that
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
supports a collection of i.i.d.
{\mathbb{F}}
-Poisson processes
\mathbf{N}:=\{\mathbf{N}_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and driving maps
\psi_{n},

n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\psi
that generate the respective
{\mathbb{F}}
-driving maps
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\mathbf{N}^{G}
.

Consistent rep-con extensions are useful because, as shown in Proposition
6.11
of the next section, under the conditions of Theorem
4.7
, a sequence of IPS will converge almost surely if it is generated by a consistent rep-con extension in the following sense.

Definition 6.7

(IPS sequences generated by consistent rep-con sequences)
.

A sequence of IPS
\{(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G,X^{G,\xi})
is said to be generated by a consistent rep-con extension
((\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}}),\{(G_{n},\xi^{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}),M_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G,\xi,\mathbf{N}^{G}),\{\varphi_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}})
if for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(G,X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})
is an
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}
-strong solution to (
3.4
) for
(G_{n},\xi^{n})
and, likewise,
(G,X^{G,\xi})
is an
\mathbf{N}^{G}
-strong solution to (
3.4
) for
(G,\xi)
.

Remark 6.8

(Reduction from random to deterministic initial data)
.

If
\{(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
(G,X^{G,\xi})
, are IPS generated by a consistent rep-con extension of
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
, then for almost every
\omega\in\Omega
,
\mathcal{L}(\{(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},

(G,X^{G,\xi})|{\mathcal{F}}_{0})(\omega)
describes the law of a sequence of IPS generated by a consistent rep-con extension of a tuple that contains the terms
\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle(\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle(\omega)
. A rigorous justification of this would follow along the same lines as Lemma
3.12
and is thus omitted.

6.2
Proof of Local Convergence in the almost sure setting

As outlined at the beginning of Section
6
, we start by proving convergence for sequences and limits with finite initial data (or, to be more precise, initial data whose underlying graphs have a uniformly bounded radius).

Lemma 6.9

(The convergence of IPS on graphs of uniformly bounded size)
.

Suppose the family of local jump rates
\mathbf{\bar{r}}
satisfies Assumption
1
, and
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
satisfies Assumption
2
. If
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\to\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
a.s., then the tuple
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
satisfies the following:

Finite Convergence Property:
given any consistent rep-con extension
((\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}}),\{(G_{n},\xi^{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}),M_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G,\xi,\mathbf{N}^{G}),\{\varphi_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}})
of
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
,
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists an a.s. finite,
{\mathcal{F}}_{T}
-measurable random variable
\overline{N}_{m}:=\overline{N}_{m,T}
such that for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\left(B_{m}([G_{n,*}]),X^{m,n}[T]\right)\cong\left(B_{m}([G_{*}]),X^{m,\infty}[T]\right)\text{ a.s. on the event }\{n\geq\overline{N}_{m}\}\cap\{m\leq M_{n}\},

(6.2)

where
X^{m,n}
and
X^{m,\infty}
are the respective
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}_{B_{m}(G_{n})}
- and
\mathbf{N}^{G}_{B_{m}(G)}
-strong solutions to (
3.4
) for the initial data
B_{m}(G_{n},\xi^{n})
and
B_{m}(G,\xi)
.

We defer the proof of Lemma
6.9
to the end of the section, and instead first establish local convergence for IPS on graphs with general (possibly infinite) initial data given the conclusion of Lemma
6.9
. To do so, the only consequence of Assumption
2
that we need is the finite convergence property. With that in mind, we encode the latter property in the following weaker assumption.

Assumption
2
′

(Generalization of Assumption
2
)
.

The tuple
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},

\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
is such that the finite convergence property of Lemma
6.9
holds.

Remark 6.10

(Reduction from random to deterministic initial data)
.

Given Assumption
′
‣
5.1
, as a consequence of Remark
6.8
, if Assumption
′
‣
6.2
holds for a tuple
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
then it holds for a.s. every realization of the isomorphism classes
\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
when they are random.

We now state our general almost sure local convergence result, which holds under the weaker conditions of Assumptions
′
‣
5.1
and
′
‣
6.2
(which are implied by Assumptions
1
and
2
, respectively), so as to make it applicable in situations where the latter may fail but the former still hold.

Proposition 6.11

(Almost sure local convergence)
.

Suppose that the local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
satisfy Assumption
′
‣
5.1
, the tuple
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
satisfies Assumption
′
‣
6.2
and
\{\langle G_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle G\rangle
a.s. consistently spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
) with local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
. Also, let
\{(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G,X^{G,\xi})
be a collection of IPS generated by a consistent rep-con extension of the tuple in the sense of Definiton
6.7
. If
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\to\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
a.s. in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
, then
\langle(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\to\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle
a.s. in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}:={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
and let
\langle(G_{\infty},\xi^{\infty})\rangle:=\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
, and
(G_{\infty},X^{G_{\infty},\xi^{\infty}}):=(G,X^{G,\xi})
respectively. We first prove the proposition under the additional assumption that
\{(G_{n},\xi^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}}
are deterministic. In this case, let
((\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}}),\{(G_{n},\xi^{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}),M_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G,\xi,\mathbf{N}^{G}),\{\varphi_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}})
be a consistent rep-con extension of
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}})
, where
(G_{\infty},\xi^{\infty}):=(G,\xi)
. In addition, let
\mathbf{N}^{G_{\infty}}:=\mathbf{N}^{G}
. Due to Assumption
′
‣
5.1
and the assumption of consistent spatial localization, there exists a consistent sequence of localizing maps
\{\mathcal{S}_{T}(\cdot;G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}}
for the SDE (
3.4
) on
\{(G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}}
, and by Proposition
5.7
, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}
, the SDE (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for
(G_{n},\xi^{n})
. Hence, the
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}
-strong solution
X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}
to (
3.4
) for the initial data
(G_{n},\xi^{n})
is well defined.

Fix
n^{\prime},m,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
m\leq M\leq M_{n^{\prime}}
. By property 3 of Definition
6.3
, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
m\leq M_{n}
, and
v\in B_{m}(G)
,
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}_{\varphi_{n,m}(v)}=\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}
. We now apply property 2 of the consistent spatial localization property in Definition
5.4
, with
{\mathcal{O}}=B_{m}(G)
,
\ell=M
,
n=\infty
,
n^{\prime}=n^{\prime}
,
\varphi=\varphi_{n^{\prime},M}^{-1}
. Then, noting that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\varphi}=\{M\leq M_{n^{\prime}}\}=\Omega
, we see that

\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})=\varphi(\mathcal{S}_{T}(\varphi^{-1}({\mathcal{O}});G_{n^{\prime}},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}}})),

which implies that

\mathcal{S}_{T}(\varphi_{n^{\prime},M}({\mathcal{O}});G_{n^{\prime}},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n^{\prime}}})=\varphi_{n^{\prime},M}(\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G}))

a.s. on the event
\{\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})\subseteq B_{M}(G)\}
. Because
\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
is a.s. finite, there must exist an
{\mathcal{F}}_{T}
-measurable a.s. finite random variable
\overline{M}_{m}
such that
\mathcal{S}_{T}({\mathcal{O}};G,\mathbf{N}^{G})\subseteq B_{\overline{M}_{m}}(G)
a.s.. Furthermore, by (
5.3
) this implies that

\displaystyle X^{G,\xi}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]

\displaystyle=X^{B_{M}(G),\xi_{B_{M}(G)}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]\quad\text{and}\quad X^{G_{n^{\prime}},\xi^{n^{\prime}}}_{\varphi_{n^{\prime},M}({\mathcal{O}})}[T]=X^{B_{M}(G_{n^{\prime}}),\xi^{n^{\prime}}_{B_{M}(G_{n^{\prime}})}}_{\varphi_{n^{\prime},M}({\mathcal{O}})}[T]

a.s. on the event
\{M\geq\overline{M}_{m}\}
. By (
6.2
) of Assumption
′
‣
6.2
, there exists an a.s. finite,
{\mathcal{F}}_{T}
-measurable random variable
N_{M}
such that

X^{B_{M}(G),\xi_{B_{M}(G)}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]=X^{B_{M}(G_{n^{\prime}}),\xi^{n^{\prime}}_{B_{M}(G_{n^{\prime}})}}_{\varphi_{n^{\prime},M}({\mathcal{O}})}[T]\text{ a.s. on the event }\{n^{\prime}\geq N_{M}\}.

The last two displays together show that a.s. on the event
\{M\geq\overline{M}_{m}\}\cap\{n^{\prime}\geq N_{M}\}
,

\displaystyle X^{G,\xi}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]

\displaystyle=X^{B_{M}(G),\xi_{B_{M}(G)}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]=X^{B_{M}(G_{n^{\prime}}),\xi^{n^{\prime}}_{B_{M}(G_{n^{\prime}})}}_{\varphi_{n^{\prime},M}({\mathcal{O}})}[T]=X^{G_{n^{\prime}},\xi^{n^{\prime}}}_{\varphi_{n^{\prime},M}({\mathcal{O}})}[T].

Applying the last display for each
M
satisfying
m\leq M\leq M_{n^{\prime}}
and noting by property 1 of Definition
5.1
that
\overline{M}_{m}\geq m
, it follows that

X^{G,\xi}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]=X^{G_{n^{\prime}},\xi^{n^{\prime}}}_{\varphi_{n^{\prime},\overline{M}_{m}}({\mathcal{O}})}[T]\text{ a.s. on the event }\{\overline{M}_{m}\leq M_{n^{\prime}}\}\cap\{n^{\prime}\geq N_{\overline{M}_{m}}\}.

Sending
n^{\prime}\to\infty
and noting that then
M_{n^{\prime}}\rightarrow\infty
by property 3 of Definition
6.1
, and
\overline{M}_{m}
is a.s. finite, it follows that

\lim_{n^{\prime}\to\infty}X^{G_{n^{\prime}},\xi^{n^{\prime}}}_{\varphi_{n^{\prime},\overline{M}_{m}}({\mathcal{O}})}[T]=X^{G,\xi}_{{\mathcal{O}}}[T]\text{ a.s.. }

This concludes the proof for deterministic sequences.

The random case can be obtained by conditioning on the initial data. More precisely, suppose
\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
is random and let
\{(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
(G,X^{G,\xi})
be the sequence of IPS generated by a consistent rep-con extension of the tuple
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
. Then by Remark
6.8
, for almost every
\omega\in\Omega
,
\mathcal{L}(\{(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\},(G,X^{G,\xi})|{\mathcal{F}}_{0})(\omega)
describes the law of a sequence of IPS generated by a consistent rep-con extension of a tuple that contains the terms
\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle(\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle(\omega)
. Moreover, by Remark
6.10
, Assumption
′
‣
6.2
holds a.s. conditioned on
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
. Therefore, by the proof of the proposition for deterministic initial data,
{\mathbb{P}}(\langle(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\to\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle|{\mathcal{F}}_{0})=1
a.s.. Therefore,
\langle(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\to\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle
a.s., as desired.
∎

We finish the section with a proof of Lemma
6.9
.

Proof of Lemma
6.9
:

Fix a consistent rep-con extension of the given tuple, write
G=(V,E,o,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})
,
G_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n},o_{n},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n},\mathbf{\kappa}^{n})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and set
M:=\max_{v\in V}d_{G}(v,o)
. We first consider the case when
\{G_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
G
are a.s. finite graphs and additionally assume that
\max_{v\in V_{n}}d_{G_{n}}(v,o_{n})\leq M
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and show that for any
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, there exists an a.s. finite,
{\mathcal{F}}_{T}
-measurable random variable
\overline{N}:=\overline{N}_{M,T}
such that,

\left([G_{n,*}],X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}[T]\right)\cong\left([G_{*}],X^{G,\xi}[T]\right)\text{ a.s. on the event }\{n\geq\overline{N}\}\cap\{M\leq M_{n}\}.

(6.3)

Note that on the event
A_{n}:=\{M\leq M_{n}\}
,
([G_{n,*}],\xi^{n})\cong([G_{*}],\xi^{n})
. To show (
6.3
), fix
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
\varphi_{n}:V\to V_{n}
be the
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable map given by
\varphi_{n}:=\varphi_{n,M}
on the event
A_{n}
(on
A_{n}^{c}
, we may define
\varphi_{n}
to be any measurable function with the appropriate domain and range, for instance, the function that maps all vertices of
G
to the root of
G_{n}
). For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
v\in V_{G}
, recall from property 3 of Definition
6.3
that
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}_{\varphi_{n}(v)}=\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}
on the event
A_{n}
. In terms of the family of constants
\{C_{k,T}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}},T>0}
from Assumption
1
, define

\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}=\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}_{T}:=\left\{(v,t,r,j)\in V\times[0,T]\times(0,C_{|\text{cl}_{v}(G)|,T}]\times{\mathcal{J}}:\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}\left(\{(t,r,j)\}\right)=1\right\}.

Since
|\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}|<\infty
a.s., we can a.s. order the elements
\{(v_{k},\tau_{k},r_{k},j_{k})\}_{k=1}^{|\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}|}
of
\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}
such that
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is strictly increasing. Note that
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is the sequence of points in a time-homogeneous Poisson process and, therefore, a sequence of absolutely continuous random variables. Let
R:=\inf\left\{|r_{k}-r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi})|:k=1,\dots,|\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}|\right\}
. By the predictability of the jump rates
r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}
, the random variables
r_{k}
and
r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi})
are independent and therefore
r_{k}\neq r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi})
a.s. which implies that
R>0
a.s.. On the event
A_{n}
, let

R_{n}:=\sup\left\{\left|r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi})-r^{G_{n},\varphi_{n}(v_{k})}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi}_{\varphi^{-1}_{n}(V_{n})})\right|:k=1,\dots,|\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}|\right\},

where
X^{G,\xi}_{\varphi^{-1}_{n}(V_{n})}=(X^{G,\xi}_{\varphi^{-1}_{n}(v)})_{v\in V_{n}}
. By property 3 of Definition
6.1
,
M_{n}
a.s. diverges to infinity, so
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a sequence of events such that
{\mathbb{P}}(\cap_{n^{\prime}\geq n}A_{n^{\prime}})\to 1
so that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{A_{n}\right\}}=1
a.s.. By Properties 5 and 6 of Definition
6.1
, it follows that for each
v\in V
and
e\in E
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{I}_{A_{n}}(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(e)},\mathbf{\kappa}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(v)})=(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e},\mathbf{\kappa}_{v})\quad\text{a.s..}

(6.4)

Let
\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(E)}=(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(e)})_{e\in E}
and
\mathbf{\kappa}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(V)}=(\mathbf{\kappa}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(v)})_{v\in V}
. Then using the fact that the local jump rates are class functions (specifically, applying Remark
3.2
with
G_{1}=(V,E,o,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(E)},\mathbf{\kappa}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(V)})
,
G_{2}=G_{n}
, and
\varphi=\varphi_{n}^{-1}
) in the first equality below, and combining (
6.4
) with the absolute continuity of
\tau_{k}
and the fact that
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
and
{\bf\bar{r}}
satisfy Assumption
2
, and the jump rates satisfy (
3.1
) we have for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}r^{G_{n},\varphi_{n}(v_{k})}_{j_{k}}\left(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi}_{\varphi_{n}^{-1}(V_{n})}\right)\mathbb{I}_{A_{n}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}r^{(V,E,o,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(E)},\mathbf{\kappa}^{n}_{\varphi_{n}(V)}),v_{k}}_{j_{k}}\left(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi}\right)\mathbb{I}_{A_{n}}

\displaystyle=r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}\left(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi}\right)

a.s. on the event
\{k\leq|\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}|\}
. Because
|\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}|
is a.s. finite, it follows that
\lim_{n\to\infty}R_{n}=0
a.s.. Moreover, the fact that
R>0
a.s. implies the existence of an a.s. finite random variable
\overline{N}
such that,

R_{n}<\frac{R}{2}\text{ on the event }\{n\geq\overline{N}\}\cap A_{n}.

(6.5)

We now argue that
X^{G,\xi}[T]=X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}[T]
on the event
\{n\geq\overline{N}\}\cap A_{n}
by making use of the fact that for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
v\in V
,
X^{G,\xi}_{v}
and
X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}_{\varphi_{n}(v)}
are driven by the same Poisson processes on the event
A_{n}
. Fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and note that by the SDE (
3.4
),
X^{G,\xi}
and
X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}
are both a.s. continuous on the random set
\{t\in[0,\infty)\setminus\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\}\cap A_{n}
. Furthermore, at time
\tau_{k}
, the processes
X^{G,\xi}
and
X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}
may either remain constant or experience a jump of size
j_{k}
at the respective vertices
v_{k}
and
\varphi_{n}(v_{k})
. It follows from the SDE (
3.4
) that the processes will either simultaneously jump or fail to jump if and only if

\text{sgn}\left(r_{k}-r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi})\right)=\text{sgn}\left(r_{k}-r^{G_{n},\varphi_{n}(v_{k})}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\right),

where
\text{sgn}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the càdlàg function given by
\text{sgn}(a)=\mathbb{I}_{\left\{a\geq 0\right\}}-\mathbb{I}_{\left\{a<0\right\}}
. Suppose that
X^{G,\xi}[\tau_{k})=X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}_{\varphi_{n}(V)}[\tau_{k})
. Then, using the predictability of the jump rates (by the standing assumption and Definition
3.1
) in the first equality below, we have

sgn

\displaystyle\left(r_{k}-r^{G_{n},\varphi_{n}(v_{k})}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 24.0pt=\text{sgn}\left(r_{k}-r^{G_{n},\varphi_{n}(v_{k})}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi}_{\varphi^{-1}_{n}(V_{n})})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 24.0pt=\text{sgn}\left(r_{k}-r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi})+r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi})-r^{G_{n},\varphi_{n}(v_{k})}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi}_{\varphi_{n}^{-1}(V_{n})})\right).

However, the last line of the above display is a.s. equal to
\text{sgn}\left(r_{k}-r^{G,v_{k}}_{j_{k}}(\tau_{k},X^{G,\xi})\right)
on the event
\{n\geq\overline{N}\}
by (
6.5
). Thus,
X^{G,\xi}[\tau_{k}]=X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}_{\varphi_{n}(V)}[\tau_{k}]
a.s. on the event
\{n\geq\overline{N}\}\cap A_{n}
. It follows that
X^{G,\xi}[\tau_{k+1})=X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}_{\varphi_{n}(V)}[t_{k+1})
a.s. if
|\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}|>k
and
X^{G,\xi}_{V}[T]=X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}_{\varphi_{n}(V)}[T]
a.s. if
|\overline{{\mathcal{E}}}|=k
. Applying induction, we see that
X^{G,\xi}[T]=X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}_{\varphi_{n}(V)}[T]
a.s. on the event
\{n\geq\overline{N}\}\cap A_{n}
, and so (
6.3
) follows.

To see why this implies Lemma
6.9
in the general case of possibly infinite graphs, note that (
6.3
) implies that for any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and any sequence
\{\langle G_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle G\rangle
we may replace
(G_{n},\xi^{n}),n\in{\mathbb{N}}
by
B_{m}(G_{n},\xi^{n}),n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(G,\xi)
by
B_{m}(G,\xi)
,
\overline{N}_{m}
by
\overline{N}
, and
M
by
m
in (
6.3
) to get (
6.2
),
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
satisfies the finite convergence property. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

6.3
Proof of Theorem
4.7

We now show how Theorem
4.7
follows from Proposition
6.11
. The proof uses a simple argument involving the Skorokhod representation theorem and Proposition
5.17
.

Proof of Theorem
4.7
.

Set
\langle(G_{\infty},\xi^{\infty})\rangle:=\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
and
{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}:={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
. Since Assumption
1
holds with associated constants
{\bf C}
, and each
\langle G_{n}\rangle,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty},
is a.s. finitely dissociable with respect to
{\bf C}
, the conditions of Proposition
5.17
are satisfied a.s.. Hence, the collection
\{\langle G_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}}
a.s. consistently spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
). Assumption
1
, Proposition
4.2
, and Proposition
5.7
then imply that the SDE (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for all initial data in
\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}}
. Moreover, since
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\Rightarrow\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
, by the Skorokhod representation theorem there exists a (complete) probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})
that supports random elements
\langle(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})\rangle\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty},
such that
\langle(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})\rangle\to\langle(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{\xi})\rangle:=\langle(\widetilde{G}_{\infty},\widetilde{\xi}^{\infty})\rangle

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s..

By Lemma
6.6
there exists a consistent rep-con extension of
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}})
given by
((\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}}),\{(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n},\mathbf{N}^{\widetilde{G}_{n}}),\widetilde{M}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{\xi},\mathbf{N}^{\widetilde{G}}),

\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}})
. For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}
, let
(G_{n},X^{\widetilde{G_{n}},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})
be the resulting
\mathbf{N}^{\widetilde{G}_{n}}
-strong solution to (
3.4
) for
(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})
, where
(\widetilde{G}_{\infty},\widetilde{\xi}^{\infty})=(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{\xi})
and let
\langle(\widetilde{G}_{n},X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})\rangle
denote its isomorphism class. Such strong solutions are well defined by Lemma
3.15
(which establishes the existence of a pathwise unique strong solution for the initial data
\langle(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})\rangle
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}
). Because
\{\widetilde{G}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}}
spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
), by Assumption
1
and the fact that
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
(and therefore
\langle(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{\xi})\rangle
) satisfies Assumption
2
, Lemma
6.9
implies that the tuple
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
satisfies Assumption
′
‣
6.2
. Proposition
6.11
then implies

\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle(\widetilde{G}_{n},X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})\rangle=\langle(\widetilde{G}_{\infty},X^{\widetilde{G}_{\infty},\widetilde{\xi}^{\infty}})\rangle\quad\text{a.s..}

By well-posedness of (
3.4
),
\langle(\widetilde{G}_{n},X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})\rangle\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\langle(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{\infty}
. Thus,
\langle(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle

\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\langle(\widetilde{G}_{n},X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})\rangle\Rightarrow\langle(\widetilde{G},X^{\widetilde{G},\widetilde{\xi}})\rangle\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]
.
∎

7
Proof of Asymptotic Correlation Decay

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
4.10
. Recall that for an unrooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
-graph
G
and a vertex
v\in V_{G}
,
\mathscr{C}_{v}(G)
is the connected component of
G
equipped with
v
as its root. For the remainder of the section, we fix a sequence of finite, (possibly disconnected) unrooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random graphs
(G_{n},\xi^{n})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and a
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random element
\langle\mathscr{C}_{o}(G,\xi)\rangle
(henceforth, denoted just
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
), all defined on a common complete probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
. We additionally assume that, by extending the probability space if necessary,
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
also supports an i.i.d. pair of vertices
(o^{1}_{n},o^{2}_{n})
, with each vertex uniformly distributed on
G_{n}
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The proof of Theorem
4.10
is comprised of two steps. The first step is to establish an asymptotic independence property stated in Lemma
7.1
below.

Lemma 7.1

(Asymptotic independence of initial data)
.

Suppose
(G_{n},\xi^{n})
converges locally in probability to
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
. Then

(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{1}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle,\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{2}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle)\Rightarrow(\langle(G^{(1)},\xi^{(1)})\rangle,\langle(G^{(2)},\xi^{(2)})\rangle),

(7.1)

where
\Rightarrow
represents convergence in distribution in
({\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}])^{2}
and
\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle,i=1,2,
are two independent copies of
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
f_{1},f_{2}:{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]\to{\mathbb{R}}
be bounded, continuous functions. Then by
[
30
, Lemma 2.8]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{1}(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{1}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle)f_{2}(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{2}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[f_{1}(\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[f_{2}(\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[f_{1}(\langle(G^{(1)},\xi^{(1)})\rangle)f_{2}(\langle(G^{(2)},\xi^{(2)})\rangle)\right].

(7.2)

Since
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
is a metric space, the algebra of separable bounded functions on
({\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}])^{2}
strongly separates points and hence, is convergence determining
[
17
, Theorem 3.4.5(b)]
. Thus, (
7.2
) implies (
7.1
).
∎

Invoking the Skorokhod representation theorem, the second and main step of the proof assumes joint local convergence of the initial data
\{\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{1}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle,\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{2}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
to the i.i.d. pair
(\langle(G^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}\rangle,\langle(G^{(2)},\xi^{(2)})\rangle)
and proves convergence
in probability
of the corresponding pairs of strong solutions
\{\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{1}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle,\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{2}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
to
(\langle(G^{(1)},X^{G^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}})\rangle,\langle(G^{(2)},X^{G^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}})\rangle)
. The coupling proof proceeds as follows. For each
i=1,2
, we first construct a sequence of driving noises
\{(G,\mathbf{N}^{n,i})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that the isomorphism class of the corresponding
\{\mathbf{N}^{n,i}\}
-strong solution
\{\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{i}}(G_{n},X^{n,i})\rangle\}
for the initial data
\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{i}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle
converges a.s. as
n\rightarrow\infty
to
\langle(G^{(i)},X^{G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)}})\rangle
. From this, we construct a single sequence of
common
driving noises
\{(G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that for each
i=1,2,
the corresponding
\{\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
-strong solutions
\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{i}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}),

n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
(G^{(i)},X^{G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)}})
satisfy

\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\to\langle(G^{(i)},X^{G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)}})\rangle\text{ in probability.}

By the independence of
\{\langle(G^{(i)},X^{G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)}})\rangle\}_{i=1,2}
, this would imply the desired correlation decay result.

Lemma 7.2

(Asymptotic independence of IPS on independently rooted components)
.

Given
\{(G_{n},\xi^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, suppose there exists a countable set
{\mathcal{S}}
such that for
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. every
\omega\in\Omega
,
V_{G_{n}(\omega)}\subset{\mathcal{S}}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Suppose also that Assumption
′
‣
5.1
holds and that the collection of isomorphism classes
\{\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n})\rangle,\langle G^{(i)}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},i=1,2}
a.s. consistently spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
). In addition, assume that for each
i=1,2,
,

\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{i}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\to\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle

(7.3)

and the tuple
(\{\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle)
satisfies Assumption
′
‣
6.2
. Then it is possible to define a filtered probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
supporting solutions
(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}}),n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
\langle(G^{(i)},X^{G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)}})\rangle
,
i=1,2,
to the SDE (
3.4
) for the respective initial data
(G_{n},\xi^{n}),n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle
,
i=1,2,
such that as
n\to\infty
,

\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{i}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\to\langle(G^{(i)},X^{G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)}})\rangle,\text{ }i=1,2,\text{ in probability.}

(7.4)

Proof.

Fix any deterministic injection
\widehat{\psi}:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathbb{N}}
. For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})
be the unique
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random element such that
\widehat{\psi}|_{V_{G_{n}}}\in I((G_{n},\xi^{n}),(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}))
. To be precise, if for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(G_{n},\xi^{n})=(V_{n},E_{n},o_{n},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n},\mathbf{\kappa}^{n},\xi^{n})
, then

(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}):=(\widetilde{V}_{n},\widetilde{E}_{n},\widetilde{o}_{n},\widetilde{\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}}^{n},\widetilde{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})=(\widehat{\psi}(V_{n}),\widehat{\psi}(E_{n}),\widehat{\psi}(o_{n}),(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{\widehat{\psi}(e)})_{e\in\widetilde{E}_{n}},(\mathbf{\kappa}^{n}_{\widehat{\psi}(v)},\xi^{n}_{\widehat{\psi}(v)})_{v\in\widetilde{V}_{n}}).

Noting that all the remaining statements of the lemma depend only on
\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle=\langle\mathscr{C}_{\widehat{\psi}(o^{i}_{n})}(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})\rangle
, we may assume without loss of generality that
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
and therefore that
\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})
is a
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random element for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
i=1,2
.

Now, by Assumption
′
‣
5.1
and the spatial localization assumption, Proposition
5.7
implies that the SDE (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for all initial data in the collection of marked graph representatives
\{\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},\xi^{n}),(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},i=1,2}
, where for
i=1,2

(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})
is a random representative of
\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle
(whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
B.7
). Also, by assumption, the collection of
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random elements
\{\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},i=1,2}
is
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable. For each
i=1,2,
Lemma
6.6
implies the existence of an associated consistent rep-con extension
((\Omega^{i},{\mathcal{F}}^{i},{\mathbb{F}}^{i},{\mathbb{P}}^{i}),\{\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},\xi^{n},\mathbf{N}^{n,i}),M_{n}^{(i)}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)},\mathbf{N}^{G^{(i)}}),\{\varphi^{(i)}_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}})
where
(\Omega^{i},{\mathcal{F}}^{i},{\mathbb{F}}^{i},{\mathbb{P}}^{i})
supports a collection of i.i.d.
{\mathbb{F}}^{i}
-Poisson processes
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{i}:=\{\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{i}_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
which together with the driving maps
\overline{\psi}^{n,i}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
\overline{\psi}^{(i)}
generate the respective
{\mathbb{F}}^{i}
-driving noises
\mathbf{N}^{n,i}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
\mathbf{N}^{G^{(i)}}
. We may also identify
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}}):=(\Omega^{1},{\mathcal{F}}^{1},{\mathbb{F}}^{1},{\mathbb{P}}^{1})=(\Omega^{2},{\mathcal{F}}^{2},{\mathbb{F}}^{2},{\mathbb{P}}^{2})
and assume that
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{1}
is independent of
\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{2}
so that both collections consist of
{\mathbb{F}}
-Poisson processes. On this space, define for each
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
i=1,2,

\mathbf{N}_{2k-2+i}:=\overline{\mathbf{N}}^{i}_{k}
. Then for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
i=1,2,

\mathbf{N}^{n,i}
and
\mathbf{N}^{G^{(i)}}
are generated from
\{\mathbf{N}_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
by the driving maps
\psi^{n,i}(\cdot):=2\overline{\psi}^{n,i}(\cdot)-2+i
and
\psi^{(i)}(\cdot):=2\overline{\psi}^{(i)}(\cdot)-2+i
, respectively.

For each
n
, let
L_{n}:=d_{G_{n}}(o_{n}^{1},o_{n}^{2})
and let
\overline{M}_{n}
be the maximal
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable random variable such that
\overline{M}_{n}<\frac{L_{n}}{2}
and
\overline{M}_{n}\leq\min_{i=1,2}M_{n}^{(i)}
a.s.. Define the mapping
\psi_{n}:V_{n}\to{\mathbb{N}}
by

\psi^{n}(v):=\begin{cases}\psi^{n,1}(v)&\text{ on the event }\{d_{G_{n}}(v,o^{1}_{n})\leq\overline{M}_{n}\},\\
\psi^{n,2}(v)&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Since for
i=1,2
, the driving maps
\psi^{n,i}
have disjoint images and
\overline{M}_{n}<\frac{1}{2}d_{G_{n}}(o_{n}^{1},o_{n}^{2})
, it follows that
\psi^{n}
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable and injective and is therefore also a driving map. For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}
be the
{\mathbb{F}}
-driving noise generated by the driving map
\psi^{n}
. Then
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}
is compatible with
\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n}),

i=1,2
. Because
\overline{M}_{n}\leq M_{n}^{(i)}
,
i=1,2
, it follows that for any
i=1,2,

n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
v\in B_{m}(\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n}))
,

\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}_{v}=\mathbf{N}^{n,i}_{v}=\mathbf{N}^{G^{(i)}}_{(\varphi_{n,m}^{(i)})^{-1}(v)}\text{ on the event }\{m\leq\overline{M}_{n}\}.

(7.5)

Let
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be any deterministic sequence such that
L_{n_{k}}
(and therefore
\overline{M}_{n_{k}}
) diverges to infinity a.s. as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Such a sequence exists because
L_{n}\to\infty
in probability. Fix
i\in\{1,2\}
and consider the tuple
\mathbf{T}_{i}:=((\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}}),\{\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n_{k}}}(G_{n_{k}},\xi^{n_{k}},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n_{k}}}),\overline{M}_{n_{k}}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}},

(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)},\mathbf{N}^{G^{(i)}}),\{\varphi^{(i)}_{n_{k},m}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq\overline{M}_{n_{k}}})
. Using (
7.5
), the definition of
\overline{M}_{n}
and directly checking the three properties of Definition
6.3
, it is easy to see that this tuple is a consistent rep-con extension of
\mathbf{R}_{i}:=((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n_{k}}}(G_{n_{k}},\xi^{n_{k}})\rangle\},\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle)
. The verification is provided below for completeness.

Property 1:

By construction,
(\{\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},\xi^{n}),M^{(i)}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})
,
\{\varphi^{(i)}_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}})
is a rep-con sequence of
(\{\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle)
. Since
\overline{M}_{n}\leq M^{(i)}_{n}
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the tuple
\mathbf{\overline{R}}_{i}:=(\{\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n_{k}}}(G_{n_{k}},\xi^{n_{k}}),\overline{M}_{n_{k}}\},(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)}),\{\varphi^{(i)}_{n_{k},m}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq\overline{M}_{n_{k}}})
satisfies Properties 1,2 and 4-6 of Definition
6.1
. Because
\overline{M}_{n_{k}}\to\infty
a.s. and
\overline{M}_{n_{k}}\leq M^{(i)}_{n_{k}}
for each
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, it follows that Property 3 of Definition
6.1
holds. Thus,
\mathbf{\overline{R}}_{i}
is a rep-con sequence of
(\{\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n_{k}}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle)
.

Property 2:

This holds because
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
is part of the consistent rep-con extension of
\mathbf{R}_{i}
and
\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}}
is an
{\mathbb{F}}
-driving noise.

Property 3:

This holds by (
7.5
).

Given that
\mathbf{T}_{i}
is a consistent rep-con extension of
\mathbf{R}_{i}
, by Proposition
6.11
,

\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n_{k}}}(G_{n_{k}},X^{G_{n_{k}},\xi^{n_{k}}})\rangle\to\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle\text{ a.s..}

Lastly, note that for any deterministic subsequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a further deterministic subsequence
\{n_{k_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}L_{n_{k_{\ell}}}=\infty
a.s.. By the above result, this implies that

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n_{k_{\ell}}}}(G_{n_{k_{\ell}}},X^{G_{n_{k_{\ell}}},\xi^{n_{k_{\ell}}}})\rangle=\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle\text{ a.s.,}

which immediately implies (
7.4
) and completes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.3

(Asymptotic independence for more general IPS)
.

Note that the proof of Lemma
7.2
does not make direct use of the specific form of the SDE (
3.4
) but only relies on spatial localization of the SDE, Assumptions
′
‣
5.1
and
′
‣
6.2
, and Proposition
6.11
, all of which could potentially be verified for solutions
X^{G,\xi}
of more general Poisson-driven SDEs.

Applying Lemmas
7.1
and
7.2
, we now prove Theorem
4.10
.

Proof of Theorem
4.10
:

Lemma
7.1
implies that

(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{1}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle,\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{2}}(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle)\Rightarrow(\langle(G^{(1)},\xi^{(1)})\rangle,\langle(G^{(2)},\xi^{(2)})\rangle),

where
\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle,i=1,2,
are i.i.d. copies of
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
. By the Skorokhod representation theorem and Lemma
B.7
, there exist
\{\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(i)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(i)})\rangle\}_{i=1,2}\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\{\langle(G^{(i)},\xi^{(i)})\rangle\}_{i=1,2}
, and finite,
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random elements
\{(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{o}^{i}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},i=1,2}\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\{(G_{n},o^{i}_{n},\xi^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},i=1,2}
such that

\langle\mathscr{C}_{\widetilde{o}^{i}_{n}}(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})\rangle\to\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(i)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(i)})\rangle\text{, }i=1,2,\text{ a.s..}

By assumption,
\widetilde{G}_{n}\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}G_{n}
and
\widetilde{G}^{(i)}\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}G
are a.s. finitely dissociable for
i=1,2,
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. So by Assumption
1
and Proposition
5.17
,
\{(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{o}^{i}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}),(\widetilde{G}^{(i)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(i)})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},i=1,2}
consistently spatially localizes the SDE (
3.4
). Assumptions
1
and
2
imply by Lemma
6.9
that the tuple
((\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}),\{\langle\mathscr{C}_{o^{i}_{n}}(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(i)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(i)})\rangle)
satisfies Assumption
′
‣
6.2
for each
i
. Thus, by Lemma
7.2
, it is possible to construct a collection of driving noises
\{\mathbf{N}^{\widetilde{G}_{n}},\mathbf{N}^{\widetilde{G}^{(i)}}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},i=1,2}
such that for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
i=1,2,
the respective
\mathbf{N}^{\widetilde{G}_{n}}
and
\mathbf{N}^{\widetilde{G}^{(i)}}
-solutions
X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}_{n}}
and
X^{\widetilde{G}^{(i)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(i)}}
satisfy

\langle\mathscr{C}_{\widetilde{o}^{n}_{i}}(\widetilde{G}_{n},X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})\rangle\to\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(i)},X^{\widetilde{G}^{(i)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(i)}})\rangle\text{ in probability as }n\to\infty.

By the bounded convergence theorem, for any bounded, continuous function
f:({\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}])^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\left(\langle\mathscr{C}_{\widetilde{o}^{n}_{i}}(\widetilde{G}_{n},X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})\rangle\right)_{i=1,2}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(\left(\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(i)},X^{\widetilde{G}^{(i)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(i)}})\rangle\right)_{i=1,2}\right)\right].

(7.6)

By well-posedness of the SDE (
3.4
) on each of the graphs
(\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}),n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
(\widetilde{G},\widetilde{\xi})
, which holds by Theorem
4.3
, it follows that for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\left(\langle\mathscr{C}_{\widetilde{o}^{n}_{i}}(\widetilde{G}_{n},X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})\rangle\right)_{i=1,2}\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}\left(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{i}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle\right)_{i=1,2}
and
(\widetilde{G}^{(1)},X^{G^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}})\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}(\widetilde{G}^{(2)},X^{G^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}})\overset{\text{(d)}}{=}(G,X^{G,\xi})
. Together with (
7.6
) and the independence of
\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(1)},X^{\widetilde{G}^{(1)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(1)}})\rangle
and
\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(2)},X^{\widetilde{G}^{(2)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(2)}})\rangle
, for any bounded, continuous functions
f_{1},f_{2}:{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]\to{\mathbb{R}}
, it follows that

\begin{array}[]{l}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{1}(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{1}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle)f_{2}(\langle\mathscr{C}_{o_{n}^{2}}(G_{n},X^{G_{n},\xi^{n}})\rangle)\right]\\
\qquad\qquad=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{1}(\langle\mathscr{C}_{\widetilde{o}^{n}_{1}}(\widetilde{G}_{n},X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})\rangle)f_{2}(\langle\mathscr{C}_{\widetilde{o}^{n}_{2}}(\widetilde{G}_{n},X^{\widetilde{G}_{n},\widetilde{\xi}^{n}})\rangle)\right]\\
\qquad\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left[f_{1}(\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(1)},X^{\widetilde{G}^{(1)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(1)}})\rangle)f_{2}(\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(2)},X^{\widetilde{G}^{(2)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(2)}})\rangle)\right]\\
\qquad\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left[f_{1}(\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(1)},X^{\widetilde{G}^{(1)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(1)}})\rangle)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[f_{2}(\langle(\widetilde{G}^{(2)},X^{\widetilde{G}^{(2)},\widetilde{\xi}^{(2)}})\rangle)\right]\\
\qquad\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left[f_{1}(\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[f_{2}(\langle(G,X^{G,\xi})\rangle)\right].\end{array}

This implies the desired asymptotic correlation decay in (
4.4
).
∎

Remark 7.4

(Correlation decay for more general IPS)
.

From the proof of Theorem
4.10
, it is not hard to see that, in fact, the conclusion of Theorem
4.10
holds under Assumptions
′
‣
5.1
and
′
‣
6.2
and the spatial localization property in place of Assumptions
1
and
2
and the finite dissociability property respectively.

Appendix A
Counterexample: When the SDE is not Well-Posed

There are two ways in which the SDE (
3.4
) can fail to be well-posed. The IPS may either admit no solution, or it may admit multiple solutions. It is easy to construct a time-inhomogeneous IPS that does not admit any solution by simply specifying unbounded jump rates that increase sufficiently fast with time such that any stochastic process
X:=X^{G,\xi}
for which (
3.4
) holds almost surely, must with positive probability explode (i.e., have infinitely many discontinuities on some compact interval in
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
). However, in this section, we show that well-posedness may fail even if we restrict ourselves to time-homogeneous Markov processes with uniformly bounded jump rates (which is even stronger than Assumption
1
). More precisely, we identify a graph
G
and an associated family of jump rates
{\bf r^{G}}
for which the SDE (
3.4
) has multiple strong solutions with different laws.

Proposition A.1

(An IPS that is not well-posed)
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be a rooted tree such that for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, all vertices
v
in the
k
th generation of
{\mathcal{T}}
(i.e., those that are distance
k
from the root) have
4^{k}
children. Consider the Markovian IPS with state space
{\mathcal{X}}=\{0,1\}
, allowable jump set
{\mathcal{J}}=\{1\}
, and for
v\in{\mathcal{T}}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and
x\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{T}}
, let

r^{{\mathcal{T}},v}_{1}(t,x)=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }x_{v}(t-)=0\text{ and }\sum_{u\in{\mathcal{N}}_{v}}x_{u}(t-)>0,\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(A.1)

Then the corresponding SDE (
3.4
) with
G={\mathcal{T}}
and initial state
\xi_{v}=0,v\in{\mathcal{T}}
, has multiple strong solutions with different laws.

Note that for the SDE (
3.4
) with
G={\mathcal{T}}
, the driving noises
\mathbf{N}^{\mathcal{T}}
are simply the collection of i.i.d. Poisson processes
(\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}}}_{v})_{v\in{\mathcal{T}}}
on
[0,\infty)^{2}\times\{1\}
with intensity measure
{\rm Leb}^{2}\otimes\delta_{1}
. Also observe that the family of jump rates
{\bf r^{\mathcal{T}}}
in Proposition
A.1
satisfy Assumption
1
with constants
C_{k,T}=1
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

To prove Proposition
A.1
, we first establish a certain property of the tree
{\mathcal{T}}
. We start with a definition that generalizes the notion of causal chains given in Definition
5.18
, for the above example.

Definition A.2

(Infinite causal chains)
.

Fix
T\in[0,\infty)
, and let
\{\mathbf{N}_{v}\}_{v\in{\mathcal{T}}}
be a collection of i.i.d. rate
1
Poisson processes on
[0,T]
. Then an infinite path
\Gamma=\{u_{0},u_{1},\ldots\}
with
u_{i}\in{\mathcal{T}}
,
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0},
is said to be a
({\mathcal{T}},{\bf\mathbf{N}})
-infinite causal chain ending at
u_{0}
during
[0,T]
if there exists a decreasing sequence
T>s_{0}>s_{1}>\cdots>0
such that
s_{i}\in\{t\in[0,T]:\mathbf{N}_{u_{i}}(\{t\})=1\}
for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}.
Moreover, we say a
({\mathcal{T}},{\bf\mathbf{N}})
-infinite causal chain exists if there exists some
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
u_{0}\in{\mathcal{T}}
, and a
({\mathcal{T}},{\bf\mathbf{N}})
-infinite causal chain ending at
u_{0}
during
[0,T]
.

Lemma A.3

(Existence of infinite causal chains)
.

There exists a
({\mathcal{T}},\mathbf{N})
-infinite causal chain.

Proof.

Fix
T\in[0,\infty)
. Define the infinite path
\Gamma^{\prime}:=\{u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{1},\dots\}
using the following recursive construction. Set
u^{\prime}_{0}=o
and for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, first check if there exists
v\in c_{u^{\prime}_{n}}({\mathcal{T}})
such that
\mathbf{N}_{v}(2^{-n-2}T,2^{-n-1}T)>0
. If this condition is satisfied, then set
u^{\prime}_{n+1}=v
(if multiple children satisfy this then we choose one arbitrarily from amongst them). If not, then we set
u^{\prime}_{n+1}
to be any child of
u^{\prime}_{n}
.

Next, set
\mathcal{I}:=\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:\mathbf{N}_{u^{\prime}_{n}}(2^{-n-1}T,2^{-n}T)>0\}
, and define
U:=\{u^{\prime}_{n},n\in\mathcal{I}\}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
. We now claim that
U
contains an infinite path a.s.. To see why, we prove the equivalent claim that
U
a.s. contains all but finitely many vertices in
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Let
\alpha_{n}:={\mathbb{P}}(u^{\prime}_{n+1}\notin U)
. Note that for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
v\in c_{u^{\prime}_{n}}({\mathcal{T}})
,

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}},T}_{v}(2^{-n-2}T,2^{-n-1}T)=0\right)=e^{-2^{-n-2}T}.

The independence of the Poisson processes
(\mathbf{N}_{v})_{v\in{\mathcal{T}}}
then implies that

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in c_{u^{\prime}_{n}}({\mathcal{T}})}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathbf{N}_{v}(2^{-n-2}T,2^{-n-1}T)=0\right)\leq\left(e^{-2^{-n-2}T}\right)^{4^{n}}=e^{-2^{n-2}T}.

Because
\alpha_{n}
decreases super-exponentially fast,
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{n}<\infty
. By the Borel-Cantelli lemma, it follows that with probability 1, there exists
m=m(\omega)
such that
\{u^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0},n\geq m}\subseteq U
, thus proving the claim. In turn, setting
u_{k}:=u^{\prime}_{m+k}
,
k={\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, the claim implies that the infinite path
\Gamma:=(u_{0},u_{1},\ldots)\subset U
and thus, a.s. there exists a decreasing sequence of times
\{t_{k}\in(2^{-(m+k+1)}T,2^{-(m+k)}T)\},k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0},
such that
\mathbf{N}_{u_{k}}(\{t_{k}\})=\mathbf{N}_{u^{\prime}_{m+k}}(\{t_{k}\})=1
. The lemma follows on setting
s_{k}=t_{k}
.
∎

Remark A.4

(Infinite causal chains)
.

The notion of an infinite causal chain can be extended in a natural way to the general class of IPS considered in this article with rates that satisfy Assumption
1
with a family of constants
{\bf C}
, by simply replacing
{\mathcal{T}}
by a general graph
G
in Definition
A.2
and, for each
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, the point process family
\mathbf{N}
by the family
\mathbf{N}^{G,T}
defined via (
5.9
). Note that this is consistent with the choice of
\mathbf{N}
in Definition
A.2
because the IPS from Proposition
A.1
is such that
{\mathcal{J}}=\{1\}
and Assumption
1
holds with
C_{k,T}:=1
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}},T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. From the proof of Proposition
5.17
, it is easy to see that for any IPS, the non-existence of infinite causal chains implies spatial localization. By Proposition
5.7
, spatial localization and Assumption
′
‣
5.1
imply strong well-posedness of the SDE (
3.4
). Therefore, if Assumption
′
‣
5.1
holds, then to construct a graph
G
and jump rates
\mathbf{r}^{G}
for which (
3.4
) is ill-posed, it is necessary to ensure that an infinite causal chain exists.

For the particular IPS from Proposition
A.1
, we now show that the existence of an infinite causal chain is also sufficient to conclude that the IPS is not well-posed.

Proof of Proposition
A.1
:

Although there are infinitely many strong solutions to the SDE (
3.4
) for the initial data
({\mathcal{T}},\xi)
with
\xi\equiv 0
and family of jump rates
\{r^{{\mathcal{T}},v}_{1}\}_{v\in{\mathcal{T}}}
as given in (
A.1
), we explicitly construct two of them. First, note that the process
X^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{v}(t)=0
for all
v\in V
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is clearly one strong solution.

Next, note that the driving noises
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}}}_{v},v\in{\mathcal{T}},
of the SDE are i.i.d. Poisson process on
[0,\infty)^{2}\otimes\{1\}
, and
\mathbf{N}_{v}(A):=\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}}}_{v}(A\times(0,1]\times\{1\})
,
v\in{\mathcal{T}},
are i.i.d. unit rate Poisson processes. Then, for
(v,t)\in{\mathcal{T}}\times(0,T)
, we say
(v,t)
has an infinite causal chain if there exists an infinite
({\mathcal{T}},\mathbf{N})
-causal chain ending at
v
on the interval
[0,t]
, in the sense of Remark
A.4
. Then for
v\in V
and
t\in[0,T]
, define

\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{v}(t):=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }(v,t)\text{ has an infinite causal chain,}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(A.2)

We claim
\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}
is also a solution to the same SDE. To see why the claim is true, define

\widehat{X}_{v}(t):=\int_{(0,t]\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{J}}}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{r\leq r_{1}^{{\mathcal{T}},v}(s,(\widehat{X}_{v},\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{{\mathcal{T}}\setminus\{v\}}))\right\}}\,\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}}}_{v}(ds,dr,dj),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

(A.3)

If
(v,t)\in{\mathcal{T}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
has no infinite causal chain, then by definition, for any
s\in[0,t]
, either
\mathbf{N}_{v}([s,t])=\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}}}_{v}([s,t]\times(0,1]\times\{1\})=0
, or there does not exist any
u\in{\mathcal{N}}_{v}({\mathcal{T}})
for which
(u,s)
has an infinite causal chain. Since, from the form given in (
A.1
),
r_{1}^{{\mathcal{T}},v}(s,(\widehat{X}_{v},\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{{\mathcal{T}}\setminus\{v\}}))
depends on
\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{{\mathcal{T}}\setminus\{v\}}
only via
\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{{\mathcal{N}}_{v}({\mathcal{T}})}(s-)
, it immediately follows that for every event
\sigma
in
\mathbf{N}_{v}
that lies in the interval
(0,t]
,
r^{{\mathcal{T}},v}_{1}(\sigma,\widehat{X}_{v},\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{{\mathcal{T}}\setminus\{v\}})=0
, and hence,
\widehat{X}_{v}(t)=\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{v}(t)=0
.

If
(v,t)
has an infinite causal chain then
\widetilde{X}_{v}(t)=1
by (
A.2
) and Definition
A.2
, there exists an infinite path
\Gamma=\{v=u_{0},u_{1},\dots\}
and a sequence
t\geq s_{0}>s_{1}>\dots>0
for which
\mathbf{N}_{u_{i}}(\{s_{i}\})=1
for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. This naturally implies that for any
s_{0}>s\geq s_{1}
,
(u_{1},s)
also has an infinite causal chain, so
\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{u_{1}}(s)=1
by (
A.2
). On the other hand, invoking (
A.1
) and the fact that
u_{1}\sim v
, it follows that for all
s>s_{1}
such that
\widehat{X}_{v}(s)=0
, we have

r^{{\mathcal{T}},v}_{1}\left(s,(\widehat{X}_{v},\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{{\mathcal{T}}\setminus\{v\}})\right)=1.

Also, note that by (
A.1
) and (
A.3
), for any
s>0
,

\widehat{X}_{v}(s-)=1\quad\Rightarrow\quad r^{{\mathcal{T}},v}_{1}\left(s,(\widehat{X}_{v},\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{{\mathcal{T}}\setminus\{v\}})\right)=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\widehat{X}_{v}(s)=1,

which shows that
1
is an absorbing state of
\widehat{X}_{v}
. Together, the last two displays imply that
\widehat{X}_{v}(s_{0})=1
. Indeed, because either
\widehat{X}_{v}(s_{0}-)=1
, in which case
\widehat{X}_{v}(s_{0})=1
by the last display, or
\widehat{X}_{v}(s_{0}-)=0
in which case by the form of the SDE (
A.3
),
\widehat{X}_{v}(s_{0})=\widehat{X}_{v}(s_{0}-)+\mathbf{N}^{{\mathcal{T}}}_{v}(\{s_{0}\}\times[0,1]\times\{1\})=\mathbf{N}_{v}(\{s_{0}\})=1
, with the last equality holding by the definition of the infinite causal chain and the fact that
u_{0}=v
. Because
s_{0}\leq t
and
1
is an absorbing state of
\widehat{X}_{v}
, it follows that
\widehat{X}_{v}(t)=1=\widetilde{X}_{v}(t)
.

Thus, we have shown that
\widehat{X}_{v}=\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}_{v}
. Because
v
and
t
were chosen arbitrarily, it follows that
\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}
satisfies (
3.4
). Finally, because
({\mathcal{T}},\mathbf{N})
-causal chains are
{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi,\mathbf{N}^{\mathcal{T}}}
-adapted,
\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}
must also be
{\mathbb{F}}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi,\mathbf{N}^{\mathcal{T}}}
-adapted by (
A.2
). Thus,
\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}
is also a strong solution to (
3.4
), and by Lemma
A.3
,
X^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}\neq\widetilde{X}^{{\mathcal{T}},\xi}
a.s..
∎

Appendix B
Measurable Representatives of Graph Isomorphism Classes
