In this section, we establish the measurability of marked graph representatives of random isomorphism classes, culminating in the proofs of Lemmas
6.2
and
6.6
. Along the way, we introduce a Polish space of canonical representative graphs that is compatible with local convergence, which may be of independent interest. We start with preliminaries in Appendix
B.1
and then establish the main measurable selection results in Appendix
B.2
.

Throughout the section, we let
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}
be Polish spaces denoting the respective spaces in which edge and vertex marks lie, and let
d_{\bar{{\mathcal{Z}}}}
and
d_{{\mathcal{Z}}}
be associated metrics that induce the respective topologies. Let
\odot
be an arbitrary point not lying in
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}\cup{\mathcal{Z}}
, define the spaces
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{\odot}:=\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}\cup\{\odot\}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\odot}:={\mathcal{Z}}\cup\{\odot\}
, and endow them with the corresponding Polish topologies from
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}
, respectively, with
\odot
being an isolated point. Throughout the section, we often implicitly denote (possibly random)
[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-graphs by
G:=(V,E,o,\overline{\vartheta},\vartheta)
and
G_{n}:=(V_{n},E_{n},o_{n},\overline{\vartheta}^{n},\vartheta^{n})
.

Definition B.1

(Random closed subsets)
.

Given a Polish space
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
, let
\mathrm{clo}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime})
denote the set of closed subsets of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
. Given in addition a measurable space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}})
, a mapping
F:\Omega\mapsto\mathrm{clo}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime})
is said to be an
{\mathcal{F}}
-random closed subset of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
if it is weakly measurable in the following sense: for every open set
U\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
, the set
\{\omega\in\Omega:U\cap F(\omega)\neq\emptyset\}
lies in
{\mathcal{F}}
. Moreover,
F
is said to be non-empty if
F(\omega)\neq\emptyset
for every
\omega\in\Omega
.

Several results in this appendix make use of the following measurable selection theorem.

Theorem B.2

(Kuratowski & Ryll-Nardzewski Measurable Selection Theorem)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
is a Polish space,
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}})
is a measurable space and
F:\Omega\mapsto\mathrm{clo}({\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime})
is a non-empty
{\mathcal{F}}
-random closed subset of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
. Then there exists an
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable function
Z:\Omega\to{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
such that
Z(\omega)\in F(\omega)
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.

Proof.

This is simply a restatement of
[
9
, Theorem 6.9.3]
in our notation, in particular with
X,{\mathcal{B}}
, and
\Psi
in
[
9
]
replaced by
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
,
{\mathcal{F}}
, and
F
, respectively.
∎

B.1
A Canonical Subspace of Rooted Graphs and its Properties

In this section, we construct a canonical subspace of the space of
[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-graphs introduced in Section
2.4
and equip it with a topology that is compatible with the topology of
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
. In the ensuing definition, we use the following standard notion of convergence of subsets of
{\mathbb{N}}
. Given
S_{n}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}},n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
S\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
, we write
S_{n}\to S
if and only if

S=\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bigcap_{n^{\prime}>n}S_{n^{\prime}}=\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bigcup_{n^{\prime}>n}S_{n^{\prime}}.

Equivalently,
S_{n}\to S
if and only if for every
k\in S
, there exist only finitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
k\notin S_{n}
and for every
k^{\prime}\notin S
there exist only finitely
n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
k^{\prime}\in S_{n^{\prime}}
. In addition, we equip
{\mathbb{N}}
with the discrete topology. Lastly, recall the definition of
\text{cl}_{v}(G)
from Section
2.1
.

Definition B.3

(Canonical space of representative graphs)
.

We equip the canonical space
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
of rooted
[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-graphs

\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]:=\{[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]\text{-graphs }G=(V,E,o,\overline{\vartheta},\vartheta)\text{ s.t. }V\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}\},

(B.1)

with the following notion of convergence:
G_{n}\to G
in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
if and only if

1.

\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{n}=V
;

2.

\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{n}=E
;

3.

\lim_{n\to\infty}o_{n}=o
;

4.

\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{\vartheta}^{n}_{e}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{e\in E_{n}\right\}}=\overline{\vartheta_{e}}
for all
e\in E
;

5.

\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta^{n}_{v}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{v\in V_{n}\right\}}=\vartheta_{v}
for all
v\in V
;

6.

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[\max\{u\in\text{cl}_{v}(G_{n})\}\mathbb{I}_{\left\{v\in V_{n}\right\}}\right]=\max\{u\in\text{cl}_{v}(G)\}
for each
v\in V
.

For
G,G^{\prime}\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
, notions such as graph distance
d_{G}(\cdot,\cdot)
, truncations
B_{m}(G)
, sets of isomorphisms
I(G,G^{\prime})
and isomorphism classes
\langle G\rangle
are all defined as in Sections
2.1
and
2.4
.

Remark B.4

(Definition
B.3
.6)
.

The least intuitive condition is perhaps condition 6 of Definition
B.3
, but it is necessary for the topology on
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
to be compatible with the topology of
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{Z}}}]
(in the sense made precise in Lemma
B.6
). This is best illustrated via an example of how compatibility could fail without condition 6. Let
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}={\mathcal{Z}}=\{1\}
be trivial and suppose that for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
o_{n}=o=1
,
V_{n}=\{1,n+1\}
,
V=\{1\}
,
E_{n}=\{\{1,n+1\}\}
, and
E=\emptyset
. Then conditions 1-5 of Definition
B.3
are all satisfied, and only condition 6 fails. However, note that
G_{n}\cong G_{n^{\prime}}\ncong G
for all
n,n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, so
\langle G_{n}\rangle\to\langle G_{1}\rangle\neq\langle G\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
.

In order to apply Theorem
B.2
to find measurable representatives of isomorphism classes, it is necessary to prove that the space
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
is Polish. This can be done by direct verification using Definition
B.3
. We fill in the details for completeness.

Lemma B.5
.

The space
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
is Polish.

Proof.

Define the Polish space
{\mathcal{R}}:=\left({\mathbb{N}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}_{\odot}\times(\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{\odot})^{\mathbb{N}}\right)^{{\mathbb{N}}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
, equipped with the product topology, and consider the map
\psi:\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]\to{\mathcal{R}}
defined by
\psi(G):=((\psi_{k}(G))_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}},o)
, where for each
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\psi_{k}(G)=(c^{\prime}_{k},\vartheta^{\prime}_{k},(\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}})_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}}),
with

c^{\prime}_{k}:=\begin{cases}\max\{\text{cl}_{k}(G)\}&\text{ if }k\in V,\\
k&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\;\quad\vartheta^{\prime}_{k}:=\begin{cases}\vartheta_{k}&\text{ if }k\in V,\\
\odot&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}\;\quad\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}:=\begin{cases}\overline{\vartheta}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}&\text{ if }\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E,\\
\odot&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

We then have the following observations:

(i)

The map
\psi
is a bijection from
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
to
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
is the subset of elements
\zeta=((c^{\prime}_{k},\vartheta^{\prime}_{k},\{\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}\}_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}},o)
in
{\mathcal{R}}
that satisfy the following constraints:

(a)

o\in V_{\zeta}:=\{k\in{\mathbb{N}}:\vartheta^{\prime}_{k}\neq\odot\}
;

(b)

for every
k\notin V_{\zeta}
,
c^{\prime}_{k}=k
;

(c)

One has
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\subseteq V_{\zeta}

for every
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E_{\zeta}:=\cup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}E_{\zeta}(k)
, where
E_{\zeta}(k):=\{\{k,k^{\prime}\}\subset{\mathbb{N}}:k\neq k^{\prime},\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}\neq\odot\}
;

(d)

for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
c^{\prime}_{k}=\max[\{k\}\cup\{k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}:\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E_{\zeta}(k)\}]
.

It is trivial to check that for every
G\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
,
\psi(G)
must satisfy conditions (a)–(d) above. Thus, the image of
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
under
\psi
is contained in
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
. On the other hand, note that any
\zeta=((c^{\prime}_{k},\vartheta^{\prime}_{k},\{\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}\}_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}},o)\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
has a unique inverse under
\psi
, described by
\psi^{-1}(\zeta)=G_{\zeta}:=(V_{\zeta},E_{\zeta},o_{\zeta},\overline{\vartheta}_{k}(\zeta),\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}(\zeta))
, where
V_{\zeta}
and
E_{\zeta}
are respectively defined as in (a) and (c) above,
o_{\zeta}=o
,
\vartheta_{k}(\zeta)=\vartheta^{\prime}_{k}\in{\mathcal{Z}}
for
k\in V_{\zeta}
, and
\overline{\vartheta}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}(\zeta)=\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}\in\bar{{\mathcal{Z}}}
for
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E_{\zeta}
. Condition (c) also ensures that
\zeta\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
implies
|E_{\zeta}(k)|<\infty
for every
k\in V_{\zeta}
, and so the resulting graph
G_{\zeta}
is locally finite. Moreover, conditions (a) and (c) together ensure that the edge and vertex marks lie in
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}
, respectively, thus showing that
G_{\zeta}
is a
[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-graph. Lastly, it is easy to see that for any
G\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
and
\zeta\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
,
\psi\circ\psi^{-1}(\zeta)=\zeta
and
\psi^{-1}\circ\psi(G)=G
, thus proving
\psi
is a bijection between
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
and
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
.

(ii)

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
is a closed subset of
{\mathcal{R}}
under componentwise convergence: suppose the sequence
\zeta^{n}=((c^{\prime,n}_{k},\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k},\{\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}\}_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}},o^{n})\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}},n\in{\mathbb{N}},
converges to
\zeta=((c^{\prime}_{k},\vartheta^{\prime}_{k},\{\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}\}_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}},o)
pointwise. Then
\zeta\in{\mathcal{R}}
because
{\mathcal{R}}
is Polish. To show
\zeta\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
, it suffices to show that
\zeta
satisfies the constraints (a)–(d) in (i) above. To prove condition (a), we argue by contradiction. Suppose
\vartheta^{\prime}_{o}=\odot
. Then since
o^{n}\rightarrow o
it follows that there exists
N<\infty
such that
o^{n}=o
for all
n\geq N
. Since
\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{o}\rightarrow\vartheta^{\prime}_{o}
and
\odot
is isolated, this implies that for all sufficiently large
n
,
\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{o_{n}}=\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{o}=\odot
, which implies
o_{n}\not\in V_{\zeta^{n}}
and thus contradicts the assumption that
\zeta^{n}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
. Thus, this proves that
\zeta
satisfies condition (a). Condition (c) can be established in an exactly analogous fashion. Next, suppose
k\notin V_{\zeta}
. Then since
\zeta
satisfies (a) as shown above,
\vartheta_{k}=\odot
and since
\odot
is isolated and
\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k}\rightarrow\vartheta_{k}
, it follows that
\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k}=\odot
for all sufficiently large
n
. In turn, since
\zeta^{n}\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
, this implies
k\not\in V_{\zeta^{n}}
and hence that
c_{k}^{\prime,n}=k
. Since
c_{k}^{\prime,n}\rightarrow c_{k}^{\prime}
, this implies
c_{k}^{\prime}=k
and condition (b) follows for
\zeta
. Finally, condition (d) for
\zeta
, which implies each
E_{\zeta}(k)
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}},
is a finite set, can be deduced by similarly observing that
c_{k}^{\prime,n}=c_{k}
and therefore
E_{\zeta^{n}}(k)=E_{\zeta}(k)
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and all sufficiently large
n
, and the fact that each
\zeta^{n}
satisfies condition (d).

(iii)

The map
\psi
is a homeomorphism from
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
to
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
: For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
G_{n}=(V_{n},E_{n},o_{n},\overline{\vartheta}_{n},\vartheta_{n})\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
and
\psi(G_{n})=((c^{\prime,n}_{k},\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k},\{\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}\}_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}},o^{n})\in\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
. Then we wish to show that
G_{n}\to G=(V,E,o,\vartheta,\overline{\vartheta})
in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
if and only if
\psi(G_{n})\to\psi(G):=((c^{\prime}_{k},\vartheta^{\prime}_{k},\{\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}\}_{k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}},o)
in
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
.

(a)

If
G_{n}\to G
, then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\psi(G_{n})=\psi(G)
: for any
k,k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, conditions 1 and 2 of Definition
B.3
imply that if
k\in V
and
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E
, then
k\in V_{n}
and
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large. Then for any
k\in V
, condition 6 implies that
\lim_{n\to\infty}c^{\prime,n}_{k}\to\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
k\in V_{n}\end{subarray}}\max\{\text{cl}_{k}(G_{n})\}=\max\{\text{cl}_{k}(G)\}=c^{\prime}_{k}
and for
k\notin V
,
k\notin V_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large so
\lim_{n\to\infty}c^{\prime,n}_{k}=k=c^{\prime}_{k}
. Conditions 4 and 5 imply that for
k\in V
and
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}(\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k},\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}})=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty,(\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k},\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}})\in V_{n}\times E_{n}\end{subarray}}(\vartheta^{n}_{k},\overline{\vartheta}^{n}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}})=(\vartheta_{k},\overline{\vartheta}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}})=(\vartheta^{\prime}_{k},\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}})
. If
k\notin V
, then
k\notin V_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large and because
\odot
is an isolated point, this implies that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k}=\odot=\vartheta^{\prime}_{k}
. By the same argument, if
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\notin E
, then
\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}=\odot=\overline{\vartheta}^{{}^{\prime}}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}
. Lastly,
o^{n}\to o
by condition 3 of Definition
B.3
. Thus,
\psi(G_{n})\to\psi(G)
, which establishes the continuity of
\psi
.

(b)

If
\psi(G_{n})\to\psi(G)
, then
G_{n}\to G
: conditions 1 and 2 of Definition
B.3
follow from the convergence of
\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k}
to
\vartheta^{\prime}_{k}
and the convergence of
\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}
to
\overline{\vartheta}^{{}^{\prime}}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}
and the fact that
\odot
is isolated in
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\odot}
and
\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}_{\odot}
. This directly implies that every vertex
k\in V
and edge
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E
is in
V_{n}
and
E_{n}
respectively for all sufficiently large
n
, and likewise every non-vertex
k\in{\mathbb{N}}\setminus V
and non-edge
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in{\mathbb{N}}\setminus E
is not in
V_{n}
or
E_{n}
respectively for all sufficiently large
n
. Condition 3 follows from the convergence of
o^{n}
to
o
. To prove condition 4, recall that we have already shown that any
k\in V
is in
V_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large. Thus,
\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
k\in V_{n}\end{subarray}}\vartheta^{n}_{k}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
k\in V_{n}\end{subarray}}\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{k}=\vartheta^{\prime}_{k}=\vartheta_{k}
. The proof of condition 5 is exactly analogous except we apply the fact that
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E
implies
\{k,k^{\prime}\}\in E_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large and then apply the convergence of
\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}
to
\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\{k,k^{\prime}\}}
. Lastly, for each
k\in V
, because
c^{\prime,n}_{k}=c^{\prime}_{k}
for sufficiently large
n
, this implies that
E_{\zeta}(k)\cap\{c^{\prime}_{k}+1,\dots\}=\emptyset
for
n
sufficiently large so
c^{\prime}_{k}\geq\max\text{cl}_{k}(G)
. However,
\{k,c^{\prime}_{k}\}\in E
if and only if
\{k,c^{\prime}_{k}\}\in E_{n}
for all
n
sufficiently large, and because
\{k,c^{\prime,n}_{k}\}\in E_{n}
and
c^{\prime,n}_{k}=c^{\prime}_{k}
for
n
sufficiently large, this implies that
\{k,c^{\prime}_{k}\}\in E
so
c^{\prime}_{k}=\max\text{cl}_{k}(G)
. Thus, condition 6 holds.

Because
\psi
is a one-to-one map onto
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
, this proves the claim.

Since
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
is homeomorphic to the closed subset
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}
of the Polish space
{\mathcal{R}}
, it is also Polish.
∎

Next, in Lemma
B.6
below, we show that the map
G\mapsto\langle G\rangle
is continuous and its set-valued inverse map
\langle G\rangle\mapsto\{G\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]:G\in\langle G\rangle\}
is lower semicontinuous in the sense of
[
3
, Definition 1.4.2]
.

Lemma B.6

(Equivalence with the local topology)
.

If
G_{n}\to G
in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
, then the isomorphism classes also converge locally, that is,
\langle G_{n}\rangle\to\langle G\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
. Moreover, given the limit
\langle G_{n}\rangle\to\langle G\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
and any representative
G\in\langle G\rangle
such that
G\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
, there exists a sequence of representatives
G_{n}\in\langle G_{n}\rangle
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}},
such that
G_{n}\to G
in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
. In other words, the correspondence
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]\ni\langle G\rangle\mapsto\{G\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]:G\in\langle G\rangle\}
is lower semicontinuous.

Proof.

For convenience of notation, let
[n]:=\{1,\dots,n\}
. We start by proving the first statement. Fix any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
M_{m}:=\max\{v\in B_{m}(G)\}
. Then conditions 1 and 2 of Definition
B.3
imply that for sufficiently large
n
,
V\cap[M_{m}]=V_{n}\cap[M_{m}]
and
E\cap[M_{m}]^{2}=E_{n}\cap[M_{m}]^{2}
. Condition 3 implies that
o_{n}=o
for
n
sufficiently large. Thus, the subgraphs of
B_{m}([G_{*}])
and
B_{m}([G_{n,*}])
induced by the set
[M_{m}]
exactly match for sufficiently large
n
. Then by condition 6,
\max\{\text{cl}_{v}(G_{n})\}=\max\{\text{cl}_{v}(G_{n})\}
for all
v\in V\cap[M_{m}]
and
n
sufficiently large. Because
B_{m}(G)\subseteq[M_{m}]
, these statements imply that all of the vertices in
B_{m}(G_{n})
also fall inside
[M_{m}]
, and hence,
B_{m}([G_{*}])=B_{m}([G_{n,*}])
for all sufficiently large
n
, that is
N_{m}:=\min\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}:B_{m}([G_{n^{\prime},*}])=B_{m}([G_{*}])\text{ for all }n^{\prime}\geq n\}
is finite (where the minimum of an empty set is taken to be
\infty
). For
n>N_{m}
, let
\varphi_{n,m}:B_{m}(G)\to B_{m}(G_{n})
be the identity isomorphism. Then for each
v\in B_{m}(G)
and
e\in E_{B_{m}(G)}
, conditions 4 and 5 of Definition
B.3
imply
\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
n>N_{m}\end{subarray}}\vartheta^{n}_{\varphi_{n,m}(v)}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
v\in V_{n}\end{subarray}}\vartheta^{n}_{v}=\vartheta_{v},
and
\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
n>N_{m}\end{subarray}}\overline{\vartheta}^{n}_{\varphi_{n,m}(e)}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
e\in E_{n}\end{subarray}}\overline{\vartheta}^{n}_{e}=\overline{\vartheta}_{e}
. By Definition
2.1
, this proves that
\langle G_{n}\rangle\to\langle G\rangle
.

To prove the second statement, first consider the case when the representative
G\in\langle G\rangle
has a vertex set that is canonical in the sense that
V=[|V|]=\{1,\dots,|V|\}
where we interpret
[\infty]
as
{\mathbb{N}}
. It is easy to see that one can always choose representatives
G^{\prime}_{n}=(V^{\prime}_{n},E^{\prime}_{n},o^{\prime}_{n},\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n},\vartheta^{\prime,n})
of
\langle G_{n}\rangle,

n\in{\mathbb{N}},
such that

\displaystyle V^{\prime}_{n}

\displaystyle=[|V^{\prime}_{n}|]=\{1,\dots,|V^{\prime}_{n}|\}\text{ for }n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(B.2)

Then by Definition
2.1
, for each
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, there exist
n_{m}<\infty
and a collection of isomorphisms
\varphi_{n,m}\in I(B_{m}([G_{*}]),B_{m}([G^{\prime}_{n,*}]))
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0},n>n_{m},
such that for each
m\in{\mathbb{N}},
the inclusions
v\in B_{m}(G)
and
e\in E_{B_{m}(G)}
imply

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
n>n_{m}\end{subarray}}d_{\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}}(\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\varphi_{n,m}(e)},\overline{\vartheta}_{e})=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
n>n_{m}\end{subarray}}d_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{\varphi_{n,m}(v)},\vartheta_{v})=0.

(B.3)

Hence, there exists a sequence of non-decreasing integers
M_{n},n\in{\mathbb{N}},
converging to infinity such that for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the inclusions
v\in B_{M_{n}}(G)
and
e\in E_{B_{M_{n}}(G)}
imply
d_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{\varphi_{n,M_{n}}(v)},\vartheta_{v})<2^{-M_{n}}
and
d_{\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}}}(\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\varphi_{n,M_{n}}(e)},\overline{\vartheta}_{e})<2^{-M_{n}}
when
M_{n}>0
. Set
\varphi_{n}:=\varphi_{n,M_{n}}
, and for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}},
define
\overline{\varphi}_{n}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{N}}
by

\overline{\varphi}_{n}(v)=\begin{cases}\varphi_{n}(v)&\text{ if }v\in B_{M_{n}}(G),\\
w^{n}_{v}&\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where if
v
is the
k
th smallest element of
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus B_{M_{n}}(G)
, then
w^{n}_{v}
is the
k
th smallest element of
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus B_{M_{n}}(G_{n}^{\prime})
. Now for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, define

G_{n}:=(V_{n},E_{n},o_{n},\overline{\vartheta}^{n},\vartheta^{n}):=\left(\overline{\varphi}^{-1}_{n}(V^{\prime}_{n}),\overline{\varphi}^{-1}_{n}(E^{\prime}_{n}),\overline{\varphi}_{n}^{-1}(o^{\prime}_{n}),(\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime,n}_{\overline{\varphi}_{n}(e)})_{e\in E_{n}},(\vartheta^{\prime,n}_{\overline{\varphi}_{n}(v)})_{v\in V_{n}}\right).

By construction, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
V_{n}=V^{\prime}_{n}=\{1,\dots,|V_{n}|\}
, just as in (
B.2
). Then by definition, for each
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and noting that (i) when
m\leq M_{n}
,
\overline{\varphi}_{n}|_{B_{m}(G)}=\varphi_{n,M_{n}}|_{B_{m}(G)}
(which implies
\overline{\varphi}^{-1}_{n}|_{B_{m}(G_{n})}=\varphi_{n,M_{n}}^{-1}|_{B_{m}(G_{n})}
) and (ii)
M_{n}
increases to infinity, one has

B_{m}([G_{n,*}])=\varphi^{-1}_{n,M_{n}}(B_{m}([G^{\prime}_{n,*}]))=B_{m}([G_{*}])\text{ for }n\text{ sufficiently large.}

(B.4)

For every
v\in V
and
e\in E
, there exists
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
v\in B_{m}(G)
,
e\in E_{B_{m}(G)}
. Then by (
B.4
),
v\in V_{n}
and
e\in E_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large. On the other hand, suppose
v\notin V
. Then, because
V
is in canonical form,
|V|<v
is finite. Thus,
G
has radius
M<\infty
. Setting
m=M+1
in (
B.4
),
B_{M+1}([G_{n,*}])=B_{M+1}([G_{*}])=[G_{*}]
for
n
sufficiently large. Furthermore, because
B_{M+1}([G_{n,*}])
has radius
M
, it follows that
[G_{n,*}]=B_{M+1}([G_{n,*}])=[G_{*}]
. Thus,
v\notin V_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large, which implies that
V_{n}\to V
. Now, suppose
e=\{u,v\}\notin E
. Then either (without loss of generality)
u\notin V
, in which case
u\notin V_{n}
for sufficiently large
n
and
\{u,v\}\notin E_{n}
for such
n
, or
u,v\in V
. In the latter case, we can fix
m
so that
u,v\in B_{m}(G)
. Then for
n
sufficiently large,
B_{m}([G_{n,*}])=B_{m}([G_{*}])
by (
B.4
), so
\{u,v\}\notin E_{n}
. Thus,
E_{n}\to E
. Setting
m=0
,
o_{n}=\overline{\varphi}_{0}^{-1}(o^{\prime}_{n})=o
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Lastly, for each
v\in V
, fix
m
such that
v\in B_{m-1}(G)
. Then by (
B.4
),
\text{cl}_{v}(G_{n})=\text{cl}_{v}(G)
for
n
sufficiently large. Thus, conditions 1-3 and 6 of Definition
B.3
hold. Now for each
v\in V
and
e\in E
, there must exist
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
v\in B_{m}(G)
and
e\in E_{B_{m}(G)}
. Then, by the definition of
M_{n}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\vartheta^{n}_{v},\vartheta_{v})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\vartheta^{{}^{\prime}n}_{\varphi_{n,M_{n}}(v)},\vartheta_{v})=\lim_{n\to\infty}2^{-M_{n}}=0

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\overline{\vartheta}^{n}_{e},\overline{\vartheta}_{e})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\overline{\vartheta}^{{}^{\prime}n}_{\varphi_{n,M_{n}}(e)},\overline{\vartheta}_{e})=\lim_{n\to\infty}2^{-M_{n}}=0.

Thus, conditions 4 and 5 of Definition
B.3
also hold, proving that
G_{n}\to G
in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
.

We finish the proof of the second assertion by considering the general case in which
G
is an arbitrary representative of
\langle G\rangle
with no restriction on the vertex set. In this case, there exists
G^{\prime}:=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime},o^{\prime},\overline{\vartheta}^{\prime},\vartheta^{\prime})\cong G
whose vertex set
V^{\prime}:=V_{G^{\prime}}
is in canonical form. Using the argument above, construct the sequence
\{G^{\prime}_{n}\}
whose vertex sets
V^{\prime}_{n}:=V_{G^{\prime}_{n}}
are in canonical form and such that
G^{\prime}_{n}\to G^{\prime}
in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
. Given any isomorphism
\varphi\in I(G^{\prime},G)
, define

\overline{\varphi}(v):=\begin{cases}\varphi(v)&\text{ if }v\in V_{G^{\prime}},\\
w_{v}&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

where if
v
is the
k
th smallest element of
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus V^{\prime}
, then
w_{v}
is the
k
th smallest element of
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus V
. Then
\overline{\varphi}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{N}}
is a bijection, so for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

G_{n}:=\overline{\varphi}(G_{n}):=(\overline{\varphi}(V^{\prime}_{n}),\overline{\varphi}(E^{\prime}_{n}),\overline{\varphi}(o^{\prime}_{n}),(\overline{\vartheta}^{{}^{\prime}n}_{\overline{\varphi}^{-1}(e)})_{e\in\overline{\varphi}(E^{\prime}_{n})},(\vartheta^{{}^{\prime}n}_{\overline{\varphi}^{-1}(v)})_{v\in\overline{\varphi}(V^{\prime}_{n})}),

is isomorphic to
G^{\prime}_{n}
and
G_{n}\to G
. Thus, the correspondence
\langle G\rangle\mapsto\{G^{\prime}\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]:G^{\prime}\in\langle G\rangle\}
is lower semicontinuous by
[
3
, Definition 1.4.2]
.
∎

B.2
Existence of Measurable Selections

The goal of this section is to establish Lemmas
6.2
and
6.6
. We begin this section by showing that every random isomorphism class of rooted graphs has a measurable random representative.

Lemma B.7

(Measurable selection of representative graphs)
.

Given any
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-random element
\langle G\rangle
, there exists a
\sigma(\langle G\rangle)
-measurable representative
[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-random graph
G
in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
.

Proof.

For every
\langle H\rangle\in{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
, define

\Psi_{*}(\langle H\rangle):=\{H^{\prime}\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]:H^{\prime}\in\langle H\rangle\}.

(B.5)

Then the set
\Psi_{*}(\langle H\rangle)
is non-empty since it contains
H
and is also closed due to the continuity of the map
H\mapsto\langle H\rangle
established in Lemma
B.6
. Thus
\Psi_{*}
maps any isomorphism class in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
to the closed set in
\mathrm{clo}(\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}])
that contains all graphs in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
that lie in that isomorphism class. Given the
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-random element
\langle G\rangle
, we first argue that to prove the lemma it suffices to prove the claim that for every open set
U\subseteq\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
, the following set is open in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
:

\Lambda_{*}^{U}:=\{\langle H\rangle\in{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]:\Psi_{*}(\langle H\rangle)\cap U\neq\emptyset\}=\bigcup_{H\in U}\{\langle H\rangle\}.

Indeed, since
\langle G\rangle
is a
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-random element, the claim implies that
\psi_{*}(\langle G\rangle)
is a non-empty
\sigma(\langle G\rangle)
-random closed subset of
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
in the sense of Definition
B.1
, and the lemma follows on applying Theorem
B.2
with
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}=\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
,
F=\psi_{*}(\langle G\rangle)
, and
{\mathcal{F}}=\sigma(\langle G\rangle)
.

We now turn to the proof of the claim. If
U
is empty then so is
\Lambda_{*}^{U}
. Now, fix
U\subseteq\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
non-empty. Then
\Lambda_{*}^{U}
is trivially non-empty as well. For
\langle H\rangle\in\Lambda_{*}^{U}
, suppose there exists a sequence
\{\langle H_{n}\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of (deterministic) elements of
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
converging to
\langle H\rangle
. Select
H^{\prime}\in\Psi_{*}(\langle H\rangle)\cap U
(which is non-empty since
\Lambda_{*}^{U}\neq\emptyset
). By Lemma
B.6
, there exists a sequence of representative graphs
H^{\prime}_{n}\in\langle H_{n}\rangle
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}},
that converges to
H^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
. Because
H^{\prime}\in U
and
U
is open,
H^{\prime}_{n}\in U
for all but finitely many
n
. By the definition of
\Lambda_{*}^{U}
, this implies
\langle H_{n}\rangle\in\Lambda_{*}^{U}
for all but finitely many
n
. Because this is true for any sequence converging to an element in
\Lambda_{*}^{U}
, and because
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
is a Polish space (in which convergence is equivalent to sequential convergence)
\Lambda_{*}^{U}
is open. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark B.8

(A Polish space of maps)
.

For
W_{1},W_{2}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
, let
{\mathscr{W}}(W_{1},W_{2})
be the space of mappings from
W_{1}
to
W_{2}
, and define
{\mathscr{W}}:=\bigcup_{W_{1},W_{2}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathscr{W}}(W_{1},W_{2})
. Then any map
f\in{\mathscr{W}}(W_{1},W_{2})\subset{\mathscr{W}}
can be embedded in
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{{\mathbb{N}}}
(equipped with the discrete product topology) via the bijective map

\beta(f):=(\beta_{n}(f))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\text{ where }\begin{cases}f(n)&\text{ if }n\in W_{1},\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}\quad\mbox{ for }f\in{\mathscr{W}}(W_{1},W_{2}).

(B.6)

We equip
{\mathscr{W}}
with the topology induced by
\beta
; that is, we define a subset
U\subseteq{\mathscr{W}}
to be open if and only if
\beta(U)\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{{\mathbb{N}}}
is open. With this definition,
\beta
is automatically a homeomorphism, and
{\mathscr{W}}
is a Polish space. We now apply Theorem
B.2
to select the isomorphisms in Lemma
6.2
in a measurable manner.

Applying Theorem
B.2
, it is possible to establish Lemma
B.9
, which shows that the isomorphisms in Lemma
6.2
can be chosen in a measurable manner, and Lemma
B.10
, which shows that driving maps on different random graphs can be constructed so as to be consistent with random isomorphisms between those graphs. The latter property is used to construct the driving noise in Lemma
6.6
in a measurable manner.

Lemma B.9

(Measurable selection of isomorphisms)
.

Fix a probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
that supports finite,
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-random elements
G_{i}
,
i=1,2,
that satisfy
{\mathbb{P}}(G_{1}\cong G_{2})>0
. Then given any
{\mathcal{F}}
-random closed subset
A
of
{\mathscr{W}}
such that
A
is a non-empty subset of
I(G_{1},G_{2})
on the event
\{G_{1}\cong G_{2}\}
, there exists an
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable map
\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}(V_{1},V_{2})
such that
\varphi\in A
on the event
\{G_{1}\cong G_{2}\}
.

Proof.

Given any
{\mathcal{F}}
-random closed subset
A
of
{\mathcal{M}}
such that
A\subseteq I(G_{1},G_{2})
, consider the set-valued mapping
B(\omega):={\mathscr{W}}(V_{1},V_{2})
if
G_{1}\ncong G_{2}
and
B(\omega):=A
otherwise. Note that by finiteness of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
,
B
is also finite because
B\subseteq{\mathscr{W}}(V_{1},V_{2})
, and thus
B(\omega)
is closed for all
\omega\in\Omega
. Let
\overline{B}=\{\beta(f):f\in B\}
, with
\beta
as in (
B.6
), and note that
\overline{B}(\omega)
is similarly finite and therefore closed and non-empty for all
\omega\in\Omega
. We claim that
\overline{B}(\omega)
is weakly
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable. That is, for every open
U\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{{\mathbb{N}}}
, the set
\{\overline{B}\cap U\neq\emptyset\}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable. If the claim holds, then
\overline{B}
is a non-empty
{\mathcal{F}}
-random closed subset, so by Theorem
B.2
with
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}={\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{{\mathbb{N}}}
and
F=\overline{B}
, there exists an
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable random variable
b
such that
b(\omega)\in\overline{B}(\omega)
for every
\omega\in\Omega
. The lemma follows on setting
\varphi=\beta^{-1}(b)
.

To prove the claim, first note that the set
\{\omega:G_{1}\cong G_{2}\}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable by assumption. Moreover, fixing any open set
U\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{{\mathbb{N}}}
, note that
\{G_{1}\cong G_{2}\}\cap\{\overline{B}\cap U\neq\emptyset\}=\{G_{1}\cong G_{2}\}\cap\{A\cap\beta^{-1}(U)\neq\emptyset\}
, and also that
U^{\prime}:=\beta^{-1}(U)\subset{\mathscr{W}}
is open since
\beta
is continuous. Because
A
is an
{\mathcal{F}}
-random closed subset, this implies
\{G_{1}\cong G_{2}\}\cap\{\overline{B}\cap U\neq\emptyset\}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable. Next, observe that
\{G_{1}\ncong G_{2}\}\cap\{\overline{B}\cap U\neq\emptyset\}=\{G_{1}\ncong G_{2}\}\cap\{{\mathscr{W}}(V_{1},V_{2})\cap\beta^{-1}(U)\neq\emptyset\}=\{G_{1}\ncong G_{2}\}\cap\{{\mathscr{W}}(V_{1},V_{2})\cap U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\}
. However, note that

\{{\mathscr{W}}(V_{1},V_{2})\cap U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\}=\bigcup_{\text{finite }W_{1},W_{2}\subset{\mathbb{N}}}\{V_{i}(\omega)=W_{i},i=1,2\}\cap\{U^{\prime}\cap{\mathscr{W}}(W_{1},W_{2})\neq\emptyset\},

which is a countable union of
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable sets since
U^{\prime}
is open. Thus,
\{\overline{B}\cap U\neq\emptyset\}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable for every open
U
, and the claim follows.
∎

The following lemma ensures driving maps on different graphs can be constructed so as to be consistent with isomorphisms between those graphs, which is required in Sections
6
and
7
.

Lemma B.10

(Existence of consistent driving maps)
.

Fix a probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
that supports the
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{Z}}},{\mathcal{Z}}]
-random elements
G_{i}
,
i=1,2
. Let
M
be an
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable random variable such that
M(\omega)\in\{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:B_{m}(G_{1}(\omega))\cong B_{m}(G_{2}(\omega))\}
for every
\omega\in\Omega
. Then for any
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable isomorphism
\varphi\in I(B_{M}(G_{1}),B_{M}(G_{2}))
and
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable driving map
\psi_{1}:V_{1}\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\psi_{1}(V_{1})
is infinite, there exists an
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable driving map
\psi_{2}:V_{2}\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\psi_{1}(v)=\psi_{2}(\varphi(v))
for every
v\in B_{M}(G_{1})
and
\psi_{1}(G_{1})\cap\psi_{2}(G_{2}\setminus B_{M}(G_{2}))=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Fix an
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable random variable
M
and
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable isomorphism
\varphi\in I(B_{M}(G_{1}),B_{M}(G_{2}))
as in the statement of the lemma. Let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Z}}}:=({\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{{\mathbb{N}}})^{2}
, equipped with the product topology, and consider the
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Z}}}
-random element
b=(b_{1},b_{2})
with
b_{i}=(b_{i,k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
given, for
i=1,2
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, by

b_{i,k}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }k\notin G_{i},\\
\psi_{1}(k)&\text{ if }k\in V_{G_{1}},\\
\psi_{1}(\varphi^{-1}(k))&\text{ if }k\in B_{M}(G_{2})\text{ and }i=2,\\
\alpha(k)&\text{ if }k\in V_{2}\setminus B_{M}(G_{2})\text{ and }i=2,\end{cases}

where
\alpha:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{N}}
is an injection mapping each
k
to the
k
th smallest element of
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\psi_{1}(V_{1})
. Observe that
\alpha
is well defined because
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\psi_{1}(V_{1})
is infinite and is also an
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable element of
{\mathscr{W}}
because
\psi_{1}
is
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable. Thus,
b
is clearly also
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable. Define
\psi_{i}:=\beta^{-1}(b_{i})
,
i=1,2
, with
\beta
as in (
B.6
), and note that since
\beta^{-1}
is Borel measurable, each
\psi_{i}
is also
{\mathcal{F}}
-measurable. Because
\psi_{1}(V_{1})
and
\alpha({\mathbb{N}})
are disjoint,
\psi_{2}
is also injective and therefore a driving map. Furthermore, for each
k\in B_{M}(G_{1})
,
\psi_{1}(k)=\psi_{1}(\varphi^{-1}(\varphi(k)))=\psi_{2}(\varphi(k))
, and
\psi_{1}(G_{1})\subseteq\psi_{1}(V_{1})
and
\psi_{2}(G_{2}\setminus B_{M}(G_{2}))\subseteq\alpha({\mathbb{N}})
, so
\psi_{1}(G_{1})
and
\psi_{2}(G_{2}\setminus B_{M}(G_{2}))
are disjoint. This completes the proof.
∎

We finish the appendix with proofs of Lemmas
6.2
and
6.6
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

The first assertion follows from Lemma
B.7
and the fact that
\sigma(\langle(\overline{G}_{n},\overline{\xi}^{n})\rangle)\subseteq\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
. For the second assertion, let
\{(G_{n},\xi^{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},(G,\xi)
be any sequence of measurable representatives satisfying Properties 1 and 2 of Definition
6.1
. By Definition
2.1
, the convergence of
\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle
to
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
implies that for every
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
B_{m}([G_{*}])\cong B_{m}([G_{n,*}])
for sufficiently large
n
. Since
{\mathcal{X}}
is discrete, this further implies that there exists an a.s. finite random variable
N_{m}
such that
B_{m}([G_{*}],\xi)\cong B_{m}([G_{n,*}],\xi^{n})
for all
n\geq N_{m}
and
N_{m}
is
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable. Then for any
\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}
, define the following random variable:

\Psi_{n,m}(\varphi):=\begin{cases}\sum_{v\in B_{m}(G)}d_{{\mathcal{K}}}(\mathbf{\kappa}^{n}_{\varphi(v)},\mathbf{\kappa}_{v})+\sum_{e\in E_{B_{m}(G)}}d_{\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}}(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{\varphi(e)},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e})&\text{ if }\varphi\in I_{n,m},\\
\infty&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
I_{n,m}:=I(B_{m}([G_{*}],\xi),B_{m}([G_{n,*}],\xi^{n}))
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}^{\Psi}_{n,m}=\text{argmin}_{\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}}\Psi_{n,m}(\varphi)
, where we define
{\mathcal{M}}^{\Psi}_{n,m}
to be empty if
\Psi_{n,m}(\varphi)=\infty
for all
\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}
. On the
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable set
\{n\geq N_{m}\}
,
I_{n,m}
is non-empty and finite, and so
{\mathcal{M}}^{\Psi}_{n,m}
is also non-empty and finite. We first show that to prove the following claim:

Key claim:

{\mathcal{M}}^{\Psi}_{n,m}
is a
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-random closed subset of
{\mathscr{W}}
that is non-empty on the event
\{I_{n,m}\neq\emptyset\}
.

Deferring the proof of the claim, first note that given the claim, applying Lemma
B.9
, with
G_{1}=B_{m}([G_{*}],\xi)
,
G_{2}=B_{m}([G_{n,*}],\xi^{n})
, and
A={\mathcal{M}}^{\Psi}_{n,m}
, there exists a sequence of
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable maps
\{\overline{\varphi}_{n,m}\}_{n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
\overline{\varphi}_{n,m}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\Psi}_{n,m}
for every
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
on the event
\{n\geq N_{m}\}
. Hence, by the definition of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\Psi}_{n,m}
, the fact that
\overline{\varphi}_{n,m}\in I_{n.m}
and by Definition
2.1
, for any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty,n>\overline{N}_{m}}\min_{\varphi\in I_{n,m}}\Psi_{n,m}(\varphi)=\lim_{n\to\infty,n>N_{m}}\Psi_{n,m}(\overline{\varphi}_{n,m})=0.

This implies that for every
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable integer
\widehat{N}_{m}
such that
\Psi_{n,m}(\overline{\varphi}_{n,m})<2^{-m}
for all
n\geq\widehat{N}_{m}
. Moreover,
\widehat{N}_{m}
is non-decreasing and a.s. finite and so
M_{n}:=\max\{m\in{\mathbb{N}}:n\geq\widehat{N}_{m}\}
is
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable and increases to infinity. Therefore, property 3 of Definition
6.1
is satisfied. For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
m\leq M_{n}
, define
\varphi_{n,m}:=\overline{\varphi}_{n,M_{n}}|_{B_{m}(G)}
. It follows that
\{\varphi_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}}
is also an
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable sequence and satisfies property 4 of Definition
6.1
. Furthermore, Properties 5 and 6 follow directly from the fact that for
v\in B_{m}(G)
and
e\in E_{B_{m}(G)}
,

\max\{d_{{\mathcal{K}}}(\mathbf{\kappa}^{n}_{\varphi_{n,m}(v)},\mathbf{\kappa}_{v}),d_{\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}}(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{n}_{\varphi_{n,m}(e)},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e}))\leq\Psi_{n,M_{n}}(\overline{\varphi}_{n,M_{n}})<2^{-M_{n}}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

We now turn to the proof of the key claim. Fix
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We first prove the following:

Sub-Claim 1:
For each
\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}
,
\Psi_{n,m}(\varphi)
is
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable.

Proof of Sub-Claim 1:
Fix
\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}
. Define
{\mathcal{A}}^{\varphi}_{m}:={\mathcal{A}}_{m,1}\cap{\mathcal{A}}^{\varphi}_{m,2}
, where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{m,1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{(H_{1},H_{2})\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]^{2}:\max_{i=1,2,v\in V_{H_{i}}}d_{H_{i}}(o,v)\leq m\right\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{\varphi}_{m,2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{(H_{1},H_{2})\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]^{2}:\varphi\in I(\theta(H_{1}),\theta(H_{2}))\right\},

with
\theta:\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]\to\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[1,{\mathcal{X}}]
being the mapping that takes
H=(V_{H},E_{H},o_{H},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{H},\mathbf{\kappa}^{H},\xi^{H})\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
to
(V_{H},E_{H},o_{H},\xi^{H})\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[1,{\mathcal{X}}]
, which is the rooted representative graph
H
with only the
{\mathcal{X}}
-valued vertex marks retained. Then consider the mapping
\Theta_{m}^{\varphi}:(\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}])^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
given by

\displaystyle\Theta_{m}^{\varphi}(H_{1},H_{2}):=\begin{cases}\sum_{v\in V_{H_{1}}}d_{{\mathcal{K}}}(\mathbf{\kappa}^{H_{2}}_{\varphi(v)},\mathbf{\kappa}^{H_{1}}_{v})+\sum_{e\in E_{H_{1}}}d_{\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}}(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{H_{2}}_{\varphi(e)},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{H_{1}}_{e})&\text{ if }(H_{1},H_{2})\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\varphi}_{m},\\
\infty&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Since
\Psi_{n,m}(\varphi)=\Theta_{m}^{\varphi}(B_{m}(G,\xi),B_{m}(G_{n},\xi^{n}))
, to prove Sub-Claim 1, it suffices to show the following:

Sub-Claim 2:
The map
\Theta_{m}^{\varphi}
is continuous.

Proof of Sub-Claim 2:
Fix
\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}(W_{1},W_{2})
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. If
W_{1}
is infinite,
\Theta^{\varphi}_{m}\equiv\infty
, and so is trivially continuous. Next, suppose
|W_{1}|<\infty
. Then, since
\theta
and
H\mapsto\max_{v\in V_{H}}d_{H}(o,v)
are continuous and
\{(H_{1},H_{2})\in(\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[1,{\mathcal{X}}])^{2}:\varphi\in I(H_{1},H_{2})\}
is closed, it follows that
{\mathcal{A}}_{m,1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\varphi}_{m,2}
are closed, so
{\mathcal{A}}_{m}^{\varphi}
is likewise closed. Let
\{(H^{n}_{1},H^{n}_{2})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]\subset{\mathcal{A}}^{\varphi}_{m}
be any sequence that is convergent in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
, and let
(H^{\infty}_{1},H^{\infty}_{2})
denote its limit. Then
(H^{\infty}_{1},H^{\infty}_{2})\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\varphi}_{m}
since
{\mathcal{A}}^{\varphi}_{m}
is closed. Moreover, by conditions 1 and 2 of Definition
B.3
,
V_{H^{n}_{1}}=V_{H^{\infty}_{1}}=W_{1}
and
E_{H^{n}_{1}}=E_{H^{\infty}_{1}}
for all sufficiently large
n
. Then the convergence of
\Theta^{\varphi}_{m}(H^{n}_{1},H^{n}_{2})
to
\Theta^{\varphi}_{m}(H^{\infty}_{1},H^{\infty}_{2})
is an immediate consequence of the definition of
\Theta^{\varphi}_{m}
and conditions 4 and 5 of Definition
B.3
. Lastly note that the map
\theta_{2}:(\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}])^{2}\to(\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[1,{\mathcal{X}}])^{2}
defined by
\theta_{2}(H_{1},H_{2}):=(\theta(H_{1}),\theta(H_{1}))
is continuous and that
A^{\varphi}_{m}=\theta_{2}^{-1}(\overline{A}^{\varphi}_{m})
where
\overline{A}^{\varphi}\subseteq(\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[1,{\mathcal{X}}])^{2}
consists entirely of pairs of graphs with radius at most
m
. However, it is easily verified by Definition
B.3
that (since
{\mathcal{X}}
is a discrete space) any finite graph is an isolated point in
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[1,{\mathcal{X}}]
, which implies that
\overline{A}^{\varphi}_{m}
, and therefore
A^{\varphi}_{m}
, must also be open. Sub-Claim 2 then follows on noting that
\Theta^{\varphi}_{m}
is identically equal to infinity and thus trivially continuous on the closed set
({\mathcal{A}}^{\varphi}_{m})^{c}
.

Next, define
\Psi^{\text{min}}_{n,m}:=\min_{\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}}\Psi_{n,m}(\varphi)
where
\Psi^{\text{min}}_{n,m}=\infty
when
I_{n,m}=\emptyset
. Then
\Psi^{\text{min}}_{n,m}
always exists because
I_{n,m}
is finite. Note that since
|B_{m}(G)|+|B_{m}(G_{n})|<\infty
, it follows that

\Psi^{\text{min}}_{n,m}=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}(W_{1},W_{2}):\\
|W_{1}|+|W_{2}|<\infty\end{subarray}}\Psi_{n,m}(\varphi),

is a minimum over a countable collection of
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable random variables. Sub-Claim 1 then shows that
\Psi^{\text{min}}_{n,m}
is also
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable. For any open
U\subseteq{\mathscr{W}}
,

\{{\mathcal{M}}^{\Psi}_{n,m}\cap U\neq\emptyset\}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi\in{\mathscr{W}}(W_{1},W_{2})\cap U:\\
|W_{1}|+|W_{2}|<\infty\end{subarray}}\{\Psi_{n,m}(\varphi)=\Psi^{\text{min}}_{n,m}\}\cap\{\Psi^{\text{min}}_{n,m}<\infty\}.

Since this is a countable union of
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable sets, the key claim follows from Definition
B.1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
6.6
:

Let
\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a random sequence on
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
that converges a.s. to
\langle(G,\xi)\rangle
in
{\mathscr{G}}_{*}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
. By Lemma
6.2
, there exists a rep-con sequence
(\{(G_{n},\xi^{n}),M_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
(G,\xi),\{\varphi_{n,m}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}},m\leq M_{n}})
of
(\{\langle(G_{n},\xi^{n})\rangle\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}},\langle(G,\xi)\rangle)
defined on the same probability space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
. Let
\psi
be a
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable driving map
\psi:V_{G}\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\psi(G)
is infinite (e.g., consider the map
\psi(k)=2k
). Then, invoking Properties 3 and 4 of Definition
6.1
and repeatedly applying Lemma
B.10
with
M=M_{n}
,
G_{1}=([G_{*}],\xi)
,
G_{2}=([G_{n,*}],\xi^{n})
,
{\mathcal{F}}=\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
,
\varphi=\varphi_{n,M_{n}}
, and
\psi_{1}=\psi
, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we can construct a
\widehat{{\mathcal{F}}}
-measurable driving map
\psi_{n}:G_{n}\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every
m\leq M_{n}
and
v\in B_{m}(G)
,
\psi_{n}(\varphi_{n,m}(v))=\psi(v)
. Then extending the space
(\widehat{\Omega},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}})
to add a countable sequence of i.i.d. Poisson processes
\{\mathbf{N}_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and using the driving maps
\psi_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\psi
to generate the respective
{\mathbb{F}}
-driving noise
(G_{n},\mathbf{N}^{G_{n}})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
as in Definition
6.4
, we obtain a consistent rep-con extension.
∎

Appendix C
Verification of Assumptions

C.1
Well-Posedness Under Spatially Heterogeneous Dynamics

In this section, we extend our well-posedness results to spatially heterogeneous dynamics as mentioned in Remark
4.5
. Consider IPS with local jump rates that are parameterized by jumps
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
and graphs
H
whose vertex labels lie in
{\mathbb{N}}
(one can trivially replace
{\mathbb{N}}
by any countable, deterministic set) rather than the slightly more general space
\widetilde{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}
of rooted graphs of radius one considered in Section
3.1
; see Remark
C.1
below for the reason for this restriction. Recalling the Polish spaces
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}
and
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
from Definition
B.3
, let
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}:=\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}\cap\widetilde{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}\subset\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}
be the space of rooted (unmarked) graphs with vertex labels in
{\mathbb{N}}
and with radius one. In this framework, we denote the local jump rates as follows:

\widehat{\mathbf{\bar{r}}}:=\{\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H}_{j}:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{D}}^{V_{H}}\times\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{E_{H}}\times{\mathcal{K}}^{V_{H}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+},H=(V_{H},E_{H},o_{H})\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1},j\in{\mathcal{J}}\}.

Then, just as in the homogeneous case, the jump rates
{\bf r^{G}}
are derived from the local jump rates in a manner analogous to (
3.1
):

r^{G,v}_{j}(t,x)=\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H_{v}}_{j}(t,x_{H_{v}},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e})_{e\in E_{H_{v}}},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{v})_{v\in V_{H_{v}}}),\text{ for all }(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{D}}^{V_{G}},

(C.1)

where
H_{v}:=(G[\text{cl}_{v}],v)
. Note that the jump rates
{\bf r^{G}}
are now only well defined for
G\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
, and the SDE (
3.4
) is likewise also only well defined when the initial data
(G,\xi)
is a
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times\xi]
-random element.

Remark C.1

(Vertex labels)
.

Considering only graphs with vertex labels in
{\mathbb{N}}
is not very restrictive in practice and is done for the purely technical reason of simplifying measurability considerations. This restriction allows us to parameterize the dynamics of particles by their labels in
{\mathbb{N}}
, in which case the space
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}
is countable and discrete (all points are isolated), and the local jump rates are trivially measurable as a function of their inputs and the underlying graph that parameterizes them.

We now define the notion of
quasi-regular
local jump rates:

Definition C.2

(Quasi-regularity of local jump rates)
.

Given
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
, the family of local jump rates
\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H}_{j}:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{D}}^{V_{H}}\times\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{E_{H}}\times{\mathcal{K}}^{V_{H}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
H=(V_{H},E_{H},o_{H})\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}
, is said to be quasi-regular if for each
H\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1},j\in{\mathcal{J}}
, and
(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})\in\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{E_{H}}\times{\mathcal{K}}^{V_{H}}
,
\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H}_{j}
is Borel measurable and the map
(t,x)\mapsto\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H}_{j}(t,x,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})
is predictable in the sense that for every
t>0
and
x,y\in{\mathcal{D}}^{V_{H}}
,

x(s)=y(s)\text{ for all }s\in[0,t)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H}_{j}(t,x,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})=\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H}_{j}(t,y,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa}).

Definition
C.2
only differs from Definition
3.1
in that the symmetry condition is removed. Thus, we replace the standing assumption with the condition that the local jump rates are quasi-regular.

Assumption 3

(A weaker standing assumption)
.

Given any
G=(V,E,o,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
, the jump rates
{\bf r^{G}}
for the IPS dynamics on the graph
G
satisfy (
C.1
) for local jump rates
\widehat{\mathbf{\bar{r}}}=\{\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H}_{j}:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{D}}^{V_{H}}\times\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{E_{H}}\times{\mathcal{K}}^{V_{H}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\}_{H=(V_{H},E_{H},o_{H})\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}},j\in{\mathcal{J}},
that are quasi-regular in the sense of Definition
C.2
.

The definitions of weak/strong solutions to the SDE (
3.4
), and weak/strong well-posedness of (
3.4
) are identical to those given in Definition
3.8
and Definition
3.11
, respectively, with the driving noises
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G})
being a simple sub-collection of i.i.d. Poisson processes, as specified in Remark
3.6
.

We now introduce the analog of Assumption
1
, which is essentially exchanged except for the fact that the maximum of the local jump rates is now defined over a smaller set of graphs.

Assumption
\widehat{\ref{mod:assu}}

(Bounds on the heterogenous local jump rates)
.

There exists a family of constants
\mathbf{C}:=\{C_{k,T}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}},T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\subset(0,\infty)}
with
(k,T)\mapsto C_{k,T}
being componentwise non-decreasing such that for every
H\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}
and
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H}_{j}(t,x,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})\leq C_{|V_{H}|,T},\quad\mbox{ for all }t\in[0,T],x\in{\mathcal{D}}^{V_{H}},\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}\in\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{E_{H}},\mathbf{\kappa}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{V_{H}}.

Combined with (
C.1
), Assumption
3
implies that the jump rate
r^{G,v}_{j}
may depend on
v
arbitrarily with no need to respect graph isomorphisms as in the homogeneous case. As noted in Remark
3.2
, removal of the symmetry condition implies that the jump rates do not necessarily satisfy the class property (
3.2
). Therefore, we now state the main result of the section, noting that well-posedness is now framed as a property of a marked graph rather than an isomorphism class as in Theorem
4.3
.

Theorem C.3

(Strong well-posedness of heterogeneous IPS)
.

Fix a complete, filtered probability space
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
. Let
(G,\xi)
be an
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
-measurable
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times\xi]
-random element. If Assumption

^

‣
C.1
holds for a family of constants
\mathbf{C}
and
G
is a.s. finitely dissociable with respect to
\mathbf{C}
, then the SDE (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for the initial data
(G,\xi)
.

Proof.

Define the mapping
\psi:\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]\to\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathbb{N}}]
as follows:

\psi((G,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})):=(G,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},w)\text{ where }w_{v}=v\text{ for }v\in V.

We slightly abuse notation by defining
\psi
as in the display above for all choices of
(\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}})
. Note that for any
H\in\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}]
, the marked graph
\psi(H)
has a trivial automorphism group. The key steps of the proof are as follows. We first use this fact to construct from the quasi-regular local jump rates
{\bf\widehat{\bar{r}}}
, a collection of regular local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
(in the sense of Definition
3.1
) that act on marks that have been augmented by
\psi
. Next, we consider an auxiliary SDE (
3.4
) with jump rates
{\bf r^{\psi(G)}}
defined in terms of the local jump rates
{\bf\bar{r}}
via (
3.1
), and observe that this is strongly well-posed by Theorem
4.3
. Then we establish a one-to-one correspondence between weak solutions to the SDE (
3.4
) with jump rates
{\bf r^{G}}
for the initial data
(G,\xi),
stated in the theorem, and weak solutions to the auxiliary SDE with jump rates
{\bf r^{\psi(G)}}
for the initial data
\psi(G,\xi).

Step 1: Construction of regular local jump rates on an augmented graph.
Fix
H=(V_{H},E_{H},o_{H})\in\widetilde{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}
and
(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},w)\in\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{E_{H}}\times({\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathbb{N}})^{V_{H}}
. Then define
\varphi_{w}:V_{H}\to{\mathbb{N}}
by
\varphi_{w}(v)=w_{v}
, and by some abuse of notation, define
\varphi_{w}:E_{H}\to\{\{i,j\}:i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
by
\varphi_{w}(\{u,v\})=\{\varphi_{w}(u),\varphi_{w}(v)\}
for
\{u,v\}\in E_{H}
, with the obvious definition for
\varphi_{w}(A)
when
A
is a subset of
V_{H}
or
E_{H}
. Also, when
\varphi_{w}
is injective, we define

H_{w}:=(\varphi_{w}(V_{H}),\varphi_{w}(E_{H}),\varphi_{w}(o_{H})).

(C.2)

Observe that
H_{w}
is the unique element of
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}
such that
\varphi_{w}\in I(H,H_{w})
or, in other words,
H_{w}
is the unique graph that is isomorphic to
H
and has vertex labels in
{\mathbb{N}}
that are compatible with
w
in the sense made explicit by (
C.2
). Note that
H_{w}
may fail to be well defined when
\varphi_{w}
is not injective, but we consider this case separately. Then for
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times{\mathcal{D}}^{V_{H}}
, and
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
, define the auxiliary local jump rates

\displaystyle\bar{r}^{H}_{j}(t,x,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},w)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\widehat{\bar{r}}^{[H_{w}]}_{j}(t,(x_{\varphi_{w}^{-1}(v)})_{v\in\varphi_{w}(V_{H})},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{\varphi_{w}^{-1}(e)})_{e\in\varphi_{w}(E_{H})},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{\varphi^{-1}_{w}(v)})_{v\in\varphi_{w}(V_{H})})&\text{ if }\varphi_{w}\text{ is injective},\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

We now show that this auxiliary family of local jump rates satisfies the symmetry property in Definition
3.1
. Suppose that
\widehat{H}\cong H
, and fix some
\widehat{\varphi}\in I(\widehat{H},H)
. For
w\in{\mathbb{N}}^{V_{H}}
(chosen such that
\varphi_{w}
is injective), define

\widehat{w}_{v}:=w_{\widehat{\varphi}(v)}\text{ for all }v\in V_{\widehat{H}}.

(C.3)

We claim that
H_{w}=\widehat{H}_{\widehat{w}}
, where
\widehat{H}_{\widehat{w}}
is defined as in (
C.2
), but with
H
and
w
replaced by
\widehat{H}
and
\widehat{w}
, respectively. To see why, note that for any
v\in V_{\widehat{H}}
, by the definition of
\varphi_{\widehat{w}}
given above, (
C.3
), and the choice of
\widehat{\varphi}
,
\varphi_{\widehat{w}}(v)=\widehat{w}_{v}=w_{\widehat{\varphi}(v)}=\varphi_{w}(\widehat{\varphi}(v))
. Thus, we have shown that
\varphi_{\widehat{w}}=\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi}
. Together with the definition of
\widehat{H}_{\widehat{w}}
from (
C.2
), this implies that

V_{H_{w}}=\varphi_{w}(V_{H})=\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi}(\widehat{\varphi}^{-1}(V_{H}))=\varphi_{\widehat{w}}(V_{\widehat{H}})=V_{\widehat{H}_{\widehat{w}}}.

Likewise,
E_{H_{w}}=E_{\widehat{H}_{\widehat{w}}}
and
o_{H_{w}}=o_{\widehat{H}_{\widehat{w}}}
, and so
H_{w}=\widehat{H}_{\widehat{w}}
as claimed.

Fix
t>0
,
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
,
\widehat{H}\cong H\in\widetilde{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}
,
\widehat{\varphi}\in I(\widehat{H},H)
, and
(x,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},w)\in{\mathcal{D}}^{V_{H}}\times\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}^{E_{H}}\times({\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathbb{N}})^{V_{H}}
. Again, define
\widehat{w}
as in (
C.3
). If
\varphi_{w}
is injective, applying the definitions of
\bar{r}^{H}
and
\bar{r}^{\widehat{H}}
in the first and last equalities, respectively, and using
\widehat{\varphi}\in I(\widehat{H},H)
and
(\widehat{H}_{\widehat{w}},\varphi_{\widehat{w}})=(H_{w},\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi})
in the second and third equalities, respectively, we conclude that

\displaystyle\bar{r}^{H}_{j}(t,

\displaystyle(x_{v})_{v\in V_{H}},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e})_{e\in E_{H}},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{v},w_{v})_{v\in V_{H}})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bar{r}}^{[H_{w}]}_{j}(t,(x_{\varphi_{w}^{-1}(v)})_{v\in\varphi_{w}(V_{H})},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{\varphi_{w}^{-1}(e)})_{e\in\varphi_{w}(E_{H})},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{\varphi^{-1}_{w}(v)})_{v\in\varphi_{w}(V_{H})})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bar{r}}^{[H_{w}]}_{j}(t,(x_{(\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi})^{-1}(v)})_{v\in\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi}(V_{\widehat{H}})},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{(\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi})^{-1}(e)})_{e\in\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi}(E_{\widehat{H}})},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{(\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi})^{-1}(v)})_{v\in\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi}(V_{\widehat{H}})})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bar{r}}^{[\widehat{H}_{\widehat{w}}]}_{j}(t,(x_{(\varphi_{\widehat{w}})^{-1}(v)})_{v\in\varphi_{\widehat{w}}(V_{\widehat{H}})},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{(\varphi_{\widehat{w}})^{-1}(e)})_{e\in\varphi_{\widehat{w}}(E_{\widehat{H}})},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{(\varphi_{\widehat{w}})^{-1}(v)})_{v\in\varphi_{\widehat{w}}(V_{\widehat{H}})})

\displaystyle=\bar{r}^{\widehat{H}}_{j}(t,(x_{\widehat{\varphi}(v)})_{v\in V_{\widehat{H}}},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{\widehat{\varphi}(e)})_{e\in E_{\widehat{H}}},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{\widehat{\varphi}(v)},w_{\widehat{\varphi}(v)})_{v\in V_{\widehat{H}}}),

where we make use of the fact that the map
\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi}:V_{\widehat{H}}\to{\mathbb{N}}
satisfies
\varphi_{w}\circ\widehat{\varphi}(v)=w_{\widehat{\varphi}(v)}
and is injective if and only if
\varphi_{w}
is injective. If
\varphi_{w}
is not injective, then

\bar{r}^{H}_{j}(t,(x_{v})_{v\in V_{H}},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e})_{e\in E_{H}},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{v},w_{v})_{v\in V_{H}})=0=\bar{r}^{\widehat{H}}_{j}(t,(x_{\widehat{\varphi}(v)})_{v\in V_{\widehat{H}}},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{\widehat{\varphi}(e)})_{e\in E_{\widehat{H}}},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{\widehat{\varphi}(v)},w_{\widehat{\varphi}(v)})_{v\in V_{\widehat{H}}}).

Thus,
\mathbf{\bar{r}}=\{\bar{r}^{H}_{j}\}_{H\in\widetilde{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1},j\in{\mathcal{J}}}
satisfies the symmetry condition of Definition
3.1
. The predictability condition follows directly from Assumption
3
. Thus,
\{\bar{r}^{H}_{j}\}_{H\in\widetilde{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1},j\in{\mathcal{J}}}
is a family of regular local jump rates.

Step 2: Introducing an auxiliary SDE.
Next, in terms of the specified
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times\xi]
initial data
(G,\xi)
and the local jump rates
\mathbf{\bar{r}}
defined above, we define the jump rates
{\bf r^{\psi(G)}}=\{r^{\psi(G),v}_{j}\}_{v\in V_{G},j\in{\mathcal{J}}}
via (
3.1
). Then we define the auxiliary SDE to be the SDE (
3.4
) with jump rates
{\bf r^{\psi(G)}}
in place of
{\bf r^{G}}.
Since
G
is a.s. finitely dissociable by assumption, Definition
5.11
trivially ensures that the unmarked version
[G]
and hence,
\psi(G)
, are also a.s. finitely dissociable. Further, since
{\bf\widehat{\bar{r}}}
satisfies Assumption

^

‣
C.1
, it follows from the definition given above that
{\bf\bar{r}}
satisfies Assumption
1
. Thus, by Theorem
4.3
, the auxiliary SDE is strongly well-posed for the initial data
\ is{\psi(G,\xi)}
.

Step 3: Establishing a one-to-one correspondence.
For
G=(V,E,o,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},w)
,
v\in V_{G}
,
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
, and
(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{D}}^{V_{G}}
, setting
H^{v}:=[G[\text{cl}_{v}]]=(V_{H^{v}},E_{H^{v}},v=o_{H^{v}})
, and using first (
3.1
), then the definition of
{\bf\bar{r}}
in Step 1 and the fact that
\varphi_{w}
is the identity map, and lastly (
C.1
), it follows that

\displaystyle r^{\psi(G),v}_{j}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\bar{r}^{H^{v}}_{j}(t,(x_{u})_{u\in V_{H^{v}}},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e})_{e\in E_{H^{v}}},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{u})_{u\in V_{H^{v}}},(u)_{u\in V_{H^{v}}})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bar{r}}^{H^{v}}_{j}(t,(x_{u})_{u\in V_{H^{v}}},(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e})_{e\in E_{H^{v}}},(\mathbf{\kappa}_{u})_{u\in V_{H^{v}}})

\displaystyle=r^{G,v}_{j}(t,x).

It follows immediately from the equivalence above that a
\widehat{{\mathscr{G}}_{*}}[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{D}}]
-random element
(G,X)
is an
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
-weak solution to the SDE (
3.4
) (with jump rates
{\bf r^{G}}
) for the initial data
(G,\xi)
if and only if
\psi(G,X)
is an
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
-weak solution to the auxiliary SDE with jump rates
{\bf r^{\psi(G)}}
for the initial data
\psi(G,\xi)
.

To conclude the proof, note that from step 2, we know that the auxiliary SDE is strongly well-posed for the initial data
\langle\psi(G,\xi)\rangle
. Therefore, by Remark
3.14
, it is also strongly well-posed for the initial data
\psi(G,\xi)
. The one-to-one correspondence established in Step 3 then shows that the SDE (
3.4
) (with jump rates
\mathbf{r}^{G}
) is strongly well-posed for the initial data
(G,\xi)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

C.2
Characterization of Strong Well-Posedness on Random Graphs

Proof of Lemma
3.12
.

The key issue here is to show that conditioning on the initial data does not change the driving noise structure. Note that this is slightly non-standard as the driving noise is indexed by the vertices of the graph and thus is not completely independent of the initial data. Let
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
be a complete, filtered probability space that supports
(G,\xi)
and a filtration-Poisson process pair
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
such that
(G,X)
and
(G,Y)
are two
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
-weak solutions of (
3.4
) for
(G,\xi)
. To prove the lemma, it suffices to prove the following claim: for
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.
\omega\in\Omega
, setting
(H,\xi^{H}):=(G(\omega),\xi(\omega))
, there exists a filtration-Poisson process pair
({\mathcal{F}}^{H},\mathbf{N}^{H})
on some probability space
(\Omega^{H},{\mathcal{F}}^{H},{\mathbb{F}}^{H},{\mathbb{P}}^{H})
and two
({\mathcal{F}}^{H},\mathbf{N}^{H})
-weak solutions
(H,X^{H})
and
(H,Y^{H})
to (
3.4
) for
(H,\xi^{H})
such that
\mathcal{L}((G,X,Y,\mathbf{N}^{G})|{\mathcal{F}}_{0})(\omega)=\mathcal{L}((H,X^{H},Y^{H},\mathbf{N}^{H}))
. Indeed, if the claim holds, then a.s. strong well-posedness of (
3.4
) for every realization of the random graph
(G,\xi)
implies that
{\mathbb{P}}(X=Y|{\mathcal{F}}_{0})(\omega)={\mathbb{P}}^{H}(X^{H}=Y^{H})=1
for
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.
\omega\in\Omega
. Hence
{\mathbb{P}}(X=Y)=1
, which proves strong well-posedness. This claim can be proved via direct verification of Definitions
3.7
,
3.8
. We include the details for completion.

To prove the claim, let
(H,X^{H},Y^{H},\mathbf{N}^{H})
be a random element with law
\mathcal{L}((G,X,Y,\mathbf{N}^{G})|{\mathcal{F}}_{0})(\omega)
and fix a complete, filtered probability space
(\Omega^{H},{\mathcal{F}}^{H},{\mathbb{F}}^{H},{\mathbb{P}}^{H})
that supports
(H,X^{H},Y^{H},\mathbf{N}^{H})
where
{\mathbb{F}}^{H}
is the minimal filtration satisfying the usual conditions such that
X^{H},Y^{H}
and
\mathbf{N}^{H}
are all
{\mathbb{F}}^{H}
-adapted in the sense that for any
v\in V_{H}
,
X^{H}_{v},Y^{H}_{v}
and
\mathbf{N}^{H}_{v}
are all
{\mathbb{F}}^{H}
-adapted (point) processes. Since by assumption
\mathbf{N}^{G}
is a
{\mathbb{F}}
-driving noise, condition 1 of Definition
3.7
immediately implies
\mathbf{N}^{H}
is a collection of i.i.d. Poisson processes on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\times{\mathcal{J}}
with intensity
{\rm Leb}^{2}\otimes{\mathcal{J}}
, indexed by the vertices of
H
, and hence that
(H,\mathbf{N}^{H})
is a
{\mathbb{F}}^{\mathbf{N}^{H}}
-driving noise.

Since (
3.4
) holds a.s., for
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.
\omega\in\Omega
,
(H,X^{H})
and
(H,Y^{H})
are both graphs with random càdlàg marks that, together with
(H,\mathbf{N}^{H})
,
{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot|{\mathcal{F}}_{0})(\omega)
-a.s. solve (
3.4
) for
(H,\xi^{H})
. Thus,
(H,X^{H})
and
(H,Y^{H})
satisfy conditions 1 and 3 of Definition
3.8

{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. with respect to the filtered probability space
(\Omega^{H},{\mathcal{F}}^{H},{\mathbb{F}}^{H},{\mathbb{P}}^{H})
(noting that any
{\mathbb{P}}(\cdot|{\mathcal{F}}_{0})(\omega)
-null event is also
{\mathbb{P}}^{H}
-null), and so it suffices to prove that
\mathbf{N}^{H}
is
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. a collection of i.i.d.
{\mathbb{F}}^{H}
-Poisson processes. To do this, we note that condition 2 of Definition
3.7
implies that for any
t>0
and
A\in{\mathcal{B}}((t,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{J}})
, the random element
(G,\mathbf{N}^{G}(A))
is conditionally independent of the
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
-measurable random element
(G,X[t],Y[t],\mathbf{N}^{G}_{t})
given
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
, where
\mathbf{N}^{G}_{t}=\mathbf{N}^{G}|_{[0,t]\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{J}}}
. Thus,
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.,
(H,\mathbf{N}^{H}(A))
is independent of
(H,X^{H}[t],Y^{H}[t],\mathbf{N}^{H}_{t})
. Then using a standard approximation argument exploiting the fact that Borel sigma algebras of subsets of Polish spaces are countably generated and that
{\mathbb{F}}^{H}
is complete, it follows that
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.,
\mathbf{N}^{H}_{v}(A)
is independent of
{\mathcal{F}}^{H}_{t}
for all
v\in V_{H}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
A\in{\mathcal{B}}((t,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathcal{J}})
. Thus,
\{\mathbf{N}^{H}_{v}\}_{v\in V_{H}}
is
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. a collection of
{\mathbb{F}}^{H}
-Poisson processes. Therefore,
\mathbf{N}^{H}
is
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. an
{\mathbb{F}}^{H}
-driving noise in the sense of Definition
3.7
, so condition 2 of Definition
3.8
is also
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. satisfied. This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

C.3
Verification of Assumptions for Examples in Section
3.2

In this section, we verify conditions under which the examples in Section
3.2
satisfy Assumptions
1
and
2
.

Example
3.3
:
If there exist non-decreasing constants
\{C_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
\sup_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}^{V_{H}}}|\widetilde{r}^{H}_{j}(x)|\leq C_{|H|}
for all
j\in{\mathcal{J}}
and
H\in\widetilde{{\mathscr{G}}}_{*,1}
, then Assumption
1
holds with
C_{k,T}:=C_{k}
. Moreover, Assumption
2
holds trivially for all initial data because the mark spaces
\overline{{\mathcal{K}}}={\mathcal{K}}=\{1\}
are trivial. Note that in this case, the solution
X^{G,\xi}
to the SDE (
3.4
) with initial data
(G,\xi)
is a homogeneous Markov process.

Example
3.4
:
The jump rate is continuous with respect to the initial marks
(\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa})
, so Assumption
2
holds for all initial data. This model satisfies Assumption
1
when (1) there exist deterministic constants
\mathbf{\kappa}^{*}
and
\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{*}
such that
\sup_{v\in V}\mathbf{\kappa}_{v}\leq\mathbf{\kappa}^{*}<\infty
and
\sup_{e\in E}\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}_{e}\leq\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{*}<\infty
, and (2) the functions
\lambda
and
\rho
are bounded from above by respective non-decreasing functions
\widehat{\lambda},\widehat{\rho}:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Under these conditions,
C_{k,T}=\mathbf{\kappa}^{*}\widehat{\rho}(T)+(k-1)\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}}^{*}\widehat{\lambda}(T)
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}},T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

Example
3.5
:
Assumption
1
holds when the functions
\beta
and
\gamma
are bounded from above. Note that in the Markov case,
\beta\equiv\lambda
and
\gamma\equiv 1
are both constant. Finally, if both
\beta
and
\gamma
are also continuous, then Assumption
2
holds for all initial data.

Appendix D
Well-Posedness for Finite Initial Data

Under Assumption
1
, well-posedness of (
3.4
) is common knowledge when the initial data is finite, but we establish it here for completeness. In this case, we also show that the trajectories also satisfy the following additional regularity property. Recall the definition of the discontinuity set
{\rm Disc}_{t}\left(x\right)
given in (
2.2
).

Definition D.1

(Proper trajectories)
.

Given a countable set
W
and
t\in[0,\infty)
, we say
x\in{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{W}
is
proper
if
{\rm Disc}_{t}\left(x_{w}\right)\cap{\rm Disc}_{t}\left(x_{v}\right)=\emptyset
for all distinct
v,w\in W
. Moreover, we say a trajectory
x\in{\mathcal{D}}^{W}
is proper if its restriction
x[t]
to
[0,t]
is proper for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
and trajectories being a.s. proper:

Let
(G,\xi)=(V,E,o,\overline{\mathbf{\kappa}},\mathbf{\kappa},\xi)
any a.s. finite
[\overline{{\mathcal{K}}},{\mathcal{K}}\times{\mathcal{X}}]
-random graph. By Lemma
3.12
, it suffices to prove that (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for
(G,\xi)
with a.s. proper solutions under the additional assumption that
(G,\xi)
is deterministic.

Let
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
be a filtration-Poisson process pair in the sense of Remark
3.9
. First note that for any
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
-weak solution
(G,X)
to (
3.4
), any distinct vertices
u,v\in V
and any
T>0
,

{\rm Disc}_{T}\left(X_{v}\right)\cap{\rm Disc}_{T}\left(X_{u}\right)\subseteq\{s\in[0,T]:\mathbf{N}^{G}_{u}(\{s\}\times(0,C_{k,T}]\times{\mathcal{J}})\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v}(\{s\}\times(0,C_{k,T}]\times{\mathcal{J}})=1\}=\emptyset\text{ a.s.,}

by (
3.4
) and Assumption
1
where
\{C_{k,T}\}
is the family of constants from Assumption
1
and
k:=\max\{|\text{cl}_{u}|,|\text{cl}_{v}|\}
. Since this holds for all
T
,
(G,X)
is a.s. proper.

Next, fix a filtration-Poisson process pair
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
. Define the finite collection,

{\mathcal{E}}:=\{(\tau_{n},r_{n},j_{n},v_{n})\in[0,T]\times[0,C_{K,T}]\times{\mathcal{J}}\times V:\mathbf{N}^{G}_{v_{n}}(\{(\tau_{n},r_{n},j_{n})\})=1\},

K=\max_{v\in V}|\text{cl}_{v}|
and
\{\tau_{n}\}_{n=1}^{|{\mathcal{E}}|}
is increasing. Note that
\{\tau_{n}\}
is, in fact, strictly increasing. define the
{\mathcal{D}}^{V}_{T}
-random element
X[T]
by,

X_{v}(t)=\begin{cases}\xi_{v}&\text{ if }0\leq t<\tau_{1},\\
X_{v}(\tau_{n})&\text{ if }\tau_{n}\leq t<\tau_{n+1},n<|{\mathcal{E}}|,\\
X_{v}(\tau_{n})&\text{ if }\tau_{n}\leq t\leq T,n=|{\mathcal{E}}|,\\
X_{v}(\tau_{n})&\text{ if }t=\tau_{n+1},r_{n+1}>r^{G,v}_{j_{n+1}}(t_{n+1},X),\\
X_{v}(\tau_{n})+j_{n+1}&\text{ if }t=\tau_{n+1},r_{n+1}>r^{G,v}_{j_{n+1}}(t_{n+1},X).\end{cases}

Then note that for
t\in[0,T]
,
X(t)
is clearly
{\mathcal{F}}^{G,\xi,\mathbf{N}^{G}}_{t}
-measurable so
X
is a
\mathbf{N}^{G}
-strong solution to (
3.4
) for
(G,\xi)
on the interval
[0,T]
. Furthermore, it is clear that any
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
-weak solution to (
3.4
) must satisfy the above display, so all
({\mathbb{F}},\mathbf{N}^{G})
-weak solutions equal
X
on the interval
[0,T]
. Because
T
is arbitrary, and for any
T^{\prime}>T
the corresponding solution
X^{\prime}
satisfies
X^{\prime}[T]=X[T]
a.s., it follows that there exists a
\mathbf{N}^{G}
-strong solution to (
3.4
) and that solution is pathwise unique. Therefore (
3.4
) is strongly well-posed for
(G,\xi)
.
∎
